#145. Timing of Climate Science Reports Ignites a Hot Fight at U.N. Talks

Several countries that rely heavily on fossil fuels want to delay the next round of scientific assessments that governments use to inform their climate policies.

A squabble over the publication timetable for several climate reports has turned into one of the burning issues of this year’s United Nations climate conference, as some warn that fossil fuel interests might be interfering with the world’s pre-eminent climate science organization.

The U.N.-backed Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change produces landmark reports that generally come out every five to seven years. The next round of reports would take on topics like physical changes to the earth and greenhouse gas emissions.

Timing is critical because in 2028 nations are supposed to assess their progress and then ramp up their efforts to do more, based on the best science. That process, known as a stocktake and occurring every five years, is the engine of the Paris Agreement.

Saudi Arabia, India and Russia are among those pushing to delay the reports so that at least two of them come out after 2028.

The fact that those nations rank among the largest fossil fuel exporters or importers has led to accusations that coal, oil and gas interests might be interfering with science.

“Saudi Arabia is trying to destroy the I.P.C.C. in order to prevent the next global stocktake from using the accurate scientific data on fossil fuels,” said former Vice President Al Gore, who is in Turkey for the talks.

#143. Torture survivors may have been sent to Sussex asylum camp unlawfully, documentary reveals

Undercover ITV report also shows inadequate welfare checks at ex-military base run by firm of asylum camp billionaire Graham King

Asylum seekers accommodated at a controversial military base in East Sussex have been subjected to inadequate welfare checks, and torture survivors may have been placed there unlawfully, a new undercover documentary for ITV has revealed.

Mass accommodation sites are central to the government’s plan to end the use of hotels to house asylum seekers. For the documentary Inside The Asylum Camp, ITV’s undercover reporter got a job as a welfare officer at Crowborough and covertly filmed inside the former military training camp. The documentary claims that poor screening and welfare checks are having serious consequences for some of the asylum seekers.

Last May, the high court ruled that a Home Office policy change forcing torture survivors to share rooms was unlawful. The accommodation at Crowborough is in large dormitories.

The Home Office’s decision to accommodate asylum seekers on two former military sites – Crowborough, and Wethersfield in Essex – have triggered opposition from local residents. Plans to use three other sites – Bicester, Barnham and Linton-on-Ouse – have caused a similar outcry, mostly around fears about asylum seekers causing a rise in violent crime. However, very low levels of even minor crimes have been recorded by police at Wethersfield and Crowborough among asylum seekers at the two camps.

The Crowborough site is run by Clearsprings Ready Homes. According to its latest accounts, it is generating millions of pounds in income every day from its asylum accommodation contracts. Its boss, Graham King, has become the first asylum accommodation billionaire.

The documentary footage shows that, although two comprehensive welfare checks are supposed to be carried out each month by Clearsprings welfare officers, some staff appear to admit cutting corners and not asking all the questions they are required to ask of asylum seekers. On one occasion a welfare officer is filmed telling the undercover reporter “you don’t need to go in too deep”.

Staff appear to admit to falling behind on the 1,000 welfare checks they are supposed to carry out each month saying: “We’re so fucked.”

Concerns about an asylum seeker can be flagged on their file. A welfare officer is filmed telling the undercover reporter: “Those flags mean nothing” and that “nobody ever looks at them again”. When asked by the undercover reporter if some asylum seekers fall through the net due to brief welfare checks carried out, the welfare officer replies: “Oh, absolutely.”

The undercover reporter says he has identified four or five survivors of torture who should not have been sent to the military camp.

Louise Calvey, the executive director of the charity Asylum Matters, raised concerns about the findings of the undercover documentary. Her organisation has a campaign to accommodate asylum seekers in communities not camps. “The reports of torture survivors being accommodated on military barracks are appalling,” she said. “Brave survivors endure appalling experiences arriving to a country where they thought they’d be safe – a supposed civilised country, placing them in camps without the support they desperately need.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

“People being held in isolation for prolonged periods of time without access to sufficient water demonstrate just how far we have fallen as a country. That government contractors can fail so resoundingly in their duty of care to people seeking safety in our country demonstrates how much our system dehumanises refugees.”

Calvey said the government needed to urgently review its use of the camps, which she described as “a choice to punish people for having the audacity to exercise their lawful right to seek asylum here”.

The Home Office and Clearsprings have been approached.

Inside The Camp airs on Sunday 15 November at 22.15 on ITV1 and ITV X

#141. Greenpeace boulders may delay building of £12bn windfarms in North Sea

Activists dumped rocks off Norfolk coast on site of Burnham-backed project in protest against seabed trawling

The giant offshore windfarms backed by Andy Burnham this week may face unexpected stumbling blocks after Greenpeace activists unwittingly dumped about 15 tonnes of boulders on to the site as part of a campaign against seabed trawling in the North Sea.

Greenpeace created the “boulder barrier” to safeguard harbour porpoises off the Norfolk coast by dropping giant stone blocks, each weighing up to 2 tonnes, on to the sea floor to prevent a method of fishing known as bottom trawling.

The barrier will pose an additional challenge for the green energy developers because most of the boulders were dropped on to the site earmarked for one of the UK’s biggest planned windfarms, known as the Norfolk Vanguard project.

Greenpeace began dumping boulders on to the site, without consent from the marine authority, days before the prime minister told the Cop31 summit that the UK would invest £1bn of public money in the Vanguard West and Vanguard East windfarms in what is believed to be the biggest investment of its kind in UK history.

The Guardian understands that Greenpeace checked the seabed for any existing cables, pipelines or shipwrecks, but it was not aware of the plans to build the Vanguard East windfarm.

The creation of the underwater barrier in one of “the most heavily trawled” areas of the southern North Sea was timed to coincide with the premiere of the BBC’s Blue Planet III documentary, narrated by Sir David Attenborough.

Some boulders bear the signatures of celebrities including Simon Pegg and Benedict Cumberbatch, who have voiced marine conservation documentaries, and agreed to support the Greenpeace campaign.

However, they are likely to be removed within months after renewable energy developers agreed a £12bn deal alongside the UK government to begin building the offshore windfarm next year. Industrial fishing activities, including bottom trawling, are also likely to move elsewhere to avoid the construction area.

The Guardian understands that the offshore wind developer, the German utility company RWE, is in talks with Greenpeace to verify the exact placement of the boulders so that they can update the seabed surveys they began last summer. The company is also investigating any potential impact to the construction of the project or its undersea cables.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

A spokesperson for RWE described its communication with Greenpeace as “constructive” and said the company would “continue to investigate the precise implications” for the Norfolk Vanguard East project.

Will McCallum, the co-executive director of Greenpeace UK, said any suggestion that the boulders would cause a genuine obstacle was “absurd”.

“Anyone familiar with offshore wind in the North Sea knows that energy companies routinely move naturally occurring boulders from the seabed before building windfarms,” he said.

“Some of these boulders can be 10 times heavier than those we placed.

“Greenpeace has long championed offshore wind and will continue to do so. While we wait for this windfarm to be built, our boulders will provide an essential barrier to destructive bottom trawlers that are decimating the seabed in this protected area.”

#140. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#138. African Miss Universe Contestants Struggle to Get U.S. Visas

A crackdown on entry by some citizens of African countries by the U.S. immigration officials means that some contestants might not get visas in time to attend the pageant in Puerto Rico this month.

At least two African contestants for Miss Universe said they were in danger of missing the pageant in Puerto Rico this month because they had not secured U.S. visas amid a crackdown by the Trump administration.

One contestant, Miss Zambia, Mubanga Pamela Hatyoka, said she would not travel to the annual event on Nov. 24 because she had not received her visa, according to Arm Model Management, which said it was the franchise holder of Miss Zambia 2026.

Her nonattendance is “linked to the United States presidential travel restrictions affecting Zambia and other African nations,” the agency said in a statement on social media. “She earned her crown, she earned her title, and she deserves the opportunity to represent the nation she proudly serves.”

The contestant from Zimbabwe, Roseanna Hall, said on social media that she, too, had not received a U.S. visa. “I will not give up,” she said. Contestants from South Sudan and the Republic of Congo also faced delays, according to news reports.

The Miss Universe organization did not immediately reply to a request for comment. The Department of Homeland Security also did not respond to a similar request. It was unclear why the contestants had not received visas.

The social media page of the Miss Universe organization listed 20 African countries among its contestants for the pageant to held in the U.S. territory.

The Trump administration has curtailed several avenues to legal migration to the United States as part of a crackdown, including visa bans and restrictions that have hit African countries hard. The administration in January imposed full or partial travel bans and visa restrictions on 36 countries, and 29 of those are in Africa.

In December, the Trump administration widened travel restrictions to additional countries, including Zambia and Zimbabwe, while keeping in place full restrictions for citizens of the Republic of Congo and adding them for South Sudan.

Homeland Security has said it was “slamming the door shut on the foreign invaders who have undermined our national security.”

Citizens of African countries have for decades complained of draconian visa rules imposed by the governments of the United States and European countries. Governments say the restrictions aim to control migration and prevent fraud, but many Africans say they impede legitimate professional, business and study opportunities and stop family members from attending occasions such as births, weddings and funerals.

Those restrictions have become more severe since public attitudes in many Western countries have turned against immigration over the past decade.

Matthew Mpoke Bigg is the East Africa bureau chief for The New York Times, based in Nairobi, Kenya.

#137. Espionage accusations: a pressure tactic for Turkish government critics

This week's arrest of an employee of a German foundation in Istanbul has put focus back on the Erdogan administration's pressure tactics. The arrest is far from the first of its kind.

When police officers appeared at her home in Istanbul last Wednesday evening, Yonca Verdioglu Sik was surprised — even more so when they took her in for questioning. Her lawyer says she was accused of political and military espionage.

But, according to transcripts of the interrogation that were published later, Verdioglu Sik was never asked any questions about espionage. Instead the public prosecutor's office appeared to be more interested in the activities of the German organization, the Heinrich Böll Foundation, for whom Verdioglu Sik works. For 18 years, Verdioglu Sik, who was born and raised in Germany, has been the foundation's project coordinator. In her role, she organizes projects that focus on democracy and women's, minority and LGBTQ+ rights.

During the interrogation, transfers of money from the foundation into her bank account were discussed. Verdioglu Sik found out that she was now subject to a travel ban too.

Verdioglu Sik was eventually released. Nonetheless her arrest caused outrage among critics of the Turkish government. Sik is married to investigative journalist and member of parliament, Ahmet Sik. He belongs to the left-wing Workers' Party of Turkey, or TIP, and is known as one of the current government's harshest critics.

Recently Sik has been looking into a stock market scandal, which allegedly benefitted members of the ruling Justice and Development Party, or AKP, headed by Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan, and their associates.

Opposition critics drew a clear conclusion: As a member of parliament, Sik has parliamentary immunity. So instead, the justice system is putting pressure on him through his wife.

"If you take away a state's law, what is left is no longer called a state, but a gang," Sik responded to the arrest in writing.

Verdioglu Sik's arrest is not the first such case in Turkey. Ever since the attempted coup against the Erdogan government in 2016, accusations of espionage have been levelled at a wide variety of groups in Turkey: journalists, opposition figures and human rights activists as well as employees of foreign embassies and staff of foreign institutions like non-governmental organizations and foundations.

Such accusations were often followed by acquittals or convictions for other offenses, albeit sometimes years later. What the many instances have in common though is what's known as a "chilling effect." Any investigation can mean lengthy pre-trial detention, reputational and professional damage and even social isolation.

In the past such cases have even triggered diplomatic spats.

For example in 2015, Can Dundar and Erdem Gul, both senior journalists at the opposition daily Cumhuriyet, were accused of espionage after reporting that Turkey had tried to ship arms to rebels fighting the Syrian government. Dundar now lives in exile in Berlin.

In 2017, Erdogan said that reporter Deniz Yücel, then working for German newspaper Die Welt, was a "German agent." That led to a serious diplomatic crisis between Berlin and Ankara.

Espionage charges have also been laid against local employees of the US diplomatic corps and against a lawyer working for the German embassy. US pastor Andrew Brunson and his wife were also arrested because of alleged links to Kurdish anti-government groups. Turkish philanthropist and human rights activist Osman Kavala, who was charged with espionage and attempting to overthrow the constitutional order, remains in prison.

Most recently, espionage charges have been brought against the former Istanbul mayor and Erdogan's political competitor, Ekrem Imamoglu, and a number of journalists.

Hakki Tas, a research fellow and expert on Turkey at the German Institute for Global and Area Studies, or GIGA, notes that the number of such cases have been increasing over the past few years.

On the one hand, Turkey has the right to protect itself from foreign intelligence services — especially given geopolitical competition and conflict in the region, he argues. On the other hand, there's a long tradition of bringing the Turkish opposition to court to face security charges.

"What foreigners or employees of foreign institutions have experienced in recent years is a continuation of this pattern," Tas told DW.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Media outlets and NGOs critical of the government have faced investigations, trials and other sorts of state sanctions for years. The ruling AKP party has also brought in its own version of a so-called "foreign agent law."

These sorts of espionage cases serve both a domestic and foreign policy function, Tas observes.

For locals, "contact with a foreign institution, foreign funding or even a perfectly normal salary could become part of an investigation someday," Tas explains. "For opposition figures and civil society organizations, in particular, this can become a security issue."

In terms of foreign policy, the cases also show other countries that there are limits to their activities inside Turkey. These kinds of accusations seem to become more frequent when there's some sort of foreign policy tension — for example, after Western criticism of how opposition groups were dealt with after the alleged coup in 2016, the aforementioned disputes about arms deliveries in connection with Syria, or criticism from abroad about the removal of elected mayors in Turkey.

It can also serve as a kind of "hostage diplomacy," Tas says.

The case of US cleric Brunson is a good example. After his arrest, the Turkish government proposed a trade with the Americans. They wanted to exchange Turkish preacher Fethullah Gulen, who lived in the US and who the Turkish government blamed for the 2016 coup attempt.

This suspicion of foreign institutions isn't something that the Erdogan government invented, Tas points out. In the early 2000s, several representatives of German organizations were already standing trial in Turkey. At the time, it was alleged that, among other things, the German spy service was using German foundations as a front for conspiracy.

In some of the best-known cases, defendants often don't end up convicted of espionage.

The Kavala case demonstrates this. He was initially acquitted of espionage several times and spent two years in prison, just because of those charges.

But Tas says that's no surprise, referring to a 1979 work about the sociology of law written by US law professor Malcolm Feeley. It's title: "The Process Is the Punishment."

This story was originally published in German.

#136. Microsoft says services fully restored after global outage hits governments

Tech company says operations restored to all customers and regions affected by one of worst IT failures in years

Microsoft has said its service has been fully restored after a massive outage that saw widespread disruption to governments, businesses and public services around the world.

The technology company said on Saturday morning it had restored service to the remaining customers affected by one of the worst IT failures in years. On its Microsoft 365 status page, the US-based firm said: “We have completed our efforts to address the underlying issue of the incident and restored service to the remaining impacted customers and regions.

“Further updates can be found at MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center.”

The outage, which began on Thursday, affected governments, courts, healthcare services, businesses, airlines and banks. Governments including those of Japan, Singapore and Australia reported that the hours-long episode had affected some of their operations.

In the UK, where NHS England has a five-year deal to use Microsoft 365, vital public services were hit, with hospital staff resorting to paper records and one union reporting that ambulance services had suffered a “serious impact”.

The already backlogged court system was also hit, with juries in some cases being sent home early or prisoners not being sent to court, while at Minshull Street crown court in Manchester staff scrambled to locate a fax machine.

In the US, school districts told parents they must reach out by phone or online apps to contact their child’s school, while staffers at the House of Representatives told Politico they had lost a day of work.

One detention facility in Colorado said they were unable to take online bond payments to release detainees and told anyone needing to post bond for immediate release to do so in person at the jail.

In Australia, many people who rely on Microsoft 365 reported being unable to access their inboxes, including at councils, courts and schools. The federal police and Asio, Australia’s national domestic security and intelligence agency, were also affected by the outage.

Asio’s director general, Mike Burgess, said the agency’s systems “operate on multiple levels”, adding that they had been affected “on one level, yes, but not completely”.

Across Europe, government agencies in the Netherlands, Belgium and France reported problems, with a French municipality instructing citizens to use the phone instead of emailing.

In Japan, government email services became unavailable and staff resorted to text messaging. The shipping firm Sagawa Express was unable to accept some online reservations while Taisho, a major pharmaceutical company, said it was having to handle orders by phone.

The Indian airlines IndiGo and Akasa Air said their email support services were affected, and Taiwanese security firms, life insurers and investment trust companies told Reuters that fund management services had been disrupted.

Users reported that Microsoft 365 services, including Outlook, Teams and the AI assistant Copilot, were interrupted. Downdetector, an online tracker, said it received outage reports from users in more than 70 countries and territories.

Approximately 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps, Microsoft said on its website. Problems began at about midday on Thursday in the US and at 6pm in the UK.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

The Washington-based company apologised on Friday for the disruptions and said the outage was “not related to cyber” or artificial intelligence agents. It said the incident “was introduced by an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade that surfaced” after a recent network change.

The disruptions offer the latest example of the cascading problems that can erupt when multiple communications and technology platforms depend on the same provider. The outage has fuelled concerns in the UK about an overreliance on a small number of tech companies.

Steven Murdoch, a professor of security engineering at University College London, told the Guardian an outage of this size was usually “the result of some surprising interaction between systems. Each of the systems is normally quite well understood but interactions between them are complex and frequently the cause of large outages like this.

“It’s very hard to build a complex system without there being some risk of some failure.”

Tim Clement-Jones, a former chair of the House of Lords artificial intelligence select committee, said the widespread outages of vital government services were an “I-told-you-so moment” highlighting Britain’s dependence on US tech.

Jeremy Singer, a professor at the University of Glasgow’s School of Computing Science, said: “This problem serves to emphasise the dependence we have built on one company’s digital infrastructure, and may lead us to question whether we need more resilience in our IT ecosystem.”

The total number of people affected was not clear, but Microsoft said in January it had more than 450m paid commercial subscriptions to its 365 services.

#135. Interest rate rises likely in coming months, Bank of England’s chief economist says

‘We have further to go,’ says Huw Pill after increase to 4% last month as Bank seeks to tackle rising inflation

The Bank of England will probably need to raise interest rates next month and again in early 2027 to combat rising inflation, its chief economist has said.

Signalling a sharp rise in mortgage payments for an estimated 2.3 million homeowners who will have to refinance their loans next year, Huw Pill said that while the decision to nudge interest rates from 3.75% to 4% earlier this month was a “significant step” towards getting monetary policy into a better place, “we ⁠have further to go”.

Pill told the Financial Times: “My view would be, we will see evidence that motivates further rises,” adding that the central bank would “do ‌what is necessary to get inflation back to 2% through time”.

The chief economist is one of a number of the Bank’s policymakers who believe the UK economy has gained in strength over the last year, handing workers the power to bid up wages.

Recent figures showed the economy grew by 0.7% in the third quarter, to give an annual rate of growth that was only surpassed by the US among the G7 group of wealthy countries.

With inflation expected to rise to 5% in spring next year, driven by high oil and gas prices as a result of the Iran war, Pill expects public and private sector workers to demand substantial pay rises, which would keep inflation high for a longer period.

Pill said he considered an end to the US-Israeli ‌conflict with Iran and a fall in energy prices to be a “very low probability”, signalling to the newspaper that he was poised to vote for another ​quarter-point increase next month.

“My bias, for what it’s worth, is that I worry about the inflationary impact being magnified by second-round effects,” he said, adding that he believed the central bank should have acted sooner to combat inflation. “In my ⁠view, we waited too long.”

While the Bank has not seen evidence of wage increases or companies being able to pass on higher costs to consumers, Pill said the absence of evidence was not evidence of absence.

“As we go into next year, we will begin to see some evidence, and it could play out in one direction or another – we’ll see.”

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Financial markets expect UK interest rates to rise to almost 5% in 2027. Pill said these expectations were “not outrageous”, hinting that he could vote for more substantial increases in the cost of borrowing at the next meetings of the Bank’s monetary policy committee (MPC) because the “ability to be ​modest [in the scale ​of rate rises] has diminished”.

City analysts have interpreted the increasingly hawkish comments of Pill and other MPC members as making an interest rate rise in December almost inevitable. But there is disagreement about the strength of the economy, with many concerned that further increases in the cost of borrowing will hit the manufacturing sector and the construction industry, which has contracted for much of the year.

Households are also likely to cut back on spending in the new year, when the energy price cap is expected to rise to almost £2,000 for a typical home and council tax bills increase by an average of almost 5%.

#132. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

Scan the QR code to download the CNN app from the Apple Store.

#131. Here’s the latest.

The stoppage stranded thousands of passengers at airports across the country. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said old equipment was overloaded, causing “instability” in radar communications.

The nationwide ground stop that brought all air travel to a halt across the country and stranded thousands of passengers has been canceled, according to the Federal Aviation Administration, though delays from the outage are expected to persist in the coming days.

The order on Friday halted nearly all departures and stranded thousands of passengers at airports across the country. Hundreds of flights were diverted to other airports and more were canceled, even as some internationally bound flights managed to secure permission to take off. Many passengers were taken off flights or told to go home. Chaos spread at airports as travelers sought answers from airline and airport employees.

Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, warned that it would take “some time” for air travel nationwide to recover from the ground stop. Speaking at a news conference on Friday night shortly after flight traffic had been restored, Mr. Duffy predicted that flight schedules would be fully back to normal by the time the Thanksgiving travel season begins.

“I believe that it’s going to take us some time to get back to capacity,” he said, adding: “We’re going to have some makeup to do.”

Mr. Duffy decried Congress, accusing lawmakers of dragging their feet when it came to fully funding upgrades he warned were vital to prevent similar failures from happening again.

“It’s going to continue to fail,” Mr. Duffy told reporters, adding: “How many times does this have to happen for Congress to give the money to fix it?”

Mr. Duffy said that the antiquated radar and telecoms systems that controllers rely on to transmit data and other information between facilities began to falter at 3:36 p.m. The aging system, which has undergone a number of upgrades, became overloaded, officials said.

Appearing alongside him, Markwayne Mullin, the secretary of homeland security, stressed that there was never any indication that the communications failures had been triggered by a cyberattack, but he added, “we are still looking, and we’re not going to rule it out.” He emphasized that lives were never in danger.

But the incident appeared to inject new fuel into Mr. Duffy’s now monthslong campaign to secure at least $30 billion in extra funding for air traffic control modernization.

Last year, Congress approved $12.5 billion to upgrade aging air traffic facilities and technology, funds Mr. Duffy has repeatedly referred to as a “down payment” when asking for tens of billions more. On Friday night, Mr. Duffy appeared to up the ante, telling reporters that he didn’t just want another $30 billion for the project, but another $50 billion. He argued that it should be delivered in emergency funding before the end of the year, after which control of Congress — and the budget — switches from Republicans to Democrats.

Some Democratic lawmakers have already raised questions about how efficiently and effectively the F.A.A. has spent the money they have been given thus far. But Mr. Duffy has long argued that his department cannot be expected to complete a meaningful overhaul of the nation’s air traffic control system without a massive influx of funding.

Friday’s nationwide ground stop was only the third that the F.A.A. has issued this century. It ranks as the second-longest, after the ground stop issued on Sept. 11, 2001, in the wake of the terrorist attacks on the World Trade Center in New York. There was a shorter one on Jan. 11, 2023, because of an outage in the F.A.A.’s system to alert pilots to safety issues.

More than 2 million people pass through airport security checkpoints on a typical day, according to Transportation Security Administration data. On Thursday, the agency screened nearly 2.5 million people. According to FlightRadar24, an aviation tracking site, there were more than 1,000 airborne aircraft bound for U.S. airports during the nationwide ground stop. According to the flight tracking app Flighty, there were more than 30,000 departures and arrivals scheduled for the remainder of the day.

It could take days for air travel to fully normalize after an event of this scale, said Vikrant Vaze, a Dartmouth professor specializing in commercial aviation logistics.

“It’s going to take a lot of gymnastics” to get every airline’s aircraft, passengers and crew members back in the correct positions, he said.

If your flight is canceled: You are entitled to a refund if your flight is canceled in the United States, regardless of the reason, or if a flight is significantly delayed or changed and you decide not to take it. Here are other travel tips that might be helpful. Read more ›

A busy travel month: The ground stop happened about two weeks before Thanksgiving, one of the busiest times of the year for air travel. On average, U.S. airlines are expected to fly about 2.7 million passengers each day over the holiday period, according to industry trade group Airlines for America.

The last ground stop: The last nationwide ground stop came in the early morning of Jan. 11, 2023, and lasted around 90 minutes. The disruption came after a system to alert pilots of real-time dangers went down. Read more ›

Christine Chung, Niraj Chokshi, Luke Broadwater, Dustin Volz, Lisa Lerer and Robert Chiarito contributed reporting.

At Los Angeles International Airport, a number of travelers who had been hoping to get a late flight out have now been notified that their flights have been canceled. Many travelers here are scrambling to find new flights for Saturday as well as accommodation for the night.

Their flight to Jacksonville cancelled, longtime friends Ann Bruns, 79, and Janice Simpson, 78, opted to hire a driver to shuttle them six hours from Atlanta to Florida, where they hope to catch a cruise ship departing on Sunday.

“We’re about to go on a cruise ship with no clothes,” Ms. Simpson explained, because their luggage had already been loaded onto a plane bound for Jacksonville.

Ms. Bruns described a day punctuated by confusion, saying when the duo arrived in Atlanta from Kansas City, “We saw pilots just standing there, and we knew something was up.” They still feel like they have almost no idea what went wrong to send air travel into turmoil.

The Federal Aviation Administration issued a rare nationwide ground stop Friday afternoon, after outdated air traffic communications equipment became overloaded with data and began to fail, officials said.

The outage stranded thousands of passengers and caused widespread delays from which airlines and airports are still struggling to recover. It also highlighted persistent vulnerabilities in the aging U.S. air traffic infrastructure and a funding fight, as administration officials demanded lawmakers approve tens of billions of dollars more to accelerate systemwide upgrades.

Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, told reporters at the White House Friday night that the radar and other air traffic communications started to falter at 3:36 p.m. when old routers became overloaded with data. While the failures were not universal, they were pervasive enough to prompt officials to issue the unusual nationwide moratorium on takeoffs, Mr. Duffy said.

Appearing alongside him at the White House, Markwayne Mullin, the homeland security secretary, stressed that there was no evidence that nefarious activity had triggered the technology failures.

“We’ve seen zero evidence that this was a cyberattack,” Mr. Mullin said, suggesting that new equipment and new technology the Mr. Duffy’s department had added to the old infrastructure was what had overwhelmed it.

Mr. Duffy insisted that the failure was caused by the antiquated, legacy communications equipment, not recent upgrades to the system.

“What we have is an incredibly old air traffic control system,” Mr. Duffy said, predicting that until significant upgrades are made, “it’s going to continue to fail.”

Mr. Duffy warned that it would take “some time” for air travel nationwide to recover from the ground stop, predicting flight schedules would be fully back to normal by the time the Thanksgiving travel season begins.

“I believe that it’s going to take us some time to get back to capacity,” he said, adding: “We’re going to have some makeup to do.”

Mr. Duffy said that the F.A.A. would prioritize larger commercial flights ahead of private and charter flights, to get previously grounded flights off the ground.

Mr. Duffy has long been pushing for a sweeping overhaul of the U.S. air traffic infrastructure, routinely complaining that controllers have been hampered by the country’s failure to upgrade outdated equipment.

“We have old, old switches, old routers — we use 1980s little copper lines,” he said to reporters Friday. Only last week, the F.A.A. announced that it had begun to phase out the use of floppy disks.

Last year, Congress approved $12.5 billion to fund upgrades to the country’s aging air traffic facilities and networks, some of which has been directed toward communications system upgrades. Mr. Duffy noted Friday that air traffic facilities that had been upgraded to digital systems did not experience the same overloading issues that prompted the F.A.A. to issue the ground stop order.

But he stressed that having an air traffic control system dependent on a “patchwork” of upgrades and fixes was untenable, calling on Congress to spend tens of billions more to allow for more upgrades.

For months, Mr. Duffy has been calling on the Republican-controlled Congress to give his department another $30 billion to accelerate efforts to upgrade air traffic control systems. On Friday night, he upped the ante, telling reporters he wanted $50 billion — and that he wanted it before the end of the year.

“This is an emergency, I want Congress to give us emergency funding,” he said, adding: “How many more times does this have to happen for Congress to give the money to fix it?”

But it is unclear whether Republicans will prioritize such a sum before the end of the year, after which control of Congress shifts to the Democrats, who are expected to scrutinize how the F.A.A. has utilized air traffic funding they have received so far. Republican lawmakers have filed bills in the House and Senate to authorize $30 billion in air traffic upgrades, but neither piece of legislation has cosponsors.

Later on Friday, Representative Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the House’s top-ranking Democrat, appeared to reject Mr. Duffy’s accusing tone and shifted the blame back to the Trump administration.

“Today’s unprecedented breakdown is the latest in a series of unacceptable air travel incidents under the Trump administration’s failed leadership,” he said in a statement, adding that “American travelers deserve timely answers from the Trump administration about how and why this happened and assurances that steps are being taken to ensure that our air travel system remains safe and functional.”

Nationwide, more than 8,200 flights — or over 31 percent of those scheduled in the United States for Friday — were canceled, according to Cirium, an aviation data firm. And the disruption was already spilling over into Saturday, usually a slower day for travel, with more than 5 percent of flights canceled before the day had even begun.

Reporting from Denver, where they are stranded along with much of the Democratic Party.

Pete Buttigieg could not believe what he had just watched. The former transportation secretary stood at the corner of the hotel bar in Denver, where CNN had just finished broadcasting the Trump administration’s news conference about the ground stop that stopped flights nationwide on Friday.

Mr. Buttigieg was among the thousands of Democrats who had gathered in Denver this week for a conference designed to chart the party’s future and celebrate their midterm wins. The ground stop stranded some of the party’s top rising stars and their staffs.

People accustomed to having inside information suddenly had very little.

At a table to Mr. Buttigieg’s left sat Gov. Abigail Spanberger of Virginia, Gov. Mikie Sherrill of New Jersey and Senator Elissa Slotkin of Michigan. They munched on Parmesan fries and checked their phones as they waited for news of their flights.

To his right, Senator Raphael Warnock of Georgia scanned his Senate group chats to check for updates from secure channels and Senator Mark Warner of Virginia.

But Mr. Buttigieg, glancing up at the television, struggled to comprehend the performance of his successor, Secretary Sean Duffy.

“You can just write down the comic book asterisk swearing characters,” Mr. Buttigieg said when a New York Times reporter asked for his impressions of the news conference. “I have questions.”

The downtown hotel bar resembled the famous bar scene in “Star Wars.” The stranded politicians bemoaned the lack of information from the federal government and their inability to get home.

One New England-based operative booked a rental car and planned to drive back to Boston. Others looked up how many hours it would take to get to the East Coast on Amtrak. (The answer: more than 40.) Aides to one prominent Democratic governor, who had flown to Denver on a private jet, fielded questions from others in the bar about whether they could hitch a ride home.

A few people working for Gov. JB Pritzker of Illinois — who did not come to Denver — commandeered a long table where Anne Caprara, his chief of staff, worked two phones as she juggled rumors, emergencies back home and calls from the boss. She said Mr. Pritzker was furious about the lack of information coming from the Trump administration.

“Welcome to Denver’s hottest bar, the Mos Eisley Cantina of politics,” Ms. Caprara said, referring to the “Star Wars” bar. “With ‘Veep’ characters and a ‘Terminator 2’ vibe.”

Representative Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the leader of the House Democrats, shifted the blame for Friday’s ground stop back to the Trump administration. In a statement, he called the stop “the latest in a series of unacceptable air travel incidents under the Trump Administration’s failed leadership.”

Earlier Friday night, Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, had blamed Congress for not adequately funding his department’s plans to upgrade the nation’s air traffic infrastructure.

“American travelers deserve timely answers from the Trump Administration about how and why this happened,” Mr. Jeffries added.

A communications outage on Friday led the Federal Aviation Administration to pause departing flights nationwide, but pilots were not entirely cut off from federal air traffic controllers.

In a Friday night news conference, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said the outage was intermittent, so federal controllers were still able to reach pilots.

“It wasn’t a complete blackout in communication or radar,” he said. “So we were able to keep some of those communications going.”

But even in a complete outage, aviation authorities have multiple backups to maintain contact with pilots.

Pilots and air traffic controllers primarily communicate by voice radio, using primary, backup and emergency frequencies.

Controllers and airline dispatchers can also send text-based messages to pilots. And many commercial planes are equipped with satellite communications, which are especially useful for reaching people on the ground when the planes are flying in remote airspace like over the ocean.

If a control tower loses radio capabilities, it can also communicate with pilots with high-intensity colored lights, which pilots can acknowledge by rocking their wings or flashing lights in response.

Airlines began to restore some flights after the nationwide stop was lifted. United Airlines said that while many of its flights had been canceled, some were still expected to operate.

American Airlines said it was prioritizing flights to non-hub airports and international destinations in order to get planes and crews in place to recover quickly. Southwest Airlines said it was resuming a “limited number of flights” on Friday.

Some professional and college sports teams were stuck on runways, or opting to pile into buses, ahead of games this weekend that are now uncertain due to the nationwide ground stop that was lifted Friday night.

Markwayne Mullin, the secretary of homeland security, said at a news conference on Friday that the ground stop was caused by technology upgrades “overwhelming” the flight communication system. Thousands of flights have been canceled, but no high-profile games have been affected so far, ahead of what is set to be a busy weekend in sports.

Dozens of teams had made it out of airports just ahead of the stop. But the N.B.A.’s Brooklyn Nets were not so lucky.

The Athletic reported that the Nets were stuck on a team plane on Friday. They have a game Saturday night against the Memphis Grizzles.

Also stuck at an airport was the University of Texas Longhorns football team, slated to play Louisiana State University in Baton Rouge, La., on Saturday night. Minutes before the stoppage, the Longhorns posted a video of players boarding a plane with the caption “Bayou bound.”

Both teams are in the Southeastern Conference, which said in a statement that it would work “to determine whether any adjustments to scheduled competitions become necessary.”

On Friday night, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy told reporters that he encouraged college football teams to coordinate their flights with the Federal Aviation Administration “to make sure we have games going off tomorrow.”

While some teams, including the Longhorns, headed home from the airport to wait out the delay, a handful of others opted to travel the old-fashioned way: By bus.

The Athletic reported that among the longest drives was a 330-mile ride for the Purdue University Boilermakers, who are playing the University of Iowa Hawkeyes on Saturday night.

The University of Southern California women’s basketball team said they were grounded in Los Angeles ahead of a marquee game Sunday against the University of South Carolina Gamecocks, but were preparing to travel on Saturday.

In men’s college basketball, a Saturday game between University of North Carolina Greensboro and Cornell University in Ithaca, N.Y., was postponed because of the ground stop.

N.F.L. teams typically travel on Saturday for Sunday games. Five teams told The Athletic that their travel plans were not affected.

According to The Athletic, the stakes of the ground stop may be most significant for the National Women’s Soccer League, which is scheduled to hold its semifinals this weekend. The stop affected the Kansas City Current’s flight to New York City ahead of its game against Gotham FC on Sunday. The Utah Royals, playing the Washington Spirit on Saturday, traveled before the stoppage.

The N.B.A.’s Cleveland Cavaliers had planned to catch a flight home after their Friday night game in Miami. Kenny Atkinson, the Cavaliers head coach, told reporters before the game that he didn’t know if the team would make their flight.

“I think we’re scrambling to find hotel rooms in case,” Mr. Atkinson said, adding: “If not, we’ll sleep on the bus.”

Alexander CardiaNeil Bedi and Robin Stein

Radio transmissions between active pilots and air traffic controllers at major airports offer a peek at how they navigated the Federal Aviation Administration’s nationwide ground stoppage on Friday.

An air traffic controller at Newark Liberty International Airport can be heard sharing the news with pilots.

Minutes later, at airports in Atlanta and Chicago, controllers said they had limited information.

As planes landed over the next hour and departures were stuck at their gates, air traffic controllers can be heard talking about significant gate issues and comparing the tarmac to a “parking lot.”

Amid the chaos, one pilot noted the date while talking to a Newark controller: “It’s not lost on me that it’s Friday the 13th.”

Passengers groaned loudly as an announcement at Newark Liberty International Airport ordered them back to their gates for both domestic and international flights. “Are you kidding me? When will this end?” one woman cried out.

At Kennedy International Airport in New York, passengers waited to board a flight to Paris that was delayed because the pilot hadn’t been able to take his flight from Boston to J.F.K. Instead, he decided to drive, and passengers have been receiving updates about his E.T.A. over the loudspeaker. The latest update said he was expected to arrive at the airport at 9:45 p.m. When it was announced that the flight would begin boarding before his arrival, one woman raised both hands over her head with her fingers crossed.

More than 6,600 flights on Friday had been canceled by 9:30 p.m. Eastern, according to Flight Aware, a flight-tracking service. And that disruption had already spilled over into the weekend, with about 1,500 flights on Saturday canceled as well.

Pete Buttigieg, who served as the transportation secretary during the Biden administration, is stranded in Denver and mingling in the bar at the Ritz-Carlton hotel. After watching the Trump administration’s press conference, Buttigieg seemed exasperated. “You can just write down the comic book asterisk swearing characters,” Buttigieg said. “I have questions.”

It could take days for air travel to fully normalize after an event of this scale, said Vikrant Vaze, a Dartmouth professor specializing in commercial aviation logistics. “It’s going to take a lot of gymnastics” to get every airline’s aircraft, passengers and crew members back in the correct positions, he said.

Some contingency measures can be taken by airlines to get things back on track, such as flying reserve crews or “ferrying” an empty aircraft. It’s helpful that airlines have the nighttime hours, when there are typically fewer domestic flights, to push through some of the backlog, Vaze said.

Cheers and applause erupted in the Delta Sky Club at Los Angeles International Airport, where travelers just heard an announcement that the nationwide ground stop had been lifted. At least three Delta flights at the airport were already boarding, but it could take some time for other flights to get going, a Delta agent said in the announcement.

The White House briefing by the cabinet secretaries is now over.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy appeared to suggest that his effort to upgrade air traffic control infrastructure may have contributed to the outage, but he defended the upgrades by arguing that they had to be done.

“By doing nothing, you inject way more risk into the system,” he said, adding, “I didn’t anticipate that we would have an outage like this.”

Duffy has criticized Congress throughout this briefing for not allocating more funding to air traffic infrastructure.

“I can’t build things if I don’t have money,” Duffy said, shaking his head. “We should talk to our senators and members of Congress to say, ‘We don’t deserve this.’”

Sean Duffy has been clamoring for another $30 billion in air traffic modernization funds from Congress for several months now. On Friday night, he appeared to up the ante, saying he didn’t want just $30 billion — he wanted $50 billion. But the official ask, which he has made repeatedly and which is reflected in legislation he has endorsed, is another $30 billion.

In the last few weeks, Republicans in the House and Senate filed bills to give Duffy that $30 billion. Neither has any co-sponsors.

Asked if travelers should expect disruptions to their Thanksgiving travel plans, Sean Duffy said he thought flights would be “back up and operational as we come into the heat of travel” ahead of the holiday.

In the aftermath of a frightening air-traffic control technology failure that affected Newark Liberty International Airport in April 2025, Duffy argued that the nation’s air-traffic control systems were outdated and in need of a $12.5 billion upgrade. Tonight he is using the latest technology failure as an argument for another huge increase of funding.

Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said that new technology upgrades overloaded the existing, antiquated system, “overwhelming” the existing equipment.

“It’s going to continue to fail,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said of the antiquated technology that the F.A.A. was trying to upgrade, triggering the telecom failures that prompted the nationwide ground stop.

The question of older F.A.A. systems ceasing to work has been a constant throughout Duffy’s tenure as transportation secretary, going back to disputes he had with Elon Musk about what type of technology could be used for backup systems.

The Congress that Duffy has been criticizing has been entirely in Republican hands for the last two years. It’s now about to switch to Democratic control.

“Our fix gives us a pretty good indication that it was a legacy network problem that went down,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said, hinting at the origins of the crisis. He said the problem was related to old equipment being overloaded.

Duffy and Mullin were asked if they could say with “100 percent certainty” that this was not the result of a cyberattack or rogue artificial intelligence. “There’s nothing certain right now,” Mullin said. “It doesn’t appear that way.”

“I want Congress to give us emergency funding,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said, calling on lawmakers to give the Trump administration another $30 billion, at least, to pay for further upgrades to air traffic control infrastructure. But some lawmakers have raised questions about how the F.A.A. has managed the funds it has been given thus far.
