#129. Sudan: Misery worsens amid plummeting aid for displaced people

Sudan's humanitarian crisis is exacerbated by cuts in international aid. Almost 34 million people are in need as war and drone strikes are making it difficult to deliver aid to the population.

The International Organization for Migration (IOM) and the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees (UNHCR) are sounding the alarm: Serious funding gaps have depleted the supplies of emergency shelter and basic household goods intended for Sudan. Without urgent provision of flexible funding, even more families will have to cope without adequate protection from extreme weather conditions, insecurity, violence and exploitation in the coming months.

According to a recently published joint statement, $2.9 billion (€2.62bn) is needed for the United Nation's Humanitarian Needs and Response Plan. This funding is intended to provide life-saving aid to 20.4 million people in Sudan. So far, however, only 47% of the required funds have been raised.

A total of 33.7 million people in the war- and famine-stricken country are dependent on humanitarian aid. Some 15.4 million alone require emergency shelter and basic household items. However, due to a lack of funding, aid organizations have been able to assist only 10%.

More than three and a half years after the war began between the Sudanese Armed Forces (SAF) and paramilitary Rapid Support Forces (RSF), Sudan is still the site of the world's largest humanitarian and displacement crisis. About 8.6 million people fled within the country to escape the escalating violence and more than 4.6 million fled to neighboring countries. Another 5.2 million have returned to their regions of origin despite the ongoing war. However, their towns and infrastructure have been destroyed. Fighting, drone strikes and logistical problems further complicate the delivery of humanitarian aid.

Between 40,000 and 250,000 people have been killed. Accurate or updated figures are not available as fighting continues, satellite-based internet communication is curbed, and many aid organizations and observers have left the country.

"It's hard to imagine how people are supposed to survive," Marina Peter, chairwoman of the Sudan and South Sudan Forum in Germany, told DW. In large parts of the country, people have been cut off from humanitarian aid since the war began. Some have survived by eating roots. According to the Sudan expert, the fact that people are still returning to war zones from neighboring countries is also linked to shortages: Food rations have been drastically cut in refugee camps.

Ivo Körner, Sudan specialist at Caritas International, criticizes the international community for the sharp decline in aid. "Globally, we're seeing a dramatic drop in funding for humanitarian aid," Körner told DW. It's not just the United States that has slashed its contributions; Germany and the European Union are also providing significantly less funding than they did four or five years ago, he said.

In Sudan, Caritas International supports refugees and the communities hosting them with cash assistance, drinking water supplies, basic medical care and seeds. However, Sudan's Caritas' projects in 2026 have been financed exclusively through donations. While Körner hopes for additional funding from the German Foreign Office for Sudan projects, he predicts that funding opportunities will be significantly more limited than in previous years.

According to Sudan expert Marina Peters, the consequences of the crisis are becoming increasingly difficult to assess. "In many parts of the country, people have already been completely cut off from humanitarian aid since the start of the war," she told DW. Peter also notes that child mortality in Sudan is above average. However, it is nearly impossible to determine the actual extent of the crisis more precisely because the warring parties continue to obstruct humanitarian access.

On October 23, the United Kingdom and 31 other international partners, including Germany, issued a joint statement on the looming famine in Sudan. According to the statement, 19.5 million people — about 41% of the population — are suffering from acute food insecurity. The countries are calling on the warring parties to allow unimpeded access for humanitarian aid and to refrain from attacks on civilian infrastructure. At the same time, they are demanding an increase in international aid funding.

While Marina Peter welcomes the appeal in principle, she sees a contradiction. "The question is: Who is this appeal actually directed at?" Most of the signatories have themselves systematically cut funding for humanitarian aid and development cooperation. The appeal to the international donor community is thus ultimately directed at the states themselves.

Meanwhile, the Sudanese government based in the country's capital Khartoum — led by General Abdel Fattah Al-Burhan's SAF — rejects all warnings of a famine.

In a statement dated October 26, it claims that the food situation in the country is reportedly reassuring and that government reserves and ongoing agricultural production are sufficient to meet demand. According to Khartoum, the warnings from Western countries amount to a politicization of the humanitarian situation.

Peter criticized this statement as "truly cynical and almost perfidious. The government's claim that humanitarian organizations have unrestricted access also does not reflect reality, she said. In many places, aid continues to be obstructed.

In addition to hunger and displacement, the threat to civilians posed by the use of drones is also growing. The Peace for Sudan Platform issued an appeal on November 5 calling for an end to attacks on civilians, markets, hospitals, and water and electricity supplies. In the first three weeks of October alone, at least 306 people were killed in attacks. According to the UN Human Rights Office, drones claimed the lives of at least 1,120 civilians in the first half of 2026.

"Drone strikes are a major problem," Caritas' Körner confirmed. They cause widespread anxiety among the population and often strike civilians. Added to this is the danger to humanitarian workers. This makes local partner organizations all the more important, he said. They enjoy the trust of the local population, are deeply rooted in the regions and in some cases are able to reach areas that international aid workers can hardly access.

#128. Microsoft says all services working again

US tech giant Microsoft says it has fixed problems that led to a global outage of its services. The glitch severely disrupted online communications in businesses and governments for more than 24 hours.

The US technology conglomerate Microsoft said on Saturday that a more than 24-hour global outage affecting its Microsoft 365 applications used by businesses and governments across the world has now ended.

"We ​have completed our efforts to address ​the underlying issue of the ⁠incident ​and ​restored service to ​the remaining impacted ‌customers and regions," ​the ⁠company posted on X.

Microsoft applications such as Teams, Outlook and Copilot were all hit by the outage, leading to communications problems in scores of countries.

The company has said it was "not aware" of any outside interference that could have been behind the outage, blaming the problem on server issues.

The disruption was one of the longest in Microsoft's history and the second this year.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The outage, which began around 1725 UTC on Thursday according to web testing and network diagnostics company ​Ookla, affected government agencies and businesses across much of the world.

It made it impossible to carry out activities such as sending emails and holding online meetings.

Germany was no exception, with a spokesperson for its Federal Ministry for Digital Transformation and Government Modernization saying that while the outage did not have a major impact on federal government functions, it did significantly affect ​German businesses.

Britain's state health service (NHS) was also severely affected, with personnel forced to have recourse to paper records rather than those stored online.

Altogether, there were more than 7.4 million reports of problems worldwide logged by Downdetector, which monitors online outages.

The disruption highlights the widespread and perilous reliance of governments and businesses on a very small number of Big Tech companies.

The problems at Microsoft are so far believed to be unconnected with the major communications outage that has affected US aviation.

#127. UK ministers told not to engage formally with Indian mega-zoo after lobbying

Guardian and Mongabay reveal approaches to officials and MPs on behalf of Vantara zoo ahead of UK-chaired Cites meeting on endangered animal imports

British government ministers were told not to engage formally with Vantara, the controversial mega-zoo run by the Indian billionaire Anant Ambani, after lobbying efforts by the private animal sanctuary in the run-up to a UK-chaired meeting on the trade in endangered species, according to documents.

This week, the UK heads a 32-country committee meeting in Geneva of Cites, the United Nations body that regulates trade in endangered species. The meeting will examine how India has responded to compliance concerns about imports of endangered animals.

Documents obtained by the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay under the UK’s Freedom of Information Act reveal Vantara’s efforts to lobby British officials and ministers before the meeting.

Ambani, 31, is the heir to the oil and gas fortune of one of India’s richest men, Mukesh Ambani. He set up a 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo near his family’s oil-refining facilities in Gujarat, where more than 150,000 animals have reportedly been amassed since 2022. Vantara boasts high welfare standards for animals in its care, including facilities such as an elephant hydrotherapy pool, where more than 260 elephants can also receive oil therapies and Ayurvedic massage.

But conservation groups have repeatedly questioned how such a vast number of species, many of which are endangered, were gathered so quickly. Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo’s operators of any wrongdoing.

A subsequent Cites fact-finding mission looking into the zoo raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Its November 2025 report said: “There is a need for caution to ensure that imports of large numbers of animals by these facilities are not inadvertently creating a demand for illegally sourced animals.”

In May, officials from the UK’s Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra) met a representative from Vantara and the US lobbyist firm Checkmate Government Relations, founded by Ches McDowell, a hunting friend of Donald Trump Jr.

Before the meeting, internal briefing documents said Checkmate was “seeking to persuade the UK to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara and of Checkmate’s argument that responsibility for verifying legality sits primarily with exporting or re-exporting parties rather than with India as importer”.

During the meeting, the Vantara representative invited UK officials to visit the zoo – an invitation that was subsequently declined to ensure the impartiality of the UK’s Cites chair role.

After the meeting, a representative from another lobbying firm, Atlas Strategy, working on behalf of Vantara, approached the then secretary of state, Emma Reynolds, and then nature minister, Mary Creagh, also raising the prospect of a visit to the zoo.

When a department official asked for advice, a note was issued recommending that ministers did not engage formally with Vantara.

The government documents also reveal concerns about an application by Vantara to import falcons and falcon hybrids, after an unnamed organisation made a £50,000 “donation” to a breeder in the UK, whose identity was also not disclosed. British Cites officials classified the transaction as commercial, according to the documents. The falcons were not exported to India.

A spokesperson for Vantara said it had not imported falcons from the UK, nor had the zoo hired or paid a lobbying firm. It is understood that Checkmate and Altas Strategy were working pro bono for Vantara and not required to register with the Office of the Registrar of Consultant Lobbyists.

After recent scrutiny, Vantara has sought to “reset” its activities by voluntarily suspending animal imports for a year and hiring John Scanlon, a former Cites chief, to oversee its operations. In an interview with the Sunday Times this year, Ambani said he only brought animals to Vantara legally to ensure their welfare, mentioning a group of hippos he had saved from a hunting lodge in South Africa and jackass penguins saved from a shopping centre in Qatar.

“I think since I was a baby I have been taking care of animals. It’s always been a passion,” he said.

Vantara was used to host Ambani’s lavish pre-wedding celebrations in 2024, which were attended by celebrity guests and politicians. In September, Newcastle University awarded the oil heir a professorship of practice for “his work in conservation, biodiversity protection, animal welfare and environmental stewardship”, sparking a backlash from conservationists.

A Defra spokesperson said: “The UK has strict rules in place to protect endangered species and ensuring international wildlife trade is legal and sustainable, with every application assessed individually against internationally agreed and domestic requirements.”

#124. How Does a 74-Year-Old in Argentina Escape House Arrest on the Fifth Floor?

It involved a heating pad, animal fat and a bomb scare. The police are still looking for an Italian man facing extradition on 1970s-era terrorism charges.

By Zane Irwin and Lucía Cholakian Herrera

Zane Irwin reported from New York. Lucia Cholakian Herrerra reported from Buenos Aires.

Time was running out for Leonardo Bertulazzi. After decades of safe harbor in Argentina, Mr. Bertulazzi, a former member of a far-left militant group that kidnapped and murdered Italy’s prime minister in 1978, faced imminent extradition.

Back in Italy, a 27-year prison sentence for terrorism-related crimes awaited Mr. Bertulazzi, according to Argentina’s defense ministry.

Mr. Bertulazzi, 74, was placed under house arrest. The police were stationed across the street from the fifth-floor apartment he shared with his wife in Buenos Aires, Argentina’s capital. He’d had three strokes in the past two years, his lawyer said, and he was required to wear an ankle monitor.

None of that seemed to stop Mr. Bertulazzi from planning his escape.

On Tuesday, while much of the law enforcement apparatus of Buenos Aires was focused on the visit by Pope Leo, two police officers checked on Mr. Bertulazzi during a routine shift change, according to an official with knowledge of an investigation into Mr. Bertulazzi’s flight who was not authorized to speak publicly.

But this time, when the officers rang the apartment’s doorbell, nobody answered. Investigators believe Mr. Bertulazzi had escaped between 6 a.m. and 8 a.m. that morning.

Mr. Bertulazzi’s ankle monitor still showed he was at the apartment, the official said. A search revealed a metal box holding a mess of wires plugged into an outlet, prompting the officers to call an explosives disposal unit, according to the official.

#123. The private creative world of Rabindranath Tagore, the man Yeats called ‘greater than any of us’

A previously unseen family archive offers a glimpse into the Indian Nobel prize winner’s artistic process and personal relationships in his final years

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

It’s easy to run out of words to describe Rabindranath Tagore. The first non-European to win the Nobel prize in literature, Tagore was a Bengali poet, musician, novelist, philosopher, songwriter, painter, humanist and educator.

Born in 1861, Tagore left an indelible mark on the subcontinent’s literature, music, education and art. Among his enduring legacies are the national anthems of India and Bangladesh.

As Nobel laureate economist Amartya Sen once wrote, Tagore was “a towering figure in the millennium-old literature of Bengal”, whose songs resonate across eastern India and Bangladesh, while his ideas on education, nationalism and freedom of the mind remain relevant today.

Yet his work also made a deep impression on the literary world far beyond the shores of India. WB Yeats is said to have described Tagore as “greater than any of us”. Gitanjali, the collection of poems that won Tagore the Nobel prize that same year, was praised by the committee for its “profoundly sensitive, fresh and beautiful verse”. Tagore’s English translation was published in London that March and reprinted 10 times by the time the prize was announced in November.

Tagore’s life and works have, of course, been well documented, but a collection of material preserved in a private family archive is now being brought into public view for the first time at an exhibition by Christie’s, opening in Mumbai this month. The archive draws from Tagore’s visits to the family home of his protege, Maitreyi Devi, in the bucolic village of Mungpoo in Darjeeling between 1938 and 1940. He died in 1941.

The collection on display – artworks, manuscripts, letters, photographs and personal effects – offers an intimate glimpse into Tagore’s creative process and personal world in his final years.

I spoke to the exhibition curator, Nishad Avari, head of south Asian modern and contemporary art at Christie’s, about the stories behind the archive.

One instructive document Avari points to is a manuscript from 1940. It appears to be a draft of Tagore’s memoir Chelebela or My Boyhood Days, in which he reflects on his life – from growing up in a large family in Calcutta (modern-day Kolkata) to the rapidly changing world ushered in by electricity and the motorcar.

For Avari, what stood out was how the ideas in the document echoed the educational philosophy Tagore put into practice in Santiniketan (or Abode of Peace), the experimental school he set up about 100 miles from the state capital.

“He speaks about how education is like being in prison and that it should be open and free … about the idea of wonder and even fear as inspiring creativity,” Avari told me.

“That’s a direct relationship to Santiniketan and the open model of the school where people sat under trees with their gurus and learned in the open. Education didn’t feel like a restriction, prison or imposition,” he added.

Avari points to another detail that makes this particular document valuable. Of the 10 complete manuscripts in the exhibition, it is the only one dictated to Devi, whose father was a close friend of Tagore’s. For Avari, this speaks to their close relationship and the trust he placed in her.

“If you look at the collection as a whole, she has copies of almost every single one of his publications signed to her by him,” he says. The collection includes hundreds of letters, too: first between Tagore and her father, and later between Tagore and Devi herself.

There are doodles among corrections, Avari explains, which show how his literary practice fed into his artistic practice. Alongside the manuscripts and correspondence are vintage photographs and personal effects, including a kurta and a lungi that Tagore left behind at the family home.

Among the artworks in the collection is a portrait of Devi, particularly notable because Tagore’s figure studies were not generally attributed to specific individuals in the way that portraits by his nephews, Abanindranath and Gaganendranath, often were, Avari said. There is also a landscape depicting the forests of Mungpoo, which Tagore embraced as a retreat.

On the international art market, Tagore’s paintings command significant prices. This year, Sotheby’s sold one of his watercolours for £1.02m (Rs 12.97 crore), nearly four times its low estimate.

Institutions such as Visva-Bharati in Santiniketan and the National Gallery of Modern Art hold much of Tagore’s archival materials, making the discovery of a private archive particularly rare.

He hopes that making these materials public will offer historians, scholars and admirers alike additional perspectives on Tagore. “We hope to add to the understanding of the artist, their career, and in this case, of course, our understanding of the man beyond his art as well.”

Rabindranath Tagore and Mungpoo runs between 10 November and 18 December at Christie’s space in south Mumbai.

Politics | India has invited Pakistan to attend the Shanghai Cooperation Organisation’s Civilisational Dialogue Forum in New Delhi later this month, the Indian Express reported. It is the first invitation since the two neighbouring states engaged in a military conflict last year.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Academia | Indian academic Mahmood Kooria, a lecturer at the University of Edinburgh, is among this year’s recipients of the Philip Leverhulme prize in Humanities. The £100,000 award recognises his work on the premodern Indian Ocean world.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Freddy Birdy, a copywriter whose delightful words have earned him a devoted Instagram following, recommends writer and historian Malvika Singh’s cookbook, Cooking for My Firefly.

“This book isn’t about recipes, though it does include them. It is a voyage through another time and place, tracing the social fabric of India of the 60s, 70s and 80s, trickling down to today. Told in Malvika’s beautiful prose, it takes us to a time when people entertained in homes, not in restaurants; when dishes were cooked in-house and there were no caterers or party planners. An era long before Instagram posts, WhatsApp invitations, celebrity chefs and food delivery apps, when the point of having a party was simply to have fun. It is an archive of photographs, food, culture, gossip, movie stars, politicians, royalty, set against the spectacular backdrop of Malvika’s dining tables through the decades. I like to dip into it every now and then, much as one might do standing in a dark kitchen with the light of the refrigerator enticing you to midnight goodies.”

Indians lost Rs 22,495 crore (£1.77bn) to cyberfraud in 2025, Times of India reported citing data from the Home Ministry. While the figure is marginally down from the previous year, losses have risen sharply since 2023.

At a time when public anger has spilled into the streets, artist Aamir Aziz’s new track, Dopeher Blues (Afternoon Blues), captures the frustration and angst of a generation that has lived through many crises.

Aziz describes the song as an expression of a period of deep suffocation. Issues such as farmer suicides, unemployment and conflicts in Palestine and Kashmir, he told me, are things he has grown up with even as public attention shifts with the news cycle. It is also a warning, in part, against internet culture, where injustices risk becoming normalised.

Aziz, 35, works in acting, music and writing, and became an internet sensation with his defiant poem Sab Yaad Rakha Jayega (Everything Will Be Remembered) during the citizenship protests. At the heart of his new track is a note of defiance and hope: “We’ll find a way to sing,” he says, as he calls on everyone “to keep speaking up even in a climate of fear, misinformation and oppression.”

#122. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#120. Environmentalists criticise choice of Earthshot prize venue owned by fossil fuel firm

Mumbai facility is part of Reliance Industries, owner of world’s biggest oil refinery and funder of controversial mega-zoo

The Prince of Wales’s Earthshot prize will host its forthcoming annual awards ceremony at a venue owned by the billionaire Ambani family conglomerate, drawing criticism from conservationists over their fossil fuel businesses and their foundation’s controversial mega-zoo.

A spokesperson for the Earthshot prize, which awards £1m to five winners every year to invest in conservation work, confirmed to the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay that this year’s ceremony will be held at the Jio World Convention Centre in Mumbai on 17 November.

The venue is part of Mukesh Ambani’s Reliance Industries, which also owns Jamnagar, the world’s biggest oil refinery in Gujarat on India’s western coast. The group’s philanthropic arm funds the 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo Vantara, which was founded by Mukesh’s son Anant Ambani and inaugurated by the Indian prime minister, Narendra Modi, last year. The zoo is embroiled in a long-running controversy over its vast collection of more than 150,000 animals, which reportedly includes some of the world’s most endangered species – from the radiated tortoise, native to Madagascar, to the Spix’s macaw, endemic to north-eastern Brazil but now considered extinct in the wild.

Megan Carr, of the Wildlife Animal Protection Forum South Africa (WAPFSA), warned that using a venue owned by Reliance “could lend an unearned environmental prestige to a corporate group currently under question by the global conservation community”.

Conservation groups, including WAPFSA, have raised concerns about the speed with which Vantara has amassed so many animals, and about how they were sourced. The site, near the family’s oil refinery, houses more than 2,000 species of animals.

This week, the 32-member committee that governs Cites, the UN-administered body that regulates trade in endangered species, requested that India submit another report on the steps it is taking to address concerns relating to its imports of protected species.

The committee also requested the Cites secretariat review 11 cases where there are unresolved questions over how animals were sent to Vantara.

Joanna Swift, the executive director of communications at the Earthshot prize, said it had not sought a philanthropic partnership for its award ceremony venue and that it was paying the standard commercial rate to rent the space.

“The [Jio World Convention Centre] was selected because it allowed us to host the event in Mumbai while meeting the practical requirements of an event of this size. It offers world-class facilities, technical infrastructure and experience of hosting major international events, including environmental ones,” Swift said. “In February this year, JWCC hosted the first ever Mumbai climate week.”

Vantara, Kensington Palace and JWCC have been approached for comment.

Vantara has previously denied allegations of wildlife trafficking and says its purpose is “to rescue wild animals in distress, provide treatment along with lifelong care, and rehabilitate those fit for the wild”.

“With regulatory approval, we also breed select endangered or extinct-in-the-wild species, serving as a modern Noah’s Ark to ensure their survival under human care and restore wild populations through reintroduction,” Vantara says on its website.

Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo of allegations that animals had been acquired illegally or mistreated.

A later Cites fact-finding mission raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Susan Cheyne, the vice-chair of the International Union for Conservation of Nature’s small apes section, said that Vantara’s model has not yet been shown to support conservation and “may be driving demand for wild animals in trade”.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

“For gibbons and other primates, this matters a great deal. Moving animals out of their range countries into a private facility does nothing for wild populations unless there is a transparent, credible plan for those animals: for welfare, for any conservation breeding with clear goals, and for return to range states where that is possible. Real rescue and sanctuary work for primates is done by underfunded centres in range countries,” Cheyne said.

Daniela Freyer, co-founder of the German NGO Pro Wildlife, added: “Our planet is increasingly threatened by the overexploitation of wildlife and by climate change driven by fossil fuels. This week, the Cites Standing Committee, which oversees international wildlife trade, decided to keep India under its compliance procedure, as questions remain about the origin of some of the world’s rarest animals imported to Vantara. A prize whose goals include protecting nature and fixing our climate should be careful not to lend its reputation to these questions.”

Vantara had sought to lobby the UK, the chair of this week’s Cites meetings, attempting to persuade officials to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara, the Guardian and Mongabay reported earlier this month. Ministers were advised not to engage formally with Vantara, internal documents revealed.

The Earthshot prize, founded by Prince William in 2020, is billed as the most prestigious environmental award. William appeared on the BBC’s Graham Norton show last month to promote the prize, saying it was designed to “talk about urgency and optimism together”.

Swift said that it was vital to host an edition of the Earthshot prize in India.

“Mumbai is the sixth largest city on earth. It is home to exceptional environmental solutions and the people behind them, with an impact felt in India and around the world. Spotlighting progress like this, to the largest possible audience, is why the Earthshot prize exists,” Swift said.

This article was amended on 9 November 2026. An earlier version said the Vantara zoo covers 400 hectares; in fact, it is 1,400 hectares.

#119. Grenades, grief and a deepening political divide: five takeaways from Sonia Gandhi’s new memoir

From the assassinations of her husband and mother-in-law to press stonewalling under Modi, Belonging offers an insider’s account of India’s political evolution

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

Few love stories have been as closely entwined with a nation’s history as that of Sonia and Rajiv Gandhi. The former’s much-awaited political memoir, Belonging, offers a strikingly candid account of her journey from a small town in Italy to the highest echelons of power in India – all for love, she says.

The veteran Congress party leader has been fiercely protective of her personal world. In Belonging, she opens up for the first time about the extraordinary twists and turns of a life that has largely unfolded in full public view. (The Guardian’s Hannah Ellis-Petersen has a rare in-person interview with Gandhi, and you can read an excerpt from the book here.)

Belonging, published today, contains moving glimpses of Gandhi’s private grief. She writes about the loss of her first child, who died shortly after birth, and the chilling discovery of grenades in a room of their house after Operation Blue Star, the 1984 military operation against Sikh separatists at the Golden Temple in Amritsar. She also provides a ringside view of several turbulent chapters in Indian politics, not least the violent assassinations of her mother-in-law, Indira Gandhi, in 1984 and her husband, Rajiv, in 1991.

Her book also revisits some of the darker episodes that have dogged the Gandhi family and the Congress party, including Indira’s imposition of the Emergency in June 1975, a 21-month period during which civil liberties were suspended, political opponents were jailed and the press was gagged, which caused fear and dismay across the country. In Gandhi’s account, it also caused a rift within the family.

Ultimately, Gandhi’s memoir is a vivid record of six decades of India’s political transformation from the Congress-dominated secular republic she moved to in 1968 until today, when Hindu nationalism has become the dominant political force under Narendra Modi.

Here are five key takeaways from her memoir and what they reveal about the state of Indian politics today.

Gandhi has had no real contact with Modi for the last 12 years

Gandhi’s account offers a glimpse of how deep the political polarisation runs in Indian politics today. In one revealing passage, Gandhi writes that she has had “practically no real contact” with Modi in the 12 years he has led the nation. In fact, at times when the two have been in the same rooms, such as the speaker’s small chamber, Modi “tends to look away, avoiding eye contact”, she says. On occasion, she adds, he has asked briefly about her health after a hospital stay.

For her, this breakdown is a departure from the past, when leaders on opposing sides of the aisle “would treat one another with cordiality”. After the 2001 terror attack on parliament, Gandhi immediately called then-BJP prime minister AB Vajpayee to enquire about his safety, a gesture he appreciated.

Gandhi sees an increasingly authoritarian style of politics, in which those in power seek to “belittle and torment” opponents. For years, Gandhi’s son, Rahul, has been the subject of BJP’s ridicule, which calls him Pappu, a derisive term intended to portray him as inexperienced and naive. She adds that similar things are happening elsewhere in the world, an observation that can be read in the context of US President Donald Trump’s use of personal insults and ridicule against opponents.

It’s lonely at the top for India’s female politicians

India has an illustrious history of women occupying top leadership positions, long before many other democracies. But their rise has often come at a cost – that of loneliness in navigating a male-dominated political world.

In the book, Gandhi describes a 2012 conversation with Hillary Clinton on the countrys’ female leaders and their backgrounds. In her recollections of Indira, she shares that, despite wielding authority, Indira could feel besieged and profoundly alone, and this was particularly acute, Gandhi writes, in the months leading up to the declaration of the Emergency by her mother-in-law.

Gandhi also writes of her own isolation, including when she decided to take on the leadership of the Congress out of a sense of duty to her family’s political legacy.

Those in power are increasingly stonewalling the press

Among the many ways in which Gandhi charts the erosion of India’s democratic conventions in Belonging, one particularly telling shift is the changed relationship between the media and those in power. She recalls a time when journalists had opportunities to question prime ministers directly.

In 12 years in power, Modi has not addressed a single press conference on his own. He has sat in on one press briefing at the conclusion of an election campaign, and appeared alongside US presidents at joint press encounters after bilateral meetings, where they took questions from the press. Modi has consistently avoided facing journalists in an open forum, and his media interactions have largely been one-on-one interviews with sympathetic, pro-government news anchors or in one much publicised exchange with a Bollywood actor who asked him how he liked to eat mangoes.

The book’s publication has brought renewed attention to concerns about the space for free expression in contemporary India, and the extent to which apprehensions about repercussions may influence what can be said, written and published.

Penguin Random House India was to publish Gandhi’s memoir but withdrew from the project in August. Gandhi told the Guardian that it was “uneasy in general with the criticism of the government” and said it had dropped the book after she declined certain suggested edits. Penguin has disputed her account, saying that the suggestion it decided not to publish the book because of refused editorial changes “does not accurately reflect the circumstances”.

The book, released in the US by Alfred A Knopf, is being published in India by HarperCollins.

In June, Penguin had also pulled out of plans to distribute graphic journalist Joe Sacco’s The Once and Future Riot, about the 2013 Muzaffarnagar communal violence. The publisher cited an inaccurate map of India and unresolved citation issues. Sacco, however, blamed the decision on the “current political climate in India”.

She’s had quiet fallouts with the ‘thoroughly dishonest’

Western political memoirs, particularly by former heads of government or senior politicians, are often forthcoming in naming political adversaries, recounting private exchanges and offering candid assessments of colleagues. David Cameron’s 2019 memoir, For the Record, took aim at his former friend, Michael Gove, describing him as “mendacious” and a “foam-flecked Faragist”.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Gandhi, in contrast, is restrained. She writes warmly of the closeness between the Gandhi family and that of Bollywood superstar Amitabh Bachchan, whose parents she lived with when she first moved to India, but she makes no mention of the subsequent estrangement between the two families. In another instance, she does not name the political leader she describes as “thoroughly dishonest” and who she says she trusted in 1999, when Congress unsuccessfully attempted to form a coalition government.

Art | Art Mumbai runs from 12-15 November at the Mahalaxmi Racecourse. Among the highlights of this year’s edition is artist Baaraan Ijlal’s collaboration with the celebrated lyricist Javed Akhtar, turning his evocative Urdu couplets into paintings. The two will be in conversation with curator Ranjit Hoskote on Friday.

Government | The official bungalow of Andhra Pradesh’s chief minister, N Chandrababu Naidu, was renovated by a government-owned enterprise for Rs 6.5 crore (£518,000), according to the Indian Express (£). The renovation work on the Delhi property included was said to have had a major makeover.

Film | Bollywood’s ambitious rendering of Hindu epic Ramayana, starring Ranbir Kapoor and Sai Pallavi, was released on 5 November. Touted as the most expensive Indian film ever made, it earned praise from the Guardian’s Peter Bradshaw, who described it as “thoroughly engaging, rousing and spectacular”.

Readers from India and around the world on the festival of lights and tell us how they celebrated.

double quotation markIn India, Diwali comes to you. You can see it in the streets, hear it in the neighbourhood and feel it in the atmosphere. In the UK, it is different. Life carries on around you. But traditions do not have to disappear when you move countries – they can change shape. A small light in a quiet room can carry the same meaning as an entire street illuminated for Diwali. double quotation markMy favorite part of Diwali back home was getting up before sunrise on Narak Chaturdashi and having a ceremonial bath, followed by a breakfast of traditional Marathi Diwali snacks. I have been living abroad for over 10 years now and it’s always a bittersweet time of the year where I miss my family and the general vibe of Diwali in India. double quotation markThere used to be more of a community spirit to Diwali. Nowadays, the celebrations are mostly private. Group celebrations are walled off within exclusive clubs, families light their own fireworks almost competitively, and people send each other sweets through a delivery service. Samyuktha, Chennai

The government has approved the sale of a 60.7% stake in IDBI bank to Canadian Fairfax Holdings for Rs 52,878 crore (£4.22bn).

It’s hard to take one’s eyes off Muhammed Riswan when he’s at work. The 23-year-old football freestyler from Areekode in Kerala’s Malappuram district conjures tricks with the ball in a delicately choreographed dance. It glides between his feet, next it is rolling on to his back right before he leaps into the air to catch it with one hand.

Riswan, who’s been freestyling for eight years, is one of the two Indians to win Instagram Rings 2026 award recognising creators. The other is Chanel model Bhavitha Mandava.

Riswan’s big break came with a video filmed at Keralamkundu waterfall, where he pulled off a freestyle move before gracefully arching the ball into the water. The clip has since clocked more than 600m views. Last year, he was invited to watch his sporting idol, Lionel Messi, play in Argentina.

#118. Chiclayo, Pope Leo’s Other Home, Comes Out In Force to Welcome Father Robert

Leo XIV returned to the city in Peru where he served as bishop for eight years, drawing large crowds who remembered him before he was pope.

It was hard to believe that anyone who lived in Chiclayo, Peru, was not lining its streets on Friday.

As the motorcade of Pope Leo XIV, who has been in South America for over a week, made its way through the city that he calls one of his homes, an almost unbroken stream of people stood by the roadside, waving, holding signs and clutching white and yellow balloons, the colors of the Vatican flag.

The last time many of them saw him, he was “Padre Robert” — Father Robert — but now they jubilantly welcomed him back as Papa Leon.

A month after being diagnosed with cancer and undergoing surgery to remove a lobe of his right lung, Leo arrived at the destination that made this 12-day tour of Uruguay, Argentina and Peru a true homecoming.

For eight years, Leo — then known as Robert Prevost — served as bishop of Chiclayo, a coastal city where he is remembered for working side by side with the Red Cross during devastating floods or driving for hours to get to parishioners in the highlands.

When he stepped out on the balcony in St. Peter’s Square after being elected pope last year, the one community he mentioned was his “dear Chiclayo.” It was while serving here that he became a Peruvian citizen.

#116. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#115. Disgraced former leader Sheikh Hasina plans return to Bangladesh from exile

Former prime minister faces death penalty upon return after being sentenced in absentia over deaths in 2024 uprising

Sheikh Hasina is “confident” she will return to Bangladesh in December, according to exiled members of her party, after the disgraced former prime minister applied for a new passport this week.

Hasina has been living in self-imposed exile since August 2024 when she was toppled from power by a mass uprising against her authoritarian rule. She crossed the border to India, where she has since been allowed to live freely in Delhi.

Last November, a tribunal in Bangladesh found Hasina guilty of crimes against humanity and sentenced her to death over her crackdown on a student uprising that left an estimated 1,400 people dead.

Her return to Bangladesh is likely to be disruptive for the country where Hasina’s party, Awami League, is still banned and thousands of its members are in jail. Should she be allowed to return, she is likely to be arrested immediately.

Hasina first raised the prospect of going back to Bangladesh in August. Speaking to the Guardian this week, several exiled Awami League leaders also living in India confirmed that Hasina was serious and “confident” about returning by next month. They confirmed that several other party figures had already returned in anticipation.

On Monday, a British lawyer acting on behalf of Hasina wrote to the Bangladesh high commission in Delhi requesting a new passport and national identity card to be issued “in a timely fashion”, in a letter seen by the Guardian.

“The reason our leader Sheikh Hasina has applied for a passport is that she genuinely wants to return,” said a party leader based in the Indian city of Kolkata, who spoke on condition of anonymity because he is not authorised to speak to the media on the party’s behalf.

Hasina has rejected the charges against her and claimed the tribunal that convicted her was illegitimate. “She is confident about her return, which is why she has applied for a new passport and a national identity card. If the authorities do not issue her a passport, she will apply for a travel pass to make her return to Bangladesh possible,” said the Awami League leader.

Since Hasina’s conviction, the Bangladesh government has put pressure on the Indian authorities to extradite the former prime minister. However, the Indian government, which was close to Hasina while she was in power, has ignored the demands, straining ties between the two countries.

This week, the Bangladesh government said that, as a “terrorist”, Hasina would only be allowed to return via an “acceptable prisoner exchange” under the India-Bangladesh extradition treaty.

Aminul Islam, the chief prosecutor of the tribunal that sentenced Hasina, said there was “no legal scope” for her to be issued a new passport as she was a “convicted fugitive”.

Islam emphasised that Hasina would be hanged if she returned, as the deadline for her to appeal against her conviction had passed. “Sheikh Hasina has no option but to face the execution of the verdict,” he said.

Despite the abuses and oppression that mired her 15 years in power and subsequent conviction for crimes against humanity, Hasina still commands significant popularity among the thousands of Awami League cadre still in Bangladesh, many of whom have been driven underground.

Analysts said they believed her decision to return was driven by an attempt to revive her support base and destabilise the newly elected government, led by Tarique Rahman. It came to power in February during Bangladesh’s first democratic election in decades.

The government has since struggled with inflation and protests that shut down garment factories, which the Awami League hopes to use to its advantage.

Another Awami League leader said all those in exile intended to follow Hasina back to Bangladesh, despite many of them facing serious criminal charges and having been given lengthy prison sentences in absentia.

“Some of us have been sentenced in absentia by kangaroo courts, and we know we will be jailed. But we are ready to face the consequences and return to our own people. We will fight this politically,” said the senior leader, who is based in India.

Some senior party figures have returned in recent months, including the former MP Mustaque Ahmed Ruhi. He was immediately arrested on his return in September and faces prosecution over an alleged murder during the 2024 uprising.

Exiled Awami League leaders have continued to meet in Kolkata, where many of them are living, and are routinely summoned to Delhi to meet Hasina.

Speaking over email this week, Hasina told the Associated Press: “If death is my destiny, I would rather die on the soil of my own country, among my own people.”

#114. In Canada, Watching the N.H.L. Has Become Expensive and Cumbersome

Games behind paywalls and exorbitant costs forcing a drop in attendance — even to watch Connor McDavid in Edmonton — have only compounded the loss of a Saturday night ritual.

The National Hockey League season is less than two months old, and much of the focus in Canada has been on how expensive it is to attend games, and how costly and cumbersome it has become to watch them on television.

Attendance has fallen in several markets, including Edmonton, where Connor McDavid has an astonishing 49 points in 20 games and is on pace to challenge Wayne Gretzky’s single-season scoring record of 215 points.

This week, The Globe and Mail reported that three TV providers had filed a complaint with the Canadian Radio-television and Telecommunications Commission asking Rogers Sportsnet to reverse its decision to put more than 50 Monday night N.H.L. games behind a paywall on its streaming service for an extra $13 a month.

Further increasing the cost and complexity of watching hockey, Wednesday night games are on Prime Video. TSN broadcasts some regional games with blackouts, and a subscription to NHL Centre Ice is needed to watch out-of-market games.

Until this season, the N.H.L. could be found for free.

In June, the CBC and Rogers Sportsnet announced that the national broadcaster would no longer air N.H.L. games on Saturday nights, a first in 75 years, ending the cultural institution “Hockey Night in Canada.” Last month, the CBC announced it would begin airing Professional Women’s Hockey League games when its season begins in December.

#113. Lewis Hamilton says ‘Israel’s genocide in Gaza is appalling’ after Rafah visit

F1 driver urges Israel to let aid into Gaza

Trip involved tour of facilities with aid workers

Formula One driver Lewis Hamilton said on Wednesday that Israel must allow aid ⁠trucks into Gaza, a day after he visited the Rafah border crossing in Egypt.

Hamilton, a seven-time ⁠world champion, arrived ⁠in ​El Arish on Tuesday, where he was briefed on how aid is prepared and coordinated before it ⁠is sent into Gaza, as he toured the Rafah crossing and the Egyptian Red Crescent’s aid warehouses.

He has frequently posted on social media about children killed in Israeli airstrikes in Gaza and called for a ceasefire. A ceasefire agreement reached last year has halted major combat operations in Gaza, but the amount of aid reaching the territory continues to fall far short of the agreed-upon minimum of 600 trucks a day.

“The past ⁠three years of Israel’s genocide in Gaza is appalling. It’s an ​injustice for people to ‌be denied their freedom, ‌their safety and their dignity. No human being should be treated ‌as though their life matters less,” the Ferrari driver wrote on Instagram.

#112. Hungary: Millions in hidden cash found in Orban graft probe

Investigators discovered bundles of banknotes buried in forests and concealed in buildings. The money was seized during an investigation into a former minister accused of accepting millions in bribes.

Hungarian authorities have recovered nearly $20 million (€17.7 million) in hidden cash during a corruption investigation into a former minister under ex-Prime Minister Viktor Orban.

The country's newly established National Asset Recovery and Protection Office said Friday that investigators discovered the money during searches linked to former National Development Minister Miklos Sesztak.

The discovery is part of a sweeping anti-corruption campaign launched by Prime Minister Peter Magyar, who defeated Orban in April's parliamentary election.

The money was discovered during two days of searches in the capital, Budapest, and three Hungarian counties.

Investigators found large quantities of cash buried in forests, concealed in agricultural buildings, warehouses and residential properties, as well as stored in an expensive safe at a secluded location.

The recovered money included 5.07 billion Hungarian forints (€13.3 million, $15.3 million), €2.66 million (€2.9 million) and $1.26 million (€1.08 million).

According to Finance Minister Andras Karman, 12 employees of the Hungarian State Treasury spent eight hours counting the banknotes using five counting machines.

The agency also released a video showing investigators kneeling beside large piles of bundled cash and digging into the ground in what appeared to be a forest at night.

Authorities said the seized money had been deposited into an account belonging to the Hungarian state treasury.

Sesztak served as Orban's national development minister between 2014 and 2018.

He is suspected of accepting more than $37 million (€33 million) in bribes and kickbacks connected to the purchase of overpriced used buses by state-owned transport companies and state construction projects.

The former minister was taken into police custody in late September. He has denied wrongdoing.

His lawyer, Gabor Papp, so far did not respond to a request for comment on the latest discovery.

Magyar described the recovered cash as a turning point in his government's efforts to tackle alleged corruption under Orban.

The discovery "officially marks the end of the Fidesz-KDNP mafia," he wrote on Facebook, referring to Orban's former governing coalition.

"As of today, Viktor Orbán is a proven mafia boss. A fallen mafia boss. And Fidesz is a criminal organization," Magyar added.

Magyar's center-right, pro-European Tisza party won a landslide victory in April, ending Orban's 16-year rule.

The new prime minister has pledged to repair Hungary's relations with the European Union, which had been strained by years of disputes under Orban.

His government established the anti-corruption agency with investigative and prosecutorial powers to pursue suspected corruption and misuse of public funds.

Magyar has dubbed the campaign "Operation Purgatory."

Several other senior figures from Orban's time in office have been detained as part of separate investigations.

Among them is former central bank governor and economy minister Gyorgy Matolcsy, once considered one of Orban's closest allies.

Matolcsy is being investigated over the alleged misuse of a central bank foundation that received 288 billion forints ($818 million, €787 million) in public assets. He and other former officials under investigation have denied wrongdoing.

Police also detained Orban's younger brother earlier this week as part of an investigation into suspected human trafficking and influence peddling.

Orban has condemned the investigations as political "tyranny" aimed at removing him from Hungary's political landscape. He has described his brother's detention as an act of political revenge.

According to a survey by pollster Median published by news website HVG this week, 70% of respondents considered the anti-corruption campaign "very important."

A separate Median survey showed support for Orban's Fidesz party had fallen to 16% of voters by November, roughly half its level before the April election.

#110. Here’s the latest.

The event is rolling into Bangkok, with performers in feathered capes and sequined corsets. Will it appeal to fans in a vast new market?

The Eurovision Song Contest will crown its first Asia champion in Bangkok on Saturday, in an event that will test whether a high-camp singing competition with an ardent fan base can appeal to audiences in a new market.

The inaugural Eurovision Song Contest Asia comprises artists and original songs from 11 countries: Bangladesh, Bhutan, Cambodia, Laos, Malaysia, Mongolia, Nepal, the Philippines, South Korea, Thailand and Vietnam. It will be live-streamed on YouTube at 8:30 p.m. local time (8:30 a.m. Eastern) and broadcast by television stations across the region.

Eurovision has been a fixture of the European cultural calendar since it began in 1956. Past winners include Sweden’s ABBA and the Canadian chanteuse Celine Dion, who represented Switzerland in 1988.

Asian artists have readily embraced the kitschy outfits and spectacular sets that are part of Eurovision tradition, with costumes that include traditional dress, as well as feathered capes and sequined corsets. Malaysia’s performer plans to enter the stage in a giant teardrop.

But the Eurovision format has not been tested in Asia. Fans and regional partners have expressed concern in recent weeks that budget constraints, production issues and weak audience demand could imperil the spinoff. Organizers say the contest could take time to build an audience.

“It would be profoundly arrogant to think that just because we have a heritage brand and a huge global audience for the European show, that we would walk in and demand it to be the same here,” said Martin Green, the European Broadcasting Union’s director of the Eurovision Song Contest. “Like any new thing, it needs to find its roots.”

Two previous attempts to take the competition to Asia were abandoned by the European Broadcasting Union, which owns Eurovision, and its partners in the region. A Eurovision spinoff in the United States, NBC’s American Song Contest, ran for one season in 2022.

The Asian edition kicking off on Saturday is far smaller than its European counterpart. Eurovision organizers said they were in talks with more countries to join future seasons. They include China, Japan and India, whose music industries have global reach.

“Bringing the world together through music is a fundamentally good idea,” Mr. Green said. “Europe shouldn’t keep it to itself.”

With about a half hour until showtime, some 7,000 people are waiting to watch the YouTube stream (I am one of them!). The contest has a long way to go to match up to the European edition of the contest. The YouTube stream for that had 11 million views in May (even though most people in Europe watch on traditional TV broadcasts).

When the Eurovision Song Contest is staged in Europe, it’s always one of the year’s biggest television events.

This year’s was in Vienna in a 16,000-capacity arena. It involved 35 participants and a glittering four-hour finale broadcast live to tens of millions of viewers across Europe. It cost the host broadcaster some 16 million euros, or $17.7 million.

That makes the debut Eurovision Song Contest Asia look like a tiny event. It will take place in a 5,000-capacity venue in Bangkok with just 11 competitors.

Inside the theater, fans are jostling for spots in front of the stage and near the so-called green room, where the artists will sit after their performances until the results are announced.

It’s less than an hour before showtime at the One Bangkok Forum, and crowds of fans have gathered outside the theater, taking photos, buying memorabilia and sneaking in a quick bite of dinner. Organizers have handed out cardboard signs with the performers’ images — I’ve been told that they’ve run out of South Korea and Thailand.

Francesca Regalado reported from Bangkok, where she will cover the Eurovision Song Contest Asia on Saturday.

The Eurovision Song Contest, Europe’s long-running singing competition, is expanding into Asia for the first time on Saturday, with singers from 11 countries vying for the title. The New York Times is covering it live.

This is the third attempt by the European Broadcasting Union to develop an Asian spinoff of the high-camp competition. With Bangkok as host city, it will test demand in Asia for an international competition based on original songs.

The Eurovision Song Contest Asia could either be Eurovision’s first successful foray into another region — or a one-off flop like NBC’s American Song Contest.

The Asian edition will have a far smaller pool of competitors than its European counterpart, which typically includes over 30 countries. But it will include South Korea and Thailand, whose pop songs have found international audiences.

The contest will be live-streamed on YouTube on Saturday night local time (Saturday morning Eastern) and broadcast by government-owned and commercial television stations in the 11 participating countries.

As in Europe, the winner will be determined by votes from a panel of judges and viewers from around the world.

Eurovision, which began in 1956 as an experiment in synchronized programming by public broadcasters across Europe, has grown into a weeklong spectacle with an international fan base. The competition’s 70th edition in May was won by Dara, a singer who represented Bulgaria, with the dance-pop hit “Bangaranga.”

Organizers have unsuccessfully tried to replicate the contest outside Europe. The American Song Contest, which pitted singers from 56 states and territories, ran for only one season in 2022. An Australian public broadcaster, SBS, called off its yearslong attempt to bring Eurovision to Asia in 2021.

This time, the Asian project got off the ground partly because pop music from countries like South Korea, Japan and Thailand has global appeal, said Assaf Blecher, the executive producer of the Eurovision Song Contest Asia.

“It’s the right time for something to happen, with the evolution of music from Asia and the curiosity of Eurovision fans to see what’s happening beyond Europe,” he said.

The 11 countries, in order of performance, are Vietnam, Cambodia, Laos, Mongolia, Bhutan, the Philippines, Bangladesh, Thailand, South Korea, Nepal and Malaysia.

Asia’s three largest media markets are conspicuously missing. Mr. Blecher said that broadcasters from China, India and Japan had been in talks to join the competition, but did not sign on in time to meet the deadline for entries.

To stage the competition in Asia, Eurovision had to overcome a fraught relationship with the continent’s broadcasters. In 2018, the European Broadcasting Union canceled a contract with China’s Mango TV, a provincial-government-owned streaming platform, after it censored elements of that year’s Eurovision show, including a gay love story depicted in an Irish singer’s performance.

Last year, China, India and Vietnam sent singers to Intervision, a Cold War-era song contest that Russia revived after it was expelled from Eurovision in 2022 over its invasion of Ukraine. A Vietnamese singer, Duc Phuc, won the contest with a song inspired by a mythical folk hero.

Each country will send a singer who won a national contest with an original song. For emerging singer-songwriters like Nepal’s Pratik Himal Rai and Bhutan’s Nyendra, winning their national contests was life-changing.

Others, like Malaysia’s Aina Abdul and Vietnam’s Toc Tien, are established pop stars with decades-long careers. Bangladesh’s entry, Beatmosphere, is a six-member a cappella group that formed in 2009.

The 11 acts will perform a wide range of genres: love ballads, a hip-hop track, a rock song and dance anthems. Some artists, like Thinla of Laos and Junhee of South Korea, will sing in English.

Each act will have three minutes to perform onstage at the One Bangkok Forum, a newly opened auditorium in central Bangkok that can seat around 5,000 people.

Once a performance is complete, a jury in each country will rank the songs. Those rankings will be converted into point scores for each singer, from zero to 12.

Public voting — in each country and among YouTube viewers watching internationally — will also be used to allocate points. The public and jury scores will then be combined to work out the winner.

Viewers from participating countries cannot vote for their own nation’s act.

A QR code will be shown onscreen to direct viewers to the Eurovision Asia voting website. Each user will have eight votes for free to distribute to at least three songs. They can purchase up to 10 extra votes.

The organizers said that they expect most viewers to watch the show from their phones and personal devices, not on television. Last year, the European contest's final competition received more than five million views on YouTube.

The winner of the Asian contest will be invited to perform at the European edition next year, though not as a competitor. And unlike in Europe, the winning country will not automatically be chosen to host next year.

Alex Marshall contributed reporting from London.

#109. Two men found guilty of helping Iran try to ‘eliminate’ UK-based journalists

Mostafa Sepahvand and Farhad Javadi Manesh carried out surveillance in an effort to inflict violence on targets

Two men of Iranian origin who were hired through social media as part of a plan to “eliminate” journalists in the UK have been found guilty of assisting Tehran’s intelligence services.

Mostafa Sepahvand, 41, and Farhad Javadi Manesh, 46, carried out surveillance and reconnaissance between August 2024 and March last year in an attempt to inflict violence on “subjects” in the UK.

These subjects included two journalists for the London-based Persian broadcaster Iran International, Mojtaba Pourmohsen and Kambiz Hosseini, and a founder member of UK Lawyers for Israel, Jonathan Turner.

The defendants visited addresses linked to their targets, took photos and videos and used platforms such as ChatGPT to research them online. They appear to be part of a growing new tactic used by hostile states in which “proxies” are hired to work for foreign security forces, with often little idea who they are targeting or why.

Last month the Norwegian teenager Johannes Natland, 19, was jailed for life after he agreed to carry out a hit in Huddersfield for the Foxtrot Network, a Swedish organised crime group backed by the Iranian regime.

The Iranian men came to the UK illegally between 2016 and 2022 – Sepahvand in the back of a lorry and Manesh in a small boat.

Sepahvand used the AI platform ChatGPT to find the home address of Pourmohsen, who worked for the London-based Persian language broadcaster Iran International. He hoped to be paid £150,000 to carry out a violent attack and help to “finish off” Pourmohsen.

Sepahvand chatted on WhatsApp to his handler, an Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps commander called Amin, as he spied on Pourmohsen in December 2024, Woolwich crown court heard.

Amin wrote: “These are new pictures of the guy/bloke.”

Referring to a picture, Amin said: “And that dot is their office?”

Sepahvand said: “Love you brother, I’ll go there tomorrow.”

The pair also discussed surveillance of the Iranian satirist Kambiz Hosseini, who eventually had to leave home because of the threats made against him.

Bill Emlyn Jones KC, prosecuting, said Iran and its rulers did not and could not tolerate dissent. “One organisation critical to the regime, and therefore very much in its sights, is Iran International, a news organisation which has come under particular attack because of its explicit support for opposition groups and its editorial stance, which is anti-Islamic Republic of Iran.

“Eliminating hostile, critical or dissident actors overseas is a staple policy of the Islamic republic. Together, the defendants were engaged in what is very obviously a form of spying,” Jones said.

Hosseini, 51, had to leave home and stop work as a standup comedian as a result of the threats he received. He was sent a message with a picture of his car saying the next time he started the engine it would blow up. Hosseini’s family in Iran also received threats, and his family had to leave the country.

The court heard Sepahvand had been convicted of theft in Iran and was drawn into the plan to “eliminate” dissidents in the hope of reducing his criminal record.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He told his Iranian handlers he was prepared to escalate from surveillance to violence, the court heard.

He hoped to receive a final payment of £150,000 for “finishing the job”, a reference to killing one of the journalists, the court was told. “If the person [the target] is inside, they pay the £150,000, and I’ll finish him off,” said Sepahvand in a voice note.

Sepahvand claimed his family was being threatened and that he never intended to go through with the hit. He also denied plotting to kill Turner.

Manesh claimed he took a picture of the Iran International site because it was “a beautiful building” and did not know they were working with the Iranian intelligence services.

Earlier this year, Roman Lavrynovych, 22, and Stanislav Carpiuc, 27, were jailed at the Old Bailey for seven and two years respectively for arson attacks on property connected to Keir Starmer. An anonymous Russian-speaking figure named El Money had recruited Lavrynovych and Carpiuc on Telegram.

Last year, six Britons were jailed for their parts in a Russian-ordered arson attack on a London warehouse providing aid to Ukraine. The ringleader, Dylan Earl, was recruited by the Wagner group, a mercenary organisation that acts on behalf of the Russian state.

Sepahvand was convicted by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct. Manesh was convicted of assisting a foreign intelligence service, but cleared of preparatory conduct.

Shapoor Noori, 57, of Ealing, was cleared by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct.

Sepahvand, Manesh and Noori had claimed asylum in the UK and received temporary leave to remain. None of them had regular full-time employment.

It is understood they met by chance and were then directed by Sepahvand and his contacts in Iran. There appeared to be no evidence of any wider plot.

Mrs Justice Cheema-Grubb remanded Sepahvand and Manesh before sentencing at the Old Bailey on 17 December.
