#108. Eurovision is finally coming to Asia - meet the stars gearing up to take the stage

Mongolia's Beck debuted as a singer in 2007 and has since made his mark in acting, hosting, modelling and producing

On Saturday, the biggest and longest-running international song contest will make a leap into unknown territory.

Eurovision, the pan-European competition famed for zany gimmicks, slick, sometimes over-the-top staging, the drama of its complex voting system and, occasionally, for outstanding music, celebrates its 70th birthday this year.

And to mark it, the European Broadcasting Union is trying out audiences in Asia.

It's an idea that's been talked about for two decades but never materialised, despite many false starts.

Until now - Eurovision Asia will hold its first grand final in Bangkok, starting at 20:30 ICT (13:30 GMT).

Transferring one of the most successful television formulas that has worked in Europe for 70 years to Asia has not been straightforward, says Sarun 'Note' Kungbunpot, a Eurovision enthusiast and Thai singer who has won several competitions in this region.

"It's still very new in Asia. I'm not sure many people have heard about it yet," he says, adding that he thinks it will take a bit of time for audiences to adjust.

"When we look at contests in Thailand, the Philippines and Korea, we actually compete based on the singer.

"It's all about popularity â fans are looking for their idols. But Eurovision is all about the song, not the singer. I think this needs to be communicated clearly."

MANG x DAOU have winning form, having previously been declared champions of the show Thailand's Chosen One

Then there is the long history of Eurovision in Europe. It began in 1956 as a project to encourage greater cultural exchanges in a Europe which was trying to forge a common future after two world wars.

This was the very start of the age of mass television ownership, and Eurovision became such a fixture it is now an indelible piece of Europe's shared cultural heritage. It has no such resonance in Asia, and arrives in this region in the digital age, when traditional TV formats are struggling to cut through.

Only 11 countries are taking part in this first Eurovision Asia contest â host Thailand, along with Malaysia, the Philippines, Vietnam, Cambodia, Laos, South Korea, Mongolia, Bangladesh, Nepal and Bhutan.

Asian giants India, China, Japan and Indonesia are noticeably absent, although there are negotiations ongoing to try to get them into next year's contest, assuming there is one.

There have also been problems with the organisation of this event.

A sudden million-dollar (Â£760,000) budget cut just three weeks before the final meant many of the technicians familiar with staging Eurovision were not brought to Thailand, and there have been a number of staff resignations too.

Eurovision fans - and there are many passionate followers around the world - have noticed, criticising audio and lighting in rehearsal videos.

Watch: Eurovision is coming to Asia - but will it succeed?

The contest's unusual voting system will be unfamiliar to Asian audiences. Like in Europe, Eurovision Asia will determine the winner through a 50/50 mix of public voting and national juries from each of the 11 contesting countries, as well as a 12th "rest of the world" category.

Anyone can vote - you get eight votes once you register - but you cannot vote for your own country's contestant and you must give your votes to at least three others.

The public and jury votes are then converted into points. The most popular song among the Bhutanese or Mongolians, for example, will get 12 points from those countries, while the song ranked last will get zero - the famous "nul points".

JUNHEE is a solo singer-songwriter from South Korea who produces bright, mainstream-sounding music

In Europe, there have long been reports of co-ordinated voting to back neighbours, for example the four Nordic countries voting for each other. This seems less likely in Eurovision Asia, but political differences, for example between Thailand and Cambodia after their border war last year, could influence how their audiences vote.

Filipinos, with their passion for singing, are expected to be enthusiastic participants and the 11 million who live overseas might make a difference. But as most live and work in countries which are not participating, their votes will only go into the "rest of the world" pool.

For Bhutan, with a population of less than a million, this is a rare chance to shine on a global stage, which puts some pressure on 24-year-old Thukten Nyendra, the singer representing the Himalayan kingdom in Bangkok with his song Lhayi Semo (The Angel). Before coming here he had never left his country or been on a plane before.

"I'm not a star. I'm just Nyendra over there," he told the BBC. "Nobody knows me. But Eurovision has given me a huge platform. Actually winning would mean the world to us... If I really make it through all of this, it would mean the entire freaking world to us."

Nyendra, Bhutan's 24-year-old contestant, is also a journalism graduate

The other characteristic of Eurovision is the song format â no more than three minutes, no more than six people on stage, and no backing band, only taped music.

This encourages performers to prepare elaborate, sometimes outrageous choreography and stunts to impress the audience â think Poland's My SÅowianie in 2014 with models in traditional costume churning butter on stage, or the Finnish heavy metal band Lordi donning flamboyant monster outfits in its 2006 entry.

There has also been a long association of Eurovision with LGBTQ+ artists. Eurovision Asia includes Malaysia and Bangladesh â where same-sex activity is illegal â and there are no visible LGBTQ+ elements this year.

Performers have been using the last days of rehearsals before the final to try to make their stage acts as striking as possible. Thinla from Laos has abandoned the short, sequinned dress she wore in the first video of her infectious reggaeton song Bae and is adopting Lao hill-tribe costume for her and her backing dancers.

Thinla's music blends traditional Lao and contemporary genres, such as pop, R&B and dance

Perhaps the most ambitious choreography is in Vietnamese singer TÃ³c TiÃªn's song Em TiÃªn ("Fairy Maiden").

It has been exhausting just watching her rehearse the elaborate moves and costume changes for more than an hour at a time.

The entry from Bangladesh's Beatmosphere, Tumi Ele ("You Came"), is unusual as an a cappella and beatboxing performance by six men with little backing music.

The youngest contestant is 19 year-old Carmelle Collado from the Philippines, whose bubbling confidence and strong stage presence in her song Sobra-Sobra ("Too Much") belie her youth.

"I'm actually quite scared because I'm the youngest, but also really happy," she told us.

"The song is about feminine energy. It's about being brave, strong, fierce, and it's about how strong Filipino women are."

Bangladesh's Beatmosphere blend traditional vocal harmony with beatbox culture

Carmelle Collado rose to prominence through a selection of televised singing competitions in the Philippines

Eurovision is famous for putting unknown acts onto a global stage, and introducing musicians who went on to become megastars â think Abba, little known when they won in 1974 with Waterloo, and Celine Dion winning in Ireland in 1988.

Because of the lack of public awareness of Eurovision Asia many of the finalists chosen for this first contest are already well known in their home markets.

Aina Abdul is perhaps the most famous singer in Malaysia, known as much for dramatic costumes as her impressive vocal range. Her song Bluest Sky â which she told us was inspired by her late cat Hiro â is a strong contender to win the contest.

Malaysia's Aina Abdul laughs as designer Melinda Looi adjusts her dress before a media shoot

Abdul's spectacular dress, by Malaysian designer Melinda Looi, who has travelled to Bangkok with her, had taken 600 hours to make, she told me. She said its glistening texture, made from materials brought from Italy and China, is meant to represent moving water.

If all goes well on the night, the organisers are hoping this could be the start of an expansion of the hugely successful Eurovision brand into a region which, after all, has half the world's population.

Eurovision Song Contest launches first-ever Asia edition

#107. Wolf Biermann at 90: The singer who defied East Germany

His life is a reflection of contemporary German history, which Biermann has addressed via his songs and sharp wit. Turning 90, he’s as feisty as ever.

He mocks Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin as "political lovers," denounces the "endless fratricidal war" over the Palestinian territories, and repeatedly invokes "Lady Liberty." It is a celebration of the freedom he once so painfully lacked in the former East Germany, and of the freedom people around the world still yearn for.

These themes run throughout Wolf Biermann's new album "Unterm Haar nur Himmel" ("Beneath Hair, Only Sky") released to mark his 90th birthday. The album's title is a characteristically poetic phrase coined by Biermann himself. It evokes the idea that however restrictive the world may become, the human mind and spirit remain free. Beneath the hair lies open sky: a metaphor for inner freedom, independence and imagination.

Biermann remains one of Germany's most influential singer-songwriters, and at 90, he shows little sign of putting down his guitar. Often described as Germany's answer to Bob Dylan, the outspoken troubadour continues to weigh in on the issues shaping the world today.

In his recent song "Moritat von den Liebespaaren" ("Ballad of the Lovers"), he delivers a simple but urgent message: no single person can save the world, yet everyone can help make it a better place. Above all, the song is a call to take responsibility for one's own life rather than remain a bystander.

This outspokenness was instilled in Biermann early on. When he was born in Hamburg in 1936, Adolf Hitler was already in power. His father was a dockworker, a committed communist, a resistance fighter against the Nazis — and Jewish. Wolf was just three months old when the Gestapo, Hitler's secret police, arrested his father. He initially served time in prison before he was murdered by the Nazis at Auschwitz in early 1943.

Just a few months later, British and American bombers attacked Hamburg, unleashing a devastating firestorm. "Nothing in my life has shaped me more," Biermann has repeatedly said in interviews. When the air raid sirens sounded, his mother took him into an air raid shelter. The house above them was burning.

Yet Biermann says his memories of that time are rooted in a profound sense of trust in life. In an interview with the German magazine Chrismon, he recalled: "I pressed my face into my mother's soft coat so I could breathe. A sense of security in the midst of the end of the world. We stumbled out over the neighboring house and into the open. The firestorm lifted a burning roof into the air. Roaring corrugated metal, smoke everywhere. We searched for a way out. (...) Death was everywhere. 40,000 people died. They had no chance. But I did. My strong mama."

His mother instilled communist and anti-fascist ideas in him with almost religious fervor. "My mother had only one goal after those dark years," Biermann recalls. "I was supposed to survive so that I could, as she put it rather grandly, one day take revenge for my father's death. I was supposed to build communism."

In 1953, while countless people were fleeing from East Germany, Biermann moved there of his own free will — after all, communism was the official state ideology. He was just 16 at the time. In East Berlin, he studied political economy, mathematics and philosophy. His role models were writers Bertolt Brecht and Heinrich Heine. He began working as an assistant director at the Berliner Ensemble. He also began writing and performing his political songs.

He soon realized that life in East Germany was far from the communist paradise he had imagined and he wrote about this openly in his songs. "The party bosses were torn," Biermann recalls. "They were delighted by this young poet who could play the guitar so beautifully and sing such cheeky songs. They thought they just needed to bring me into line. Even today, I'm amazed that I wound up cast in the role of the heroic dragon slayer."

In 1965, East Germany's ruling Socialist Unity Party, the SED, imposed a blanket ban on Biermann performing or publishing his work. His East Berlin apartment, which was under constant surveillance, soon became a gathering place for dissidents and critical thinkers.

Yet, to his great surprise, the East German authorities allowed him to travel to Cologne, West Germany in 1976. There, around 7,000 people flocked to see him. For four and a half hours, he strummed and sang, mocking the East German regime. His songs were so popular that bootleg copies found their way back to East Germany.

On November 16, while still on tour in West Germany, Biermann heard the news on his car radio that he had been stripped of his right to continue residing in the German Democratic Republic. The regime accused him of betraying and slandering East Germany. "I felt like the ground had opened up beneath me. I was overcome with fear, everything went dark," Biermann wrote in his memoirs.

In a petition, leading East German intellectuals spoke out against the decision. In response, the regime stepped up surveillance of dissidents, imposed professional bans and jailed its critics. Several prominent public figures left the country.

"The SED leadership expected an angry media response from the West. What they hadn't reckoned with was that, for the very first time, a group of respected East German writers and artists would make their protest public, disguising it as a humble petition," Biermann writes.

Biermann sank into a deep depression. "Not just because I had been torn away from the friends I knew and loved in East Berlin, but also because I had lost my familiar mortal enemies," he says. He became involved in the peace and anti-nuclear movements and continued singing about the injustices of the world. "The world's conflicts take different forms in each generation," he says. "But it's always the same struggle for freedom."

The singer-songwriter plans to spend his 90th birthday at home in Hamburg with his wife and manager, Pamela. For the first time in his life, he says, he's skipping the birthday party, even with his 10 children. "I'm so old and I can feel my strength fading. And I know that the next day I have a major concert at the Berliner Ensemble," he told the German Press Agency (dpa).

And should he ever die, he says, he wants to be buried at Berlin's cemetery where some renowned German writers and intellectuals have been laid to rest, among them Bertolt Brecht, Anna Seghers and Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel. Quite a crowd, Biermann says. He still has a thing or two to discuss with them.

This article was originally written in German.

#106. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#105. Boxer on death row in Iran vows ‘not to bow before oppression’ after winning rights prize

Political prisoner Mohammad Javad Vafaei Sani, one of seven athletes at risk of execution in Iran, given prestigious Magnitsky humanitarian award

A boxer and political prisoner on death row in Iran said he will “never surrender” as he accepted a prestigious human rights prize on behalf of “the generation that is paying the price of freedom with their lives”.

Mohammad Javad Vafaei Sani, an Iranian boxing champion, coach and political prisoner, has been sentenced to death three times and is one of seven athletes at risk of execution in Iran. He was given the Magnitsky award for outstanding human rights activist at a ceremony in London on Thursday night.

In a written message from Vakilabad prison in north-eastern Iran, he said: “I accept this award in the name of all those who, for the ‘crime’ of aspiring to a free Iran, have been imprisoned, tortured, threatened, or sentenced to death; in the name of my comrades in the resistance units and the generation that is paying the price of freedom with their lives.”

He added: “The regime demands repentance, televised confessions and surrender from us. But our answer is clear: ‘We will never surrender; we will never submit to humiliation.’”

Vafaei Sani, 31, was arrested in 2020 for taking part in nationwide democracy protests in 2019 and accused of supporting an opposition group, the People’s Mojahedin Organization of Iran (MEK).

He was convicted of “corruption on Earth” and sentenced to death for a third time in September 2024 after “a grossly unfair trial”, according to Amnesty International. The supreme court upheld his death sentence on 4 October 2025. Since his arrest he has been tortured and held in solitary confinement for extended periods.

His continued imprisonment has drawn widespread international condemnation from prominent sportspeople, human rights organisations and legal experts who have called for his immediate release and the annulment of his death sentence.

The Magnitsky awards are named after the Russian lawyer, Sergei Magnitsky, who died in Russian police custody after exposing government corruption. They began in 2015 as a way to recognise journalists, politicians and activists in the field of human rights.

Sir William Browder, head of the Magnitsky Justice Foundation and the chair of the awards committee, said Vafaei Sani’s case was emblematic of the plight of many Iranians who face execution for standing against the regime. “Most important, Mohammad Javad is still alive,” he said. “That is why this award matters. Every time the world pays attention, it becomes harder to kill him quietly.”

According to the Hengaw Organisation for Human Rights, at least 710 people have been executed in Iran since the beginning of 2026, including 79 political and religious prisoners.

In an October report, the organisation documented at least 106 death sentences against January protest detainees, 35 of whom had already been executed. In Vakilabad prison, where Vafaei Sani is detained, five people have been executed linked to the protests and 15 others are facing death sentences.

Vafaei Sani’s award comes at a time when there are growing fears for imprisoned Iranian athletes, who are considered role models in the country. Zohreh Abdollahkhani, founder of Human Rights for Sport, said: “The situation is really dire … We have seven athletes at risk of execution now, including Vafaei Sani.”

Ali-Sina Mostafavi, a mountaineer, was executed on 14 October after the anti-government protests. His death followed the killing of Siavash Jamshidi Kheirabadi, a taekwondo athlete, earlier that month, and the mixed martial arts fighter Gholamreza Khani Shakarab, who was hanged in May.

Abdollahkhani said people had been celebrating Vafaei Sani’s award. “Even people who don’t support his political view are supporting him to be heard,” she said. “I find it very inspiring that different political groups are coming together to say no to execution.”

In his message, Vafaei Sani said he wanted “a free and democratic Iran … where the people vote freely, women and men are equal, there is no discrimination on the basis of ethnicity, religion or belief, and no one is imprisoned or executed for political opposition”.

He added: “I remain steadfast in my pledge, and I will not bow before oppression until the people of Iran are free.”

#104. She Is Rewriting Mexico’s Ballads, One Woman’s Story at a Time

Grammy-nominated songwriter Vivir Quintana is known throughout Latin America for giving voice to women who fought back against domestic violence.

Grammy-nominated songwriter Vivir Quintana is known throughout Latin America for giving voice to women who fought back against domestic violence.

Against the backdrop of a centuries-old stone church in the Mexican city of Oaxaca, the singer Vivir Quintana raised her deep, husky voice above the blare of trumpets, trombones and the booming pulse of a tuba.

Behind her stood a brass band of young Indigenous girls in embroidered white blouses and brightly colored skirts. Hat in hand and fist raised, Ms. Quintana sang a corrido, a traditional Mexican folk ballad usually used to recount tales of revolutionaries, outlaws, folk heroes, and more recently, drug traffickers.

Corridos are typically sung by men, about men. Ms. Quintana sang a corrido she wrote based on the true story of a woman who fought back when her abusive partner tried to gouge out her eyes with a knife.

“I used to think that what united us women was our pain. Now I have realized it is love and resistance,” Ms. Quintana told the crowd in October during a women’s conference in Oaxaca called Decididas, drawing a roar of applause. “We must keep defending ourselves!”

On Thursday, Ms. Quintana will take the stage at the Latin Grammy Awards in Las Vegas alongside the Colombian pop superstar Juanes, with whom she is nominated for song of the year for “Humano.” It is her second Latin Grammy nomination.

#103. How the Jensen Huang scandal created a headache for business-friendly Singapore

Singapore has painstakingly cultivated a reputation as an open, business-friendly economy

A major scandal involving Nvidia founder Jensen Huang and a suspected Chinese spy based in Singapore has cast a spotlight on the city-state's success as a global chip hub - and a location for alleged illicit trade.

On Thursday, Huang stepped down from the US chip giant after admitting he had an affair. He was reportedly involved with a woman named Alice Huang, who ran Singaporean data centre company Megaspeed, a major buyer of Nvidia's chips.

For decades, Singapore has painstakingly cultivated a reputation as an open, business-friendly economy offering easy access to both the US and Chinese markets.

With the AI boom, it has become a major hub for the trade of high-end semiconductor chips in recent years.

Singapore was the second-largest billing centre globally last year for Nvidia, accounting for nearly a fifth of what the US chip giant earns.

But notably, less than 1% of its earnings were for chips that actually entered Singapore, as the island acts as a hub for many Asian companies to place orders and send them elsewhere.

Since 2022, when the US restricted advanced American semiconductors from being exported to China or acquired by Chinese companies, US officials have been concerned that other countries such as Singapore could be used by Chinese companies to gain access to the chips.

It became the biggest buyer of Nvidia's chips in South East Asia and is suspected of giving backdoor access to these chips to Chinese companies via its data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Singapore has said that while it does not enforce "unilateral export controls imposed by other countries" it expects all companies in its jurisdiction to respect such regulations.

The city-state has had some success in policing this.

It has charged four companies and five individuals - including Chinese nationals - for various offences including fraud, following US concerns that Chinese AI company DeepSeek had been buying Nvidia chips from third parties in Singapore, among other places.

They also tried to probe Megaspeed, but were faced with a number of difficulties.

In a statement released on Thursday, the government said that since February 2025, they had been looking into whether Megaspeed and other firms had made false declarations about data servers which were purchased and exported through Singapore.

The companies stated the equipment would end up in neighbouring Malaysia, but Singapore authorities suspect the equipment's final destinations were elsewhere.

Singapore said it sought assistance from US authorities several times to get more information from one of the investigated companies - Supermicro, a US-based company which the BBC understands was supplying computer servers to Megaspeed.

But "despite these repeated requests, Singapore has to date received limited information or assistance", it said in its statement. The BBC has sought comment from the US State Department.

Singapore authorities said they had also investigated Alice Huang - not for espionage, but for any possible role in Megaspeed's suspected fraud.

In the end, they found "insufficient evidence" of crime and let her leave Singapore in February this year, after holding her passport for several months.

Since launching their probe nearly two years ago, Singapore has yet to bring any charges against Megaspeed or any of the companies and individuals linked to the case.

Singapore and US investigations into Megaspeed are still ongoing.

In 2024, US officials had paid a visit to a Malaysian data centre owned by Megaspeed and saw Nvidia technology still in their boxes, raising concerns that Megaspeed was shipping high-end chips out elsewhere, according to a report by the New York Times.

Last year, Megaspeed appeared to have imported 50,000 more chips than Nvidia could account for, according to a Bloomberg report. Nvidia later adjusted its own figures after visiting more warehouses.

Megaspeed has maintained it has always acted lawfully and that previous inspections by authorities found no evidence of illegal activity.

On Thursday, the company said it "welcomes appropriate government scrutiny" and that it is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government".

China has also long refuted accusations that its companies and institutions are illegally obtaining high-end US chips.

Jensen Huang resigned from Nvidia on Thursday following a board meeting

How Megaspeed and Alice Huang came to Singapore highlights other vulnerabilities stemming from the city-state's openness.

In recent years, the city-state has become a prime destination for Chinese companies, with tech firms such as TikTok and Manus making it their global headquarters. Besides Singapore's ease of doing business, it has a population that is mostly ethnically Chinese and largely views China positively.

For a Chinese firm, Singapore offers a base near the mainland and a "neutral address" that global customers and investors accept, said Winston Ma, adjunct professor at New York University's School of Law.

But critics say this has given rise to "Singapore-washing", where Chinese companies use the city-state's clean image to obscure their origins, reduce suspicions and even cover up dubious activities.

Washington-based security analysis group C4ADS told the BBC they had assessed Alice Huang's setup of Megaspeed to be such a case.

BBC checks of corporate records in China, Hong Kong and Singapore found that in 2019, Huang sold a gaming company and its subsidiaries to Chinese gaming firm 7Road.

In March 2023, a 7Road subsidiary in Singapore named Huang as its executive director. The next day it changed its name - to Megaspeed.

Huang was in charge for eight months before she transferred the company to a Singaporean man.

During this period, Megaspeed went through "a critical period of corporate restructuring and regional expansion", and her corporate relationships raised questions about whether "Megaspeed's Singaporean identity is genuine or effectively Chinese-controlled", according to C4ADS.

Even after she transferred Megaspeed, C4ADS assessed that she was still "practically in control" of the company, based on her links to Megaspeed via another company as well as LinkedIn posts where she continued to identify herself as Megaspeed's "chairwoman" at conferences.

Megaspeed has not answered BBC queries regarding the company's and Alice Huang's links to China and the Chinese government.

Alice Huang, seen here in Taipei with Jensen Huang last year, ran Megaspeed for several months in 2023

All this reinforces the dilemma faced by Singapore.

"For the longest time, Singapore has managed to enjoy the best of both worlds with excellent relations with US and China," Nanyang Technological University politics and international studies lecturer Dylan Loh said.

Singapore has long positioned itself as a neutral party between the two superpowers. Its prime minister Lawrence Wong has insisted that his country is neither pro-China nor pro-America, but "pro-Singapore".

But Loh says this balancing act "has now fast become very difficult to manage and sometimes quite fraught too" with the US-China dispute over chips.

With the Megaspeed case, the tightrope Singapore has walked is set to get even narrower, according to analysts.

Beijing may ask why Singapore is investigating issues pertaining to US restrictions and whether they are siding with the US' interests, says Chong Ja Ian, a Singaporean political scientist and non-resident scholar with Carnegie China.

Washington, in turn, might read Singapore's latest statement as "pushing blame" on the US, which could lead to backlash from the White House, he said.

"Singapore has made a policy position not to take sides. But by not choosing a side, they run the risk of being seen as complicit" in either side's agenda, he said.

But Benjamin Ho, an assistant professor and China expert at Singapore's S Rajaratnam School of International Studies, noted that this was "not the first or last time Singapore will be caught between the US and China".

"This is the price that an open economy has to grapple with - you get caught in the middle when things turn sour."

Additional reporting by Jack Lau of the BBC's Global China Unit.

#102. Germany news: Solar power production approaches record high

Solar power output in Germany in 2026 has already surpassed that in all of last year. Meanwhile, experts say the country is headed for a budget crisis. DW has more.

This is a roundup of the top stories from and about Germany on November 14, 2026:

The Green Party in the northeastern state of Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania has given the green light to a coalition agreement negotiated with the center-left Social Democrats (SPD)and socialist Left Party.

This comes after the Left Party in the state approved the agreement on Friday.

The SPD is expected to give its stamp of approval on Saturday afternoon to the deal, which was negotiated by the three parties following September 20 elections in the state.

The incumbent state premier, Manuela Schwesig of the SPD, intends to stand for reelection on Monday.

The designated coalition will hold a total of 39 seats in the 71-seat state parliament.

The far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD), which emerged as strongest single force from the election, won 32 seats and will be the only opposition party after both the conservative Christian Democrats (CDU) and pro-businessFree Democrats (FDP) fell beneath the 5% threshold to enter parliament.

The AfD made no attempt to form a coalition with the necessary majority, as all other parties refuse to cooperate with it.

Experts in Germany have warned companies, schools and government authorities against reliance on a sole provider for digital services after Microsoft services suffered a major worldwide outage.

"Anyone who makes themselves unilaterally dependent on a single source for central digital technologies risks production downtimes and competitive disadvantages in an emergency," said Ralf Wintergerst, president of the industry association Bitkom.

IT security expert Christopher Kunz from the Heise Online trade magazine said such dependency posed a risk to both German society and economy "because we are not only at the mercy of Microsoft's technical competence, but also the goodwill of the US government."

"Through sanctions, it can ensure in a flash that individual organizations or even entire nations lose access to the Microsoft cloud," he said.

Manuel Atug, the founder and spokesperson for AG Kritis, an alliance of independent experts for critical infrastructures, said there were enough alternative providers from Germany or Europe to allow a diversification.

On Thursday, central services of the Microsoft 365 suite, including Outlook, Teams and Copilot, began to be hit by outages worldwide, with disruptions continuing into Saturday European time.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Leading economics experts have predicted a serious German budget crisis from next year if the issue of spending is not addressed.

Their remarks come as the budget committee of the Bundestag, the lower house of parliament, early on Friday approved a provisional 2027 federal budget after nearly 16 hours of negotiations.

The budget foresees new debt, including loans from special funds, totalling around €210 billion ($231.5 billion).

Spending in the core budget rises to a record €561 billion, while net borrowing climbs to almost €123 billion in the core budget alone.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"This budget policy cannot be sustained in the long term," Clemens Fuest, president of the Ifo Institute in Munich, told the Sunday edition of Die Welt newspaper, saying spending priorities had to be established

"All other spending should be frozen or, if possible, cut," Fuest said, adding that tax rises would be inevitable if this did not happen.

Jens Boysen-Hogrefe of the Kiel Institute for the World Economy (IfW Kiel) sees things similarly.

"Owing to economic fluctuations and special effects, there may be some breathing room in individual years, but the trend is clearly negative," he said, owing to the fact that the population is aging while interest payments are rising.

"Additional levies are likely even with strong spending discipline," he said.

Ansgar Heveling, the president of the Bundesrechnungshof, the federal audit court that monitors financial management by the government, called on the coalition government to do make more effort to consolidate the budget.

Noting that every third euro spent by the government is financed by borrowing, he said it was clear that it was living beyond its means, and warned that the share of interest spending in the federal budget would double by 2020.

An above-average number of sunshine hours and the growing number of photovoltaic facilities has contributed to solar energy production that is approaching a record total for Germany, an industry association says.

By September 27, solar energy output had reached 90.2 terawatt-hours, which is already more than the 90 terawatt-hours generated for all of 2025, according to the German Solar Association (BSW -Solar) in Berlin.

That means that solar energy in net electricity generation in Germany is likely to reach a record level of more than 100 terawatt-hours in a single calendar year — almost 10 times the amount of electricity used by a major city like Hamburg in a year.

It is also almost 50 times as much as the 2.2 terawatt-hours generated byx solar power 20 years ago.

Carsten Körnig, the managing director of BSW-Solar, said the likely record was an important milestone as the world seeks to move away from climate-changing fossil fuels.

"Photovoltaics has developed from a niche technology into a cornerstone of our electricity supply," Körnig said.

"Society as a whole benefits from this: through cheaper electricity, fewer energy import and significantly lower climate-related damages," he said.

At 17.8% of total power generation, photovoltaics was the third most important energy source of domestically produced electricity behind wind power — the top energy source — followed by coal.

Sunny days might be getting rarer now as winter comes into sight, but this year has nonetheless been a very good one for solar energy so far, with almost a fifth of total electricity generation coming from photovoltaics.

We will be looking at that bumper solar harvest, which seems likely to hit a record high.

On a less positive note, economics experts are warning that Germany could run into big budget problems amid record high borrowing.

And another warning has come from experts in view of the latest Microsoft outage: "Don't put all your eggs in one (tech) basket," they are telling German businesses and government agencies.

You can read more about these and other stories in this blog covering all things German.

#101. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#100. After a summer win, India's Gen Z protesters face a winter of uncertainty

India's Gen Z protesters are facing the limits of dissent

Cheers erupted at Delhi's Jantar Mantar protest site in July as news broke that Gen-Z protests had forced federal education minister Dharmendra Pradhan to resign.

One protester turned to me, beaming: "This is the youth's victory."

Just over three months later, the mood has shifted dramatically. Student protesters are now struggling even to assemble. Demonstrations by the Gen Z-led Cockroach Janta Party (CJP) last month over voting rights were met with sweeping restrictions and thousands of peaceful protesters were detained.

The summer had seen a new wave of dissent in India, driven by deep frustration among the youth and fuelled by irreverent online satire.

The movement spread across the country, and Prime Minister Narendra Modi, a leader with a strongman image, unaccustomed to open criticism or mockery, struggled to contain it. But the government's increasingly forceful security response suggests it has learned from its rare concession to protesters, invoking public order to curb dissent.

The ruling Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) national spokesperson Syed Zafar Islam rejected accusations of heavy-handedness and told the BBC: "The right to protest and freedom of expression is mandated by the constitution, but it has to be done within the law. It cannot override the rights of others, at the cost of public security".

This is by no means the first time the Modi government has faced large-scale protests.

But the breadth of them pose a challenge for the BJP, according to Yamini Aiyar, former president of the Centre for Policy Research and visiting professor at Brown University.

The CJP led massive protests in July which ended with the resignation of a federal minister

"They are not defined by caste, gender or economic class," Aiyar said. "Even some of the core BJP base, the upwardly mobile, lower-income classes, were attracted by the movement."

The scale of the youth protests is also magnified by its online presence. Protesters have broken a longstanding political taboo, openly mocking the prime minister online.

"It's more than just laughs," said Sakhi, a student protester who identifies as they/them and affiliated with one of Delhi University's left-wing student organisations.

"The fear of repercussions in other generations isn't felt as much in ours," they said. "Comedy is a new language to challenge authority."

Previous protest movements posed serious challenges to Modi but were easier to dismiss as representing narrow sections of society or to label "anti-national", according to Aiyar.

In 2020, farmers protesting against controversial agricultural reforms were blocked from entering Delhi, leading to months of demonstrations and, at times, clashes on the capital's borders. After a year of protests, the government abandoned the reforms.

Protests against a contentious citizenship law in 2019 and 2020 are a stark reminder of the powers of the judiciary should anyone be deemed a national security threat.

Student activist Umar Khalid was arrested under anti-terror laws after violent clashes in Delhi that killed 53 people, mostly Muslims. He was accused of being a "key conspirator". He has now spent more than six years in jail without his trial having begun.

His partner Banojyotsna Lahiri told the BBC they have no regrets, but that the situation instilled "a kind of fear that has prevailed".

The CJP in its early days became a unifier for Indian youth frustrated with the state of education, jobs and economy

"We are just looking at a very dark mesh of empty space, because we don't know when the endpoint is," Lahiri said. Khalid's latest appeal was lodged this month.

Aiyar believes opponents are using a familiar "playbook" today to "de-legitimise" protests, but that it isn't resonating as convincingly.

Nevertheless, the youth protests have struggled to recapture this summer's momentum.

CJP leaders, including spokesperson Saurav Das, have faced repeated detentions and legal action. Das and fellow leader Ashutosh Ranka spent a week in Delhi's Tihar jail after being accused of trespassing at Jantar Mantar.

The site of their summer victory has since been struck off the list of authorised protest venues, a decision being challenged in court.

Protesters from other groups, including the left-wing All India Students' Association (Aisa), have also faced police action over allegations of defying orders.

"We were expecting some kind of crackdown," said Das. He added that "the government has learnt a lot" since the summer.

Legal experts warn the proceedings themselves can have a chilling effect.

"A lot of cases can lead to acquittals, but it can take years. The process is the punishment, and that can deter protesters," said Ali Khan, an assistant professor at Lloyd Law College.

The restrictions on demonstrations have become excessive, according to constitutional lawyer Vrinda Grover, who is advising the CJP.

Tens of thousands of people gathered at Delhi's iconic Ramlila Maidan for the opposition protest on 1 November

"Those who are organising the protests have repeatedly emphasised that this is non-violent," she argued. "Rights have been whittled down in the name of law and order."

The BJP has rejected suggestions it is suppressing peaceful dissent.

Islam told the BBC that demonstrations "cannot override the rights of other citizens just because a handful of people want to protest and be responsible for violence".

Even dissenting online carries risk. Sakhi's viral social media posts have attracted targeted abuse and transphobic attacks. The BBC has seen posts threatening them with violence and rape.

Sometimes, it can deter Sakhi from protesting or even make them change the way they dress.

"Making people laugh can turn into being called anti-national," Sakhi said. "They want me to feel damaged," they added.

They claim some of their content was removed after getting millions of views.

Under India's information technology laws, the government can order platforms to block content on national security grounds. Notices on X and Instagram have shown accounts being withheld in India.

CJP leaders have likened the crackdown to the 1975 Emergency, when then prime minister Indira Gandhi suspended civil liberties and jailed political opponents.

Opposition parties have, in part, filled the void. Tens of thousands gathered in Delhi on 1 November for a rally organised by INDIA bloc, an alliance of opposition parties led by Congress, and permitted by the authorities.

CJP leaders, meanwhile, insist the movement is bigger than their organisation.

When Das walked out Delhi's Tihar prison on 21 October, photographs showed him smiling.

"I would just say wait and watch," Das told us. "Time is on our side. We are a very young generation."

#99. Iran says it targeted US military radar in Bahrain in retaliatory strike

First direct attack on US military installations for months was in response to US airstrike on communications tower

Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps says it has targeted and destroyed a US early-warning radar in Bahrain in response to a US airstrike on a communications tower in Sirik overlooking the strait of Hormuz. It is Iran’s first direct attack on US military installations for months.

“This is a serious warning to the aggressor enemy to end its aerial mischief and oil smuggling in the strait of Hormuz,” the IRGC said. “In the event of repeated violations and aggression, the IRGC aerospace force is on full alert to confront the enemy without constraint.”

US Central Command denied that the radar system had been destroyed and said US forces had intercepted two Iranian drones headed for Bahrain.

Donald Trump has said in a series of media interviews over the past 24 hours that he will attack Iran only if necessary, claiming that Gulf countries, such as Saudi Arabia, have urged him to hold back from planned large-scale military strikes.

Trump said he had asked the vice-president, JD Vance, to end his interest in negotiating a new deal with Iran and instead focus on corruption and fraud in Democrat-run cities and states.

Vance had been seen by the Iranians as the most trustworthy figure close to Trump, but they had rejected his request for a personal meeting with their negotiators to discuss the terms of a peace deal.

Vance has been at the forefront of Republicans saying the war was the cause of the widespread Republican losses in the midterms. Trump said he had been disappointed in the American public because higher diesel prices were a small price to pay for preventing Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.

An opinion poll in the UK showed 65% of the British public would oppose the US resuming the war in Iran, and only 14% would back it. The poll was conducted by YouGov for the Council for Arab-British Understanding (Caabu).

It also found 54% oppose UK bases being used by the US to mount attacks on Iran, with 29% in favour. Support for additional UK sanctions against Iran was 45%, with 22% opposed.

The poll was conducted on Tuesday and Wednesday this week, before two Iranians were found guilty on Thursday of spying and planning attacks on journalists in the UK. The Iranian embassy has rejected the court judgment as an attempt to advance a political agenda.

Chris Doyle, the director of Caabu, said: “The British public are sceptical about any war on Iran and what it might achieve. What might make the Burnham government take note is the very high level of opposition to any further war amongst 2024 Labour voters, at 79%.”

Iran’s Assembly of Experts, a body of elected Islamic jurists responsible for choosing the supreme leader, has become the first governing institution in Iran to call for a review of the country’s defence doctrine, potentially leading to religious permission for Iran to try to acquire a nuclear weapon.

Get the most important US headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

In a statement released on Thursday, the assembly called for a review of Iran’s membership of the nuclear non-proliferation treaty, describing the move as an “unavoidable necessity” and a “public and national demand”.

The intervention carries a religious as much as political significance because the 88-strong assembly comprises senior clerics, most of whom are supposed to be qualified to exercise independent Islamic legal reasoning.

Iran has had a longstanding prohibition on nuclear weapons, based on a fatwa issued by the late supreme leader Ali Khamenei, but the relevance of the fatwa in the wake of the US attacks on Iran has been questioned widely in political and security circles.

Whether Iran has the stockpiles of uranium or technology to build a nuclear weapon is contested.

The UN nuclear inspectorate has expressed alarm about a continued lack of information from Iran about its nuclear sites. It said: “Our concerns are growing over Iran’s refusal to allow access to design information or inspections of the Isfahan facility. We do not know the exact location of the Isfahan uranium enrichment facility or whether it contains nuclear materials. It is imperative that we carry out our verification activities in Iran without further delay.”

#98. One of France’s Most Popular Athletes Is Running for President

Centrists want alternatives to a matchup between the far right and far left in the 2027 presidential race. Enter Christine Lagarde, a former central banker — and Teddy Riner, a judo star.

When Teddy Riner, a judo champion and one of France’s most popular athletes, announced this week that he would run for president, it was less a game changer than a welcome diversion. For all of his appeal, Mr. Riner, 37, is unlikely to win the race to replace President Emmanuel Macron next spring.

Yet his quixotic candidacy reveals a hard truth about France’s presidential campaign. Five months before voters go to the polls, many are eager, even desperate, for a fresh face to upend a race that looks increasingly as if it’s on a glide path to the election of France’s first far-right government since World War II.

The main far-right candidate, Marine Le Pen, has held a commanding lead over all her rivals for months — a lead that has only been reinforced by weeks of protests, marred by violence, that have shaken France. The most prominent center-right candidate, Édouard Philippe, has faded in the polls as the unrest has sharpened the appeal of far-right calls for law and order and left-wing calls for social justice.

Another beneficiary of the upheaval is a far-left firebrand, Jean-Luc Mélenchon, who now appears to be the likeliest candidate to advance into a second round against Ms. Le Pen in France’s two-round system. As things currently stand, French voters would be confronted with a choice between two poles of the political spectrum.

Centrist voters are “afraid of being confronted with a duel between Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc Mélenchon,” said Jean-Daniel Lévy, deputy director of Toluna France, a polling firm. These voters, he said, are an “orphan electorate,” lacking an inspiring candidate, and are “still looking for someone.”

Raphaël LLorca, an analyst at the Jean-Jaurès Foundation, a left-leaning research group in Paris, said the search for a white knight showed “the panic of the centrist bloc.” He added, “It is clear that the election is slipping away from them.”

#97. New facilities in Laos and Cambodia: Is China expanding its military presence in Southeast Asia?

China has been growing its economic relationships in Southeast Asia. Now new military facilities in Laos and Cambodia are causing smaller countries to worry China's military networks are also expanding.

It's often said that maintaining good relations with your neighbors is essential. So in early November, China's Foreign Minister Wang Yi invited his counterparts from Laos, Cambodia, Vietnam, Thailand and Myanmar to Beijing.

There the six countries agreed on closer economic and security cooperation.

The visit is just one example of China's growing influence on Southeast Asia. It's not just about trade or infrastructure either, it's also increasingly about military cooperation.

For instance, at the end of September, China and Laos opened a joint military support and training center north of the Laotian capital, Vientiane. Initially Laotian air force pilots are to be trained at the Ban Keun airfield, China has said.

Earlier in 2025 China expanded its footprint at Cambodia's Ream naval base. China uses the facility on the Gulf of Thailand for logistics and training. And while Japanese and Vietnamese ships can visit the port, they're docked at a southern quay and it's unclear if other navies can use the same facilities under the same conditions as China.

"It remains to be seen how this [Ban Keun] will change China's military access to Southeast Asia," says Marco Bünte, a professor of Asian politics at the University Erlangen Nuremberg in Germany.

In recent years, China has significantly expanded its economic influence in the region.

The China-Laos railway and other infrastructure projects connect Laos more closely with its northern neighbor. Laos is also heavily indebted to China.

Bünte points out that Laos and Cambodia have been close military partners of China for some time and the new facility in Laos can be seen as another step towards deepening cooperation.

If that proves correct, "China would gain strategic depth in mainland Southeast Asia," Bünte explained. "It would also give China better military access to the South China Sea."

Johann Fuhrmann, head of the Konrad Adenauer Foundation's Beijing office, points out that one should distinguish between training Laotian pilots and any potential use of the facilities by Chinese armed forces later.

"China gains influence when it co-designs the training and operation of such facilities over several years," Fuhrmann told DW.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

But nobody should underestimate China's military positioning, Bünte continued. "China is expanding its military operational capabilities here although I wouldn't speak of military hegemony because China hasn't achieved that yet."

With regard to the Laotian airbase, China says that Laotian pilots will be trained there. But beside military transporters and helicopters, Laos actually only has three light combat aircraft by Russia's Yakovlev works.

According to the aviation media outlet FlightGlobal, four more K-8s, Chinese two-seat intermediate jet trainer and light attack aircrafts, are in service there. Satellite images analyzed by the Reuters news agency in early October show eight K-8s at the base — even thought this doesn't necessarily mean they are stationed there permanently.

Fuhrmann doesn't think anybody should jump to conclusions about this.

"The idea of ​​a Chinese air corridor is more of a future scenario than something that has already been proven," he says.

For example, the runway at Ban Keun is only 2,000 meters (6,562 feet) long, which makes it too short for large Chinese bombers. These usually require 3,000 meters.

The crucial question is what rights China will be granted, Fuhrmann continues: "Will China have to obtain the approval of the respective government for every new use? Or are there longer-term commitments that both sides can invoke, even in times of crisis?"

Surrounding countries are reacting differently to what appears to be China's growing influence in Laos, Cambodia and Myanmar because these countries are already considered closer to China.

Other Southeast Asian nations are hedging, trying to expand their relationship with China without severing ties with the US and also maintaining economic links with other Western nations. China's involvement offers them additional options and Beijing wants to explore those possibilities. That's just pragmatic though, Bünte says of the smaller Asian nations.

"Overall, these states… try to keep as many options open as possible," he notes.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Vietnam and the Philippines are watching developments particularly closely. Both have territorial disputes with China in the South China Sea. But Vietnam also has close historical ties to Laos and trained its own military there.

In early November, the Vietnamese government declared that any military cooperation must serve peace and stability and referred to the Treaty of Amity and Cooperation in Southeast Asia, commonly known as the ASEAN Treaty. China is not an ASEAN member but has also stated that its activities are not directed against any third parties.

"So far, these are individual steps," Bünte adds. "We would have to wait and see if this leads to a broader military alliance in the years to come."

Fuhrmann says that Western nations should help strengthen the smaller countries in the region and take their interests seriously.

"Ultimately, a counterweight can only be achieved through tangible contributions, not through warnings or lectures from Europe," he says.

This story was originally published in German.

#96. Arrested generals and a threatened VP: President's son fuels Ugandan succession fever

Gen Muhoozi Kainerugaba has signalled his intention to run for president in the next elections

In Uganda's military, even senior generals are finding that their rank offers little protection from the wrath of the country's powerful army chief.

Over the past 15 months, at least a dozen generals and senior officers have been arrested under Gen Muhoozi Kainerugaba, who is also the son of President Yoweri Museveni, in a crackdown that has unsettled the military's top ranks.

The arrests come amid growing political tensions over who will succeed Museveni, who has ruled Uganda for four decades.

Gen Kainerugaba, 52, is widely seen as a potential successor, although his ambitions appear to be facing resistance within the ruling party.

This week, he threatened Vice-President Jessica Alupo with dismissal and possible arrest, while suggesting he should take her position.

The ruling National Resistance Movement (NRM) has, however, rejected reports that its senior leadership had rejected a proposal to back Gen Kainerugaba as the next president, describing the claims as "completely false".

This has all fuelled speculation that the arrests are part of an effort to sideline military officers opposed to the general's presidential ambitions.

Others see the arrests as evidence of an anti-corruption drive in which senior commanders are held to account.

At the centre of the tension are two powerful powerful institutions: the military, where Gen Kainerugaba wields considerable influence, and the NRM, where questions about his political ambitions appear to be dividing senior figures.

A recent meeting of NRM's top leadership ended without agreement on a proposal to position Gen Kainerugaba as Museveni's successor, according to the AFP news agency.

Five party sources who attended the closed-door meeting told AFP that a vote was abandoned after several members spoke against the proposal.

Uganda's news website ChimpReports, however, said that most members who spoke backed the general's potential presidency, although some questioned his readiness for civilian leadership.

In a statement on Thursday, NRM Secretary General Richard Todwong Awany confirmed that the party had met on 10 November to discuss "political, social and economic issues".

He dismissed reports that the meeting had rejected a proposal to position Gen Kainerugaba as Uganda's next president, saying the party would communicate any decisions "through the right channel, at the right time".

President Yoweri Museveni's grandson (standing up, shaking the president's hand) has joined the army, alongside his father (sitting down, wearing camouflage)

Dr Gerald Bareebe, a Ugandan-born political scientist at Canada's York University, sees a connection between the arrests and political manoeuvring over Museveni's succession, often referred to as the "Muhoozi Project".

"Senior officers believed not to support the project have been arrested, detained or charged with corruption and treason," he tells the BBC.

He says the allegations against some officers were widely viewed as politically motivated, reflecting growing divisions within the military over the succession question.

Some of those detained have since been released, apparently after securing the general's forgiveness.

The detention of one of the country's most prominent former commanders, Lt Gen Peter Elwelu, has brought the crackdown into sharper focus.

The former deputy army chief was detained on 2 November over allegations that he had plotted to destabilise the eastern Teso region.

His fall from grace followed a series of social media posts by Kainerugaba, who accused him of treachery and announced that he had been stripped of his three-star rank and demoted.

"The traitor Peter Elwelu is hereby stripped of his ranks. He is demoted to Private," Kainerugaba wrote on X.

He also claimed that more than 20 soldiers linked to Elwelu had been arrested.

The announcement raised legal questions, with Ugandan media reporting that military regulations do not allow a senior officer to be stripped of his rank through a social media post.

The army later confirmed Elwelu's detention, saying they had recovered 15 guns, military uniforms and two pickup trucks linked to him. His family has rejected the allegations.

Elwelu is among at least a dozen generals and senior officers detained since April 2025, according to an investigation by Uganda's Daily Monitor newspaper.

They include three former military intelligence chiefs who once oversaw some of Uganda's most sensitive security operations.

This army chief threatened to castrate opposition leader and claimed descent from Jesus

The 'shadow army' helping Uganda's long-serving president keep an iron grip on power

The Ugandan general who lobs grenades on Twitter

Maj Gen James Birungi, a former head of defence intelligence and security, was arrested in August 2025, on allegations including treason, terrorism and corruption.

He was charged before a military court but later released after Gen Kainerugaba announced that he had "forgiven" him.

Another former intelligence chief, Maj Gen Abel Kandiho, was detained in September this year over allegations of providing false intelligence and treachery. He remains in detention, according to the Daily Monitor.

His predecessor, Brig-Gen Charles Bakahumura, was also arrested in September over similar allegations, but was later released after the army chief announced that he had forgiven him.

Other officers have faced accusations ranging from stealing military fuel to taking money to spy on military communications.

The military has defended the arrests as part of efforts to tackle corruption and criminality within the armed forces.

Maj Gen James Birungi was arrested in August 2025, on allegations including treason, terrorism and corruption

Ugandan security analyst Freddie David Egesa says the arrests reflect investigations involving different security agencies, rather than an army-led purge.

He argues that detaining senior officers helps protect witnesses and demonstrates the seriousness of the anti-corruption drive.

"Gen Muhoozi has the overall duty to permit the investigation team to arrest these high-profile people, in consultation with the commander-in-chief," Egesa says, adding: "The arrests are not summarily done."

But Bareebe sees a wider political motive, arguing that officers suspected of opposing to Gen Kainerugaba's presidential ambitions are being targeted.

"The result is a broad purge that has affected both serving and retired officers perceived to be anti-Muhoozi," he says.

He points to divisions between the elite Special Forces Command (SFC), which he says is largely loyal to the president's son, and army commanders who remain unconvinced about his succession.

The allegations against the officers are at different stages, with some facing charges, others in detention without publicly announced charges, and several having been released.

Bareebe also questions whether military trials can deliver independent proceedings.

"Many Ugandans regard the charges as politically motivated and therefore expect little justice," he adds.

He alleges that some officers have had charges dropped after closed-door negotiations with the president and his son, during which they were pressured to declare support for Gen Kainerugaba.

Government spokesperson Allan Kasujja dismissed the claims of a succession fallout as "hogwash".

Unlike his predecessors, who communicated through official military channels, Gen Kainerugaba frequently uses X to announce arrests, criticise public officials and comment on domestic and international affairs. Some have sparked diplomatic controversy.

On the night of 9 November, he turned his attention to Vice-President Jessica Alupo, demanding to take her job.

"Whoever is in that office should prepare to hand it over soon," he wrote, in posts that he later deleted.

He also questioned Alupo's loyalty and threatened her dismissal, adding that the government would operate "on military terms", if he became his father's deputy.

The following day, his X account, which had nearly 1.4 million followers, became inaccessible.

It was unclear whether he deactivated it himself, which he has done before, or the platform had suspended it.

Alupo remains in office and has yet to respond publicly.

Vice-President Jessica Alupo is yet to respond to Gen Kainerugaba's posts questioning her loyalty

Egesa says Gen Kainerugaba's controversial posts reflect his preferred style of communicating, but adds that security analysts are assessing their implications for Uganda's political and military stability.

Bareebe, however, sees them as part of a broader effort by the army chief to extend his influence into civilian politics.

He says the public confrontation with senior political figures illustrates how the succession debate was spilling beyond the army and into the ruling party.

Gen Kainerugaba was appointed army chief in March 2024, after previously commanding the SFC, which is responsible for protecting the president and strategic installations.

Under Uganda's military structure, he reports directly to Museveni, giving him considerable influence over the army.

Bareebe argues that the SFC's loyalty to the president and his son could prove crucial in any future transfer of power.

"The regular army remains important but has been sidelined by the SFC, which now functions as a personal army for Museveni and his son," he adds.

At the heart of the tension is the question of who will succeed Museveni, 82, who has ruled Uganda since 1986, when his rebel National Resistance Army seized power after a five-year guerrilla war.

He was sworn in for a seventh elected presidential term earlier this year, extending his rule into a fifth decade.

Gen Kainerugaba has been regarded as a possible successor and has indicated that he intends to seek the presidency in 2031, when his father's current term is due to end.

He also leads the Patriotic League of Uganda, a political movement that has mobilised support around his leadership.

But recent developments suggest that a transfer of power from father to son may be more complicated than his supporters anticipated.

At the recent meeting of the NRM leadership, one official reportedly urged Museveni to retire his son from the military and give him a civilian role to gain administrative experience, AFP reported.

Local media reported that some party officials consider Gen Salim Saleh, Museveni's younger brother, better suited for civilian leadership.

There is, however, no indication that he is interested in becoming president.

Others reportedly defended Gen Kainerugaba, pointing to his military leadership, anti-corruption campaign and grassroots political mobilisation.

Bareebe says opposition to the proposed succession has grown within the NRM, with both veteran figures and younger members concerned about what they see as an attempt to secure power through coercion.

He suggests Museveni's reluctance to move his son out of the military could reflect the importance of retaining control of the army during any transition.

"The underlying calculation appears to be that, so long as Muhoozi controls the SFC, he can use it to capture power even in an event of a contested transition," he says.

The military has been central to Museveni's hold on power, making the loyalties of senior commanders particularly important as succession questions intensify.

The family's military influence has also drawn attention to the possibility of a political dynasty spanning generations, with Gen Kainerugaba's son, Ruhamya, recently graduating from Britain's Sandhurst military academy.

The succession debate is unfolding against a backdrop of sustained pressure on the opposition.

Veteran opposition politician Kizza Besigye remains in detention on treason charges, which he denies.

Bobi Wine, who came second to Museveni in this year's presidential election, has gone into exile in the US, after being threatened by Gen Kainerugaba.

The government has consistently defended its security operations as lawful and necessary to maintain stability.

But the arrests, his son's threats and competing accounts of divisions within the ruling party have intensified questions about how Uganda's next political transition might unfold, and what role his family may play.
