#95. Eisenkot condemns Netanyahu’s ‘racist’ campaign against Israel’s Arab citizens

Prime ministerial hopeful calls Arab minority ‘an integral part of the Israeli story’ as he seeks to form government

Israeli prime ministerial hopeful Gadi Eisenkot condemned a “racist and aggressive campaign of delegitimisation” against the country’s Arab citizens, after the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, accused Arab parties of electoral fraud without evidence.

Netanyahu has repeatedly targeted Arab parties and Palestinian citizens of Israel after his party received a disappointing electoral showing, accusing them of manipulating the election and being linked to Hamas. Israeli electoral authorities have rejected these claims as not being backed by evidence.

Arab citizens of Israel, the vast majority of them of Palestinian descent, comprise more than 20% of the country’s population.

Shin Bet, Israel’s internal security branch, sought to question four Arab lawmakers, including the Arab-majority Ra’am party head, Mansour Abbas, leading to accusations that the security agency had become politicised.

“Arab citizens of Israel are an integral part of the Israeli story. The attempt to marginalise them and vilify them collectively is a profound violation of the principles of statesmanship,” Eisenkot said in a statement on Friday.

Eisenkot’s statement was a rare instance of a prominent Jewish political leader taking a strong stand in defence of Arab Israelis.

The statement also reflected political realities in Israel, as Arab parties found themselves kingmakers after the latest election. The two Arab-majority parties, Ra’am and the Joint List, secured 19 seats in the October election.

They have the potential to help Eisenkot form a governing coalition and become Israel’s next prime minister. If Eisenkot cannot form a coalition within 28 days, with a possible 14-day extension, the mandate to form a government would pass to another lawmaker, potentially Netanyahu.

Eisenkot has to balance the competing interests of several parties if he hopes to form a coalition. Rightwing elements of the opposition have pledged to drop their support of the prospective PM if he cooperates with Arab parties.

His statement on Friday portrayed a more statesman-like approach to ruling than his more rightwing, demagogic opponents who have used Arab parties and citizens as political scapegoats over the last three years.

“[Arab citizens] have been subjected to a racist and aggressive campaign of delegitimisation, fuelled by the outgoing prime minister and those around him for political purposes,” Eisenkot said in his statement.

“The prime minister of Israel is the prime minister of all Israeli citizens, including those who did not vote for him,” he added.

Netanyahu and his allies have repeatedly criticised leaders of Arab parties for refusing to describe Hamas as a “terror group” after its unprecedented 7 October 2023 attack on Israel.

All leaders of Arab parties have condemned the attack, in which Arab Israelis – descendants of Palestinians who remained in what became Israel in 1948 – were also among those killed.

In a speech, Netanyahu falsely claimed that the two Arab lists sought to establish either an “Islamic state” or a “nationalist Palestinian state”.

During the same ceremony, the national security minister, Itamar Ben Gvir, heckled Joint List’s leader, Yousef Jabareen, during his oath of allegiance, accusing him of having “pledged allegiance to Hamas”.

“It is entirely legitimate to disagree with representatives of the Arab parties, even to strongly disapprove of some of them. But it is neither legitimate nor acceptable to malign an entire community, and I will not allow it,” Eisenkot said.

President Isaac Herzog tasked Eisenkot with forming a new government on Sunday after consultations in which a majority of political parties, including the two Arab lists, recommended him for the role.

Israeli media has reported that Eisenkot has also been reaching out to the country’s ultra-Orthodox parties for a potential alliance. The issue of the ultra-Orthodox not serving in the Israeli military was one of the hot button issues during the October election, with Eisenkot previously saying they should also be included in the country’s mandatory draft.

#93. Jojo the gorilla has spent his life alone. Can he finally find a companion?

Jojo the gorilla has spent his life alone. Can he finally find a companion?

In 2015, a young male gorilla named Jojo took his first tentative steps onto grass.

Until that moment, the 11-year-old ape had spent almost his entire life in a tiny cage at a private zoo in Gabon, after being taken from the wild as an infant. His family had been killed by the poachers who took him.

More than a decade later, Jojo is settled into a sanctuary â but he still lives alone. He hasn't had a family since he was taken, or indeed any gorilla friends.

Jojo's carers at the Lekedi Foundation in Gabon want to find a companion - possibly another rescued or zoo-bred gorilla â that Jojo could live and bond with. But finding him a friend - and bringing them into his life - is not straightforward.

"He's probably the most extreme case of isolation I've ever seen in a great ape," says Prof Gillian Forrester from the University of Sussex, who is studying Jojo as part of research into great ape psychology and how to help rescued animals recover from trauma.

Jojo's keeper Maxe Anaclet Makaba: 'He definitely feels lonely - you can see it in his eyes'

Jojo was less than a year old â an infant clinging to his mother â when he was captured and sold.

Most gorillas spend the first 14 to 19 years of their life in an embedded family unit but he was kept completely alone and on public display at the private zoo that bought him.

Maxe Anaclet Makaba, who is head keeper at Lekedi and sees Jojo nearly every day, says it is clear he "craves human interaction".

"He will even go to the trouble of gathering leaves for us to hold, so that we [trade them] for a treat â to interact with us," he says.

Jojo also calls out when women he knows well visit his enclosure and then leave, making sounds that silverback males use to summon the females in the wild, explains Ãric Willaume, who runs the sanctuary.

When Jojo first arrived at the sanctuary after spending years in a small iron cage, he didn't look directly at people

Jojo is one of the more than 6,000 African primates estimated to have been bought or sold in the global trade of apes and other primates between 2000 and 2023. Thousands more are trafficked illegally for use as exotic pets, wild meat, and traditional medicine, the Pan African Sanctuary Alliance says., external

Experts believe when baby great apes are taken from their group at a young age like Jojo, they are left traumatised.

"If they're old enough to understand what happened to them, to understand their family was killed, they just lose the will to live," explains conservationist Tony King, from UK charity the Aspinall Foundation. "You see it in their eyes â the light goes out. I never knew where that expression came from, but I've seen it in gorillas."

King â who specialises in rehabilitating rescued great apes â remembers rescuing two infant sister gorillas who were brought to a sanctuary in the Congo Basin.

"They were clinging to each other," he says. "They stayed together in a little tight ball for about three days before they sort of unravelled. I think that's what kept them alive."

Sisters Likendze and Matoko clutch each other after they were brought in a wooden crate to the Project Protection des Gorilles sanctuary in the Congo

African charity the Gorilla Rehabilitation and Conservation Education (Grace) Center uses what it calls "gorilla-led" rehabilitation. When the team rescues a young gorilla from the pet trade, they put it with a group as soon as possible so they can socialise and essentially learn to be a gorilla.

But Jojo's case is unusual. He is fully mature and showing signs of harmful, chronic stress, according to Forrester.

In a game-changing approach to studying anxiety and trauma in apes, she has used a thermal camera to film Jojo's face. This allows her to monitor blood flow, revealing what appears to be a chronically cold nose.

It's a sign of a malfunctioning stress response. When any primate â a human, chimp or gorilla - is stressed, blood flow to the eyes and ears increases. It puts them on high alert for danger, but leaves their nose, temporarily, colder. For Jojo, this "nasal dip" as it is known, does not seem to recover.

"Being connected to others is incredibly important to our psychology as great apes," Forrester says. "Having another individual to share his life with would be so healing for him.

"I would assume other gorillas would help him process this grief and trauma."

Thermal imaging of Jojo's face reveals signs of chronic stress, with a persistently cold nose indicating a potentially disrupted stress response

Dr Katie Fawcett, from the Grace Center, says it's also "essential" for an animal to form social relationships to be considered for release into the wild - which is the ultimate goal for Jojo's keepers.

The bonds between gorillas â what we would call friendship - is key to their society, adds Dr Robin Morrison from the University of Zurich, who studies the social lives of mountain gorillas. In one recent study, she discovered that female mountain gorillas would seek out females they knew, even if they hadn't seen them for many years, when joining a group.

And she says a big part of a gorilla's normal social life is "just sitting together quietly sharing space".

But Jojo has never been given the opportunity to sit quietly with another gorilla.

No one knows how Jojo will react to a companion. Moving an animal from an accredited zoo would be a decision for an international committee and based on genetics, health and the animals' personalities and compatibility.

Any introduction to another animal would be done very carefully â perhaps giving them an object that smells of the other before they see each other.

These slow, cautious ape introductions do happen â when an animal moves from one zoo group to another, for example. Forrester has contacted a number of accredited zoos to explore the possibility of doing this for Jojo. But those discussions have fizzled out with no sign of progress.

The problem, she says, is "there aren't clear processes in place to do these kinds of trans-locations".

Encounters between two or more separate gorilla groups are complex social events

The international rules and processes for moving captive gorillas across international borders - from one zoo or safari park to another - are designed for breeding programmes.

"They're not designed for reintroductions into the wild, or to find companions for animals that might need them," says Forrester.

And because Jojo, who is probably in his 20s, has reached maturity, King thinks it might be too late for him to bond with other gorillas.

"Jojo's situation is unique, but he is probably best where he is," King says, adding that introducing him to another animal could, somewhat ironically, be a source of more stress for him.

Prof Forrester studies Jojo as part of research to understand how our understanding of human mental health - particularly stress and trauma - can be applied to our great ape relatives

Being alone is also natural, even relatively common, for mature male gorillas. According to Morrison, about a third of adult males are solitary. "When they reach adolescence, they tend to disperse and spend a good chunk of their life on their own."

Solo males will try to persuade females to form a group with them, but some don't succeed and may feel lonely as a consequence, Morrison believes. "If we think of loneliness in an evolutionary context - this mismatch between the social interactions they want and the social interactions they're getting. I suspect feeling lonely is part of the natural, evolutionary drive to attract females."

But while those phases â of family life and adult solitude - play a role in wild gorilla society, Jojo was forced into solitary confinement as an infant.

Morrison believes Jojo is probably both traumatised and lonely.

"[In the wild] gorillas move together, find food together, sleep together," she says. "So much of their lives is just being with this group."

That is what Jojo's keepers, Forrester, and others want for Jojo. A friend. Maybe a female, or a compatible male.

"It would be good for him to have a companion," says keeper Makaba. "Because being alone is like being cut off from the world - it's like being in prison.

"Having another gorilla there would be a good thing for him."

#92. Former Hamas bodyguard convicted of Birmingham knife crime also wanted in Germany

Mohammed Alfatih rearrested over alleged plot to target Israelis in Europe while detained for attack on bailiff

A former Hamas bodyguard convicted of knife crime in the UK is wanted in Germany for allegedly plotting to murder Jewish people.

Details of the alleged plot can now be reported after Mohammed Alfatih, also known as Mohammed Naim, was found guilty on Friday of a knife attack on a bailiff outside his home in Birmingham.

The 40-year-old was found guilty by a majority verdict of unlawful wounding, possession of two knives and criminal damage. He was cleared of the more serious charge of wounding with intent.

He was under surveillance by a counter-terrorism unit when he attacked the bailiff last year.

Alfatih, the son of a senior Hamas official, could not be named during his trial because of reporting restrictions.

He had been photographed working for a former leader of Hamas before being granted asylum in the UK in 2015 and British citizenship in 2020.

While detained for the attack on the bailiff in Birmingham, he was rearrested after authorities in Germany said he had been part of an alleged plot to attack Israeli targets in central Europe.

Alfatih, who was born in Germany, is expected to be extradited to the country after he has served his sentence in the UK, with his connection to Hamas stated in the German extradition request.

German prosecutors charged seven suspected Hamas members in September over an alleged plot to target Jews or Israelis in Germany or Austria around the second anniversary of the 7 October attack on Israel.

They said “Mohammed A” played a prominent role in transporting and storing weapons but was being prosecuted separately after UK authorities arrested him in London and noted he had not yet been returned to Germany.

German authorities said he was connected to Hamas’s leadership and was said to have produced a video in advance to take responsibility for the attack on its behalf.

The attack was disrupted by Germany’s domestic intelligence service after a tipoff from the Mossad, Israel’s secret service.

During a two-week trial at Birmingham crown court, the jury was shown video footage of Alfatih lashing out with a knife at the broken window of a van on 14 October 2025.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

The bailiff, Kevin Bucknall, was left with cuts to his head and required hospital treatment. The defendant pleaded not guilty on the grounds of self-defence.

The jurors, who convicted Alfatih after being shown footage of him brandishing two large kitchen knives outside his home in Kings Heath, Birmingham, were not told of his links to Hamas.

They were also not told Alfatih was under surveillance by a counter-terrorism unit after being released on bail on suspicion of a terror offence days before the bailiff was attacked.

A government source said: “This is a staggering example of the loss of border control under the last Conservative government, who granted his asylum claim and gave him citizenship.

“This Labour government has just introduced reforms that ensure those who gain citizenship must first show they are a good citizen and have contributed to this country.”

#91. Border Crossings Have Plummeted. Stephen Miller Isn’t Satisfied.

Mexico has cooperated to significantly slow the flow of migrants. The new White House chief of staff insists it is not enough.

Since President Trump resumed office, the number of illegal crossings at the U.S.-Mexico border has dropped to the lowest point in decades.

For one of Mr. Trump’s top policy advisers, that is not enough.

In conversations with both American and Mexican officials over the last several months, Stephen Miller has insisted that illegal border crossings must be reduced, according to officials in both countries familiar with the private conversations but not authorized to discuss them.

Mr. Miller’s goal, they said, is zero crossings. Mexican officials have said that, with a nearly 2,000-mile border, that is simply not possible.

In his new role as chief of staff, Mr. Miller is expected to continue driving that agenda.

One of the main forums for Mr. Miller’s demands has been his near daily calls with the U.S. ambassador to Mexico, Ronald ​Johnson, some of the officials said. On calls with staff, Mr. Miller, previously the deputy chief of staff, frequently rails about the number of border crossings, despite their low figures, the officials said.

That has been especially true in recent months, they said, as border crossings increased slightly, to the highest point in Mr. Trump’s second term.

#88. Russia is ‘setting traps’ in Ukraine talks, EU foreign policy chief warns

Kaja Kallas says Moscow is trying to divert attention from its aggression by raising issue of European security

The EU’s foreign policy chief has warned against “walking into the traps that Russia is setting” as she said Moscow was trying to start a debate about European security in order to divert attention from its aggression.

“We have one aggressor and one victim, and the aggressor does not have security concerns,” Kaja Kallas said on arrival at an EU leaders’ summit in Dublin. “Nobody has ever attacked them. They have attacked others. I mean, keep the focus, people.”

In talks on the Ukraine war, Russia has revived its hardline demands for a total overhaul of European security – demands that were rejected in 2021 because they were seen as an infringement of the sovereignty of central and eastern European countries. The latest round of talks between Ukraine, Russia and the US was held this week in Abu Dhabi.

Donald Trump’s envoy and son-in-law, Jared Kushner, said on Thursday night that Russia and Ukraine had agreed to expand the next round of peace negotiations to include the Europeans. However, Russia quickly contradicted that assurance.

European leaders have long said they will support Ukraine in any peace talks, but want to see a ceasefire before negotiations. They also stress the continent has an extensive web of security agreements that Russia has violated.

Friedrich Merz, the German chancellor, said: “Russia wants to talk about questions of a European security architecture. We do not need to discuss that; it already exists.” He added that it was a “given” that his country would be part of any European delegation.

The European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, said it was “self-explanatory that Europe is included in the peace talks to safeguard our interests, but also to support Ukraine”.

The Kremlin said on Friday: “Once we understand [Europe’s] real intentions and their willingness to contribute to the peace process, then we can make a decision on holding talks in an expanded format.” Russia’s lead negotiator in Abu Dhabi, Vladimir Medinsky, said it was “unclear what there is to discuss” with Europe.

The US call to involve Europe in talks has turned the spotlight on who would represent it.

Speaking after a summit of EU leaders in Dublin on Friday, the European Council president, António Costa, said leaders had agreed on a negotiating team: the EU institutions and E5 countries, namely Britain, France, Germany, Italy and Poland.

“Europe must speak with one clear and united voice in all discussions that shape the future of Ukraine and the security of our continent,” he said. “This is ultimately a team effort.”

The format had already emerged in recent months, but the need to formalise the grouping gained momentum after Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, this week urged his European allies to decide who will represent Europe in negotiations.

Kushner also mocked the absence of a single person to speak for the entire continent. “If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiations,” he said earlier this week.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

But there was an increasingly settled view that Europe would not be represented by a single person, but by different interlocutors from the wider continent. Paris and Berlin had argued for a continued role for their own envoys in the E3 format of Britain, France and Germany, alongside EU representatives. Smaller member states put the emphasis more firmly on the EU representatives: Costa and Kallas, the EU’s high representative for foreign affairs and security policy.

Earlier in the day, Sweden’s prime minister, Ulf Kristersson, said the discussion on who should represent Europe was “slightly overstated” as it had differing interests.

Some of the issues in peace talks were “pure EU interests”, he said, citing financing Kyiv and Ukraine’s future membership in the bloc. He also supported other formats, such as the “pretty uncomplicated” E3 and the “very legitimate” E5, which includes Italy and Poland, as well as a role for EU representatives. He said: “I am quite flexible on that. I think what we actually say is more important than the exact configuration of the people in the room.”

Several at the summit, including Kallas, also pointed to the importance of including the UK, a founder member of the coalition of the willing, a broad group of countries committed to future peacekeeping in Ukraine.

Ireland’s taoiseach, Micheál Martin, said the UK and Norway each had a significant role and “that all has to be taken into consideration because it’s a European, continent-wide issue”.

The British prime minister, Andy Burnham, said it was for the EU to decide who would represent the bloc. He said there was a strong view among European leaders that it was for Europe – in the wider sense – “to decide its own arrangements” on security.

#86. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#85. After Xi Purges China’s Generals, a Military Inquisitor Rises

The rise of Zhang Shengmin suggests that China’s top brass remains embroiled in investigations, potentially complicating preparations for war over Taiwan.

When China’s leader, Xi Jinping, endorsed the expulsion of dozens of disgraced officials at a recent Communist Party meeting in Beijing, one man stood out as a clear winner.

Taking his new place at the front of the meeting was Gen. Zhang Shengmin, the somber-faced enforcer of Mr. Xi’s purges inside the military. He had been promoted to the Politburo, the party’s top leadership council, the latest step in his improbable rise from low-profile inspector to a position second only to Mr. Xi in China’s military hierarchy.

General Zhang has been the principal overseer of Mr. Xi’s campaign to purify the People’s Liberation Army. His latest elevation signaled that the investigations, indoctrination drives and loyalty tests could continue for years, as Mr. Xi searches for officers he believes are irreproachably loyal, experts and officials said.

“Twist the blade inward, with strict measures to cure disorder,” China’s main military newspaper said after the party meeting that was held late last month, referring to the continued discipline campaign.

Three officials in Taiwan said they believed Mr. Xi’s loyalty campaign would keep the Chinese military focused on its own problems. That could hinder preparations for possible war over Taiwan, the self-governed island democracy that Beijing claims. The officials spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss internal assessments.

The leadership uncertainty comes as China’s military has continued to pressure Taiwan. Dozens of P.L.A. ships held unannounced drills in seas off the Chinese coast in recent weeks, with some, including a carrier strike group, still remaining, Joseph Wu, the secretary-general of Taiwan’s National Security Council, posted on X on Thursday.

#84. Hungary seizes $19.5m in cash found buried in forests amid graft inquiry

Anti-corruption agency carried out searches as part of investigation into Orbán-era minister accused of taking bribes

Hungary’s anti-corruption agency said on Friday it had seized cash worth $19.5m (£14.8m) buried in forests and hidden elsewhere while investigating an ex-minister of nationalist former leader Viktor Orbán’s cabinet.

Miklós Seszták, Hungary’s minister of national development between 2014 and 2018, is accused of taking bribes in a scheme involving the state transport company, purchasing secondhand buses at inflated prices.

The prime minister, Péter Magyar, won a landslide election victory in April, ending Orbán’s 16 years in power after promising to investigate alleged corruption and abuse of office during his rival’s rule.

Seszták, two other Orbán-era ministers and the former premier’s brother have been taken into custody in different cases as graft inquiries have gathered pace in recent weeks.

The National Asset Recovery and Protection Office (NVVH), an anti-graft body with sweeping powers established as part of Magyar’s accountability drive, said it searched several locations on Thursday and Friday as part of the criminal proceedings against Seszták.

“During the search, investigators found large amounts of cash: buried in forests, hidden in agricultural and warehouse buildings as well as residential properties, and stored in high-value safes in secluded locations,” the statement said.

“The total amount of cash found during the two-day search was 5,071,632,000 forints, €2,658,350 and US $1,257,100,” it added, bringing the combined value to $19.5m.

NVVH said its staff deposited the cash into the Hungarian State Treasury’s account.

“Upon receipt, 12 Treasury employees spent 8 hours counting the money using 5 counting machines,” the finance minister, András Kármán, said on Facebook.

Magyar hailed the seizure as a potential turning point.

“This officially marks the end of the Fidesz-KDNP mafia!” he said in reference to Orbán’s former governing coalition.

The former leader did not react to the latest development. Orbán had previously denounced the probes targeting his inner circle as “political persecution” aimed at imprisoning him.

#81. University hounded out of Hungary by Orbán reaches out to new government

Prospect of partial return to Budapest by Central European University suggests confidence in Hungary’s future

A university that Viktor Orbán tried to chase out of Hungary is open to expanding its presence in Budapest, in a mark of confidence in the country’s democratic future.

Carsten Q Schneider, the interim president and rector of Central European University (CEU), said it was interested in increasing its programmes in Budapest, as long as this did not damage the student experience.

So far the CEU has not held formal talks with Hungary’s new government, which has promised to restore academic freedom, “but our hand is reaching out, their hand is reaching out – it is just that they have many things to do”, the academic told the Guardian.

The CEU relocated its main activities to Vienna from Budapest in 2019, after pressure from the previous government led by Orbán. But the outlook changed when Péter Magyar won a landslide victory in April pledging to root out corruption and reform the state.

The CEU, founded by Hungarian-American financier and philanthropist George Soros in 1991 to entrench democratic change in the region, was forced to cease awarding degrees in Budapest in response to a law widely seen as targeting the institution.

The EU’s highest court struck down Hungary’s “Lex CEU” in 2020, finding that it violated the EU charter of fundamental rights, but the verdict came too late for the university. The CEU retains 312 staff in Budapest, mostly administrative, but the majority, 476, and all its teaching, are in Vienna.

Schneider said upgrading the status of the CEU, which is privately funded, in Budapest would be “a symbol for academic freedom [and] of resilience” but depended on goodwill and funding independent of the Hungarian government. He said a decision on its future in Hungary would be taken by the CEU’s 13-member board, whose members include Soros and his son, Alexander Soros.

“We don’t want special treatment,” Schneider said. ‘“We are a great asset, so let’s work out something.”

But the CEU, which he said was “thriving tremendously” in Vienna, does not plan a wholesale return to Budapest. Any change would have to ensure the quality of the student experience, for instance, assuring that students would be able to combine courses, say in economics and history, without having to travel the 200km-plus distance between the two cities.

Focused on social sciences, law and humanities, the CEU has more than 1,400 students from 103 countries, mostly doing master’s and PhD programmes.

According to Schneider, for the first time in the CEU’s 25-year history, students from the US now make up the largest cohort of master’s students (10%). The CEU has seen an increase in US applicants since Donald Trump returned to the White House and launched a sweeping campaign against elite universities, including slashing federal funding and seeking to exert control over internal policies.

The CEU rector has had informal talks with US academics on how to maintain academic freedom under pressure from rightwing populists. He described his message as: “Don’t be naive for a second. These people have a plan, so it’s not by accident that they attack universities. They want to have cultural hegemony.” He added, however, that Trump and allied movements, such as Germany’s Alternative für Deutschland, have identified “real problems”, such as in the US how universities have entrenched “a reproduction of elites”

When coming under political pressure, he said universities needed “to look for allies”, among other academic institutions, students, alumni and parents. Institutions also had to stand up for disciplines under attack, such as gender studies, Schneider added.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

He recalled the outpouring of support for CEU – when tens of thousands of people protested in Budapest in defence of the university – as “one of the most humbling experiences I have had”.

Schneider, a German political scientist who specialises in the study of democracies and regime transition, said Magyar had successfully used Orbán’s political machine against him.

“Nobody has a better idea than [Magyar] how to defeat the most potent and the most skilled illiberal populist [Orbán]. So he knows something.” He added: “The people and skills that you need to bring a non-democratic regime down are not the same and sometimes the opposite of what it takes to establish a democracy. So the question now is whether he [Magyar] has the skills to basically reinvent liberal democracy for the 21st century.”

Schneider said Hungary could be the world’s first “post-illiberal democracy” – a model for democratic restoration, just as Orbán inspired rightwing populists around the world. But he also raised doubts about this thesis, saying there were questions about how Magyar and his Tisza party might develop in the context of Hungary’s “very difficult economic situation”.

The test would be, he suggested, when Magyar faces losing support and, eventually, defeat at the ballot box. “So what happens when he loses support and then cracks in his movement appear? Will he be the visionary leader who manages … to establish a three, four, five-party system, where people compete? That is to be seen.”

#79. Watch: Divers explore US ship sunk off Florida coast to create new reef

The ship, which was once the fastest in the world and longer than the Titanic, was intentionally sunk off the coast of Florida, in order to create the world's largest artificial ocean reef.

The SS United States spent nearly 30 years docked in Philadelphia before it was moved to Florida, where it will become a home for sea life and an underwater attraction for marine enthusiasts.

Activists unsuccessfully fought for the historic liner - which crossed the Atlantic Ocean hundreds of times - to be preserved as a museum.

#78. UK-EU breakthrough will allow airport-style checks at Eurotunnel to avoid border ‘chaos’

Eurotunnel owner Getlink wants to replicate ‘airport experience’ at Folkestone and Calais, without making people get out of cars

Airport-style passport and fingerprinting controls are to be introduced at Eurotunnel in an attempt to prevent “short-term and long-term chaos” in Folkestone and Calais.

In a breakthrough for travellers, the European Commission has agreed to a new system in which an “eagle eye” camera system, already used in Rome and Paris airports to supervise fingerprinting and facial imaging, will be used at the border between the UK and France.

Yann Leriche, the chief executive of Getlink, the Eurotunnel owner, said it had tried to replicate the “airport experience” but wanted to go much further.

Instead of getting out of the car and getting biometrics taken at a terminal building, holidaymakers and business travellers will now be greeted with special drive-through terminals in Folkestone and Calais.

Both sides are now equipped with 120 kiosks under the case of an eagle-eye camera that will be monitored by police, similar to e-gates at airports where police are not physically present at each gate.

In the coming weeks, Leriche will meet UK and French authorities presenting a 40-page proposal to allow Eurotunnel to become a “laboratory” for an “intelligent border” using emerging tech and lessons learned from the US and Singapore.

The entry and exit system (EES) requires all third-country citizens including British passport holders to submit fingerprints and facial images as they enter and exit the Schengen area, but has been delayed for years.

EES was supposed to be active across the EU by 9 September, but nine of the 27 EU member states, including France, have yet to be deploy it fully because of continued technical issues.

It has always been a logistical nightmare for Eurotunnel and a potential cause of miles-long tailbacks at the approach to the terminal.

Originally, Getlink had planned to use a tablet to hand to the passengers in their cars to allow them to self-fingerprint and capture a facial image. It also looked at a system involving passengers parking up and going into the terminal to get fingerprinting.

But both models would have required the physical presence of police officers for first-time fingerprinting.

Leriche said: “We told the authorities: it is going to be a mess. Many people are going to the wrong place and you can’t have police jumping from one place to the next to solve the issues. It would also means customers would have a bad experience, particularly at peak time.

“This is not something people in airports experience, but nobody approached this before, thinking we could have an airport experience at the border here. But we said: why not?”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He said that after “many, many discussions” with the European Commission and the French authorities, finally they were “authorised” to trial “eagle-eye cameras” at each kiosk. “The French authorities are also on board because for them it is more efficient, too, so we are all aligned on the trial,” he said.

The trial will last two years, but Eurotunnel hopes the experiment will become permanent.

Each car will be able to pull up to an individual kiosk, with two stands per vehicle to read passports and take fingerprints and facial images. “It will prevent the chaos in the long term and the short term and if the experiment works, as hopefully it will, then it will become permanent,” said Leriche.

He said the company had been inspired by visits to the US/Mexico border controls, where drivers and passengers remain in their cars.

The requirement of having a police officer present each time a first-time passenger, or “primo” as they are known, submits their fingerprints was designed as a safeguard against subterfuge. Criminals, for example, could use a fellow passenger for example with a “clean skin” fingerprint.

Leriche said he had no idea when the system will be introduced by France. “It has been delayed and delayed and delayed, but our kiosks are there, they are can function but the connection with the French system is still not working. It is a question was are asking all the time.”

This week it emerged that the EU had filed a lawsuit against IBM and two other companies involved in the implementation of EES.

#77. Europe Agrees on a Team to Represent It in Talks With Russia

After coveting a seat in the Ukraine war discussions, Europe is poised to get one. The continent wants to fill it with a six-member team.

Reporting from Dublin, where European leaders are meeting, and from an airfield near the city where Americans and Ukrainians debriefed on Thursday night.

European leaders have waited on the margins of negotiations to end Russia’s war in Ukraine for months, eyeing a seat at the table. They learned this week that they would get one, and simply needed to decide which official should sit in that chair.

European Union leaders concluded in Dublin on Friday that they will tap a group of major nations — Britain, France, Germany, Poland, and Italy — along with a representative from the E.U’s leadership arms, the European Council and European Commission.

In some ways, the choice of a crowd plays into jibes Americans have made about the continent’s indecisiveness: “If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiation,” Jared Kushner, President Trump’s son-in-law and a U.S. envoy, said earlier this week.

Europe’s failure to pick one negotiator leaves open the possibility that its fragmentation will diminish its influence. Even so, Europeans have underscored that they are a democracy of many nations and that many voices must be represented.

“We decide on who represents us, and that is what we have done today,” said Ursula von der Leyen, the president of the European Commission.

“We need to work as a team,” António Costa, president of the European Union’s political leadership arm, said at a news conference on Friday, noting that America itself has two envoys.

#76. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#72. What’s behind Australia’s surge in teen extremism?

Young people are being found with ‘hard drives full of what can only be described as gore’, one expert says

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

In March, Guardian Australia revealed that most people who have been charged with the new federal crime of possession of violent extremist material are under 18.

This week Australian federal police sounded the alarm about a surge in teen extremism: 32 of the 37 young people prosecuted in counter-terrorism cases since 2020 were charged in the past two years.

The growth in youth extremism is happening within a sprawling online ecosystem. My investigations have tracked the rise of online networks that urge violence and nihilism for their own sake, in which perpetrators coerce victims not much younger than themselves to commit brutal and degrading acts.

In a review of 55 youth terrorism cases, the AFP has found that the profile is mixed. They are often young men. Many have received some support or intervention for a mental health condition. Some are from stable homes, others not.

What they have most in common is that they are young people on the internet. Experts say the age of those caught up in these crimes is decreasing.

In the last month two teenagers in Darwin have been charged with collecting documents that could facilitate acts of terrorism.

The arrests came five months after another NT teenager was charged with making posts with “pro-Nazi, anti-immigration, anti-trans and white nationalist themes”. The charge sheet alleges he also possessed videos of white nationalist terrorist attacks, including the 2019 Christchurch mosque shooting and the 2022 Buffalo shooting that it partly inspired.

Experts involved in harm prevention programs, including the Victoria University professor Debra Smith, have watched the age of this cohort decrease dramatically over the past decade.

Instead of considering the challenge exclusively through the lens of radicalisation, Smith says we need to recognise that some young people are being socialised and finding identity in online spaces that may appear exciting and transgressive but are ultimately harmful to themselves, and may encourage them to do harm to others.

She has seen cases where teenagers are being found with “hard drives full of what can only be described as gore”.

“It’s like getting involved in any other … unhealthy subcultural aspect of life … [such as] gangs or crime for payment,” she says. “There’s this offering of what young people can look at and get involved in that just simply wasn’t there years ago.”

The Guardian’s investigation into the growth of extremist material charges, as well as the surge of young people in Australia ensnared in sadistic online extortion and gore networks, reveals an amorphous online ecosystem in which young people may easily come into contact with extreme and violent content.

A teenager in New South Wales who pleaded guilty to possessing violent extremist material, among other charges, described how he first came across such content on TikTok when he was 14 and sought validation by talking to others online. He used ChatGPT to research the Boston marathon bomber Dzhokhar Tsarnaev as well as security arrangements at the Sydney Opera House. He was sentenced to a three-year community corrections order.

Others have told police how they became involved in so-called “gore” channels on Telegram, where more and more extreme footage – including of animal abuse – is traded.

Given the age of many involved, Levi West, a research fellow at the Australian National University, says they may be considered as much victims as perpetrators, which is a significant challenge for how authorities respond.

It’s important for parents, schools and others to think about what’s sitting underneath the uptick in numbers, he says, and not just the “algorithms”. He points to the legacies of Covid-19 and lockdowns as well as the presence of screens and digital technologies in the lives of young people.

“We are not going to arrest our way out of this problem,” West says.

With people as young as 13 before the courts, Australia must consider if authorities are taking the right approach and have the right resources for this challenge.

The Guardian’s investigation into the possession of extremist material charges in South Australia and around the country found that many of those who had been before the courts faced a range of health and social difficulties, including autism diagnoses and learning challenges – and raised questions about whether some young people understand a crime has been committed.

Smith has seen many young people caught up in these activities who come from troubled family backgrounds, while others have had every opportunity in life.

There is a role for everyone involved in caring for young people, she says, from parents and schools to sporting clubs, to recognise when someone might be engaging in harmful behaviours long before there is a risk of violence, then help them access non-punitive pathways for help.

“It’s really important to understand it through a developmental lens – that these are young people who are making decisions with young brains,” Smith says.

“My grave concern is that if we are just giving everyone terrorism offences at [age] 12, 13, 14, 15, we’re starting to label them the thing that they will become.”

#68. Microsoft outage: company scrambles to fix global issue that took down Outlook and Teams

Tech firm says it is ‘deeply sorry’ for disruption that took services down and has now addressed the underlying issue

What caused the outage and when will it be fixed?

Microsoft outage hits UK hospitals, GPs, schools and police

Tell us: have you been affected by the outage?

Microsoft has said it is “deeply sorry” for a widespread outage that caused disruption to millions of users, organisations and businesses across the globe – and that it has “addressed the underlying issue”.

The company said the outage was a “technical issue” not related to cyber-attacks or AI agents, and that it continued to gradually restore services.

The outage of Microsoft’s services, which began Thursday evening, lasted roughly 18 hours before services started to recover just before midday GMT on Friday. Microsoft said it was bringing back services gradually to ensure a stable restoration.

Earlier on Friday, it said email queues were “significantly reduced and approaching normal levels” as it brought back services. More than 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps, and more than 90% of Fortune 500 companies use Copilot, Microsoft’s AI chatbot, according to the company.

On Friday, government agencies worldwide, from municipalities in France to the US House of Representatives, reported problems with Microsoft services. The issues were patchy, with some but not all users reporting problems with services including Microsoft Teams and Outlook email.

The outage website Down Detector showed an increase in Microsoft 365 problems at about 6pm GMT on Thursday, with nearly 130,000 reports of problems in 74 countries affecting access to Word, Excel, PowerPoint, Outlook and Teams as well as enterprise services such as SharePoint and OneDrive. Copilot was also affected.

Microsoft has suggested the outage may have been caused by problems with the settings applied to servers responsible for keeping Microsoft 365 up and running.

Microsoft’s executive vice-president of Copilot, Charles Lamanna, said: “We regret that today’s outages caused disruptions for our customers, and we are working to address the issue.”

The Microsoft outage also hit connectors used by ⁠OpenAI’s and Anthropic’s AI ⁠services.

OpenAI said it was investigating a problem with Microsoft connectors in which new connections might be broken and tools were not working as ⁠expected. Anthropic said it had identified an issue with an upstream provider ​affecting its Microsoft 365 ​connector.

The outage has affected businesses and organisations across the world that use Microsoft products.

In the UK, healthcare systems were disrupted and several British police forces urged people not to try to report crimes online and to phone them in instead.

Some schools in the UK notified parents that reporting attendance or absence issues had to be done via phone. National Rail, which provides information across the UK network, warned people might not be able to access tickets that had been emailed to them.

US government offices across the country have been affected by the outage, including congressional offices that heavily rely on Outlook email. The US House’s chief administrative officer that handles IT for the chamber sent out a “campus-wide alert” to staffers on Thursday about the outage, Politico reported.

Multiple cities, including the City of Tampa, Florida, posted notices on their municipal websites to say that they were affected by the outage. Law enforcement offices across the US were also affected, with one sheriff’s county in Colorado saying it was unable to process bond payments because Outlook email was down, and requesting that anyone who needed to post bond do so in person.

When stock market trading opened on Wall Street, shares in Microsoft dipped slightly, down 1.1% at $516.86.

In Australia, the financial impact of the outage was expected to cost billions of dollars, according to the risk advisory firm, RSM Australia. Several government agencies, including national security sevices, were unable to access or send emails.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Hospitals and health clinics were affected, although the Australian Medical Association on Friday said had been no impacts for patient care. Courts and schools also struggled to operate as normal.

The Indian airlines IndiGo and Akasa Air said their email support services were affected, and Taiwanese security firms, life insurers and investment trust companies said fund management services had been disrupted.

In financial markets some portfolio managers have been unable to check emails, making it hard to access on some trading platforms.

In Japan, the shipping company Sagawa Express said it was unable to accept online reservations for some of its services at airports and train stations while Taisho, a leading pharmaceutical company, said it was having to handle orders by phone.

Microsoft last month reported an ​outage of its Azure cloud platform that affected a ​suite of productivity software ‌and a range ​of industries worldwide.

Mark Gregory, an associate professor in the School of Engineering at RMIT University in Melbourne, said the outage appeared to be affecting the network layer in Microsoft’s systems, and was potentially similar to the configuration issue that brought down Optus’s network in 2023.

He said that when some of the servers had configuration issues, it meant customers were forced to share a smaller pool of servers to access the cloud services.

“If you lose part of that pool, then all of the users are trying to access the remaining servers, and that overloads them. And then they go down. And so you end up with a cascading problem. Then it takes time to find the underlying problem, in this case, a misconfiguration in the network layer.”

Gregory said businesses reliant on cloud services such as Microsoft 365 should have backup options.

Associated Press and Reuters contributed to this report

→ Baca artikel asli

#67. Study Hard, Get a Career and Get Ahead? It’s Not What It Used to Be.

From India to France, the implicit meritocratic bargain that underwrote stable societies by providing advancement to new generations is breaking down.

Protest movements have long relied on students as some of the most ardent foot soldiers. Young people have been on the front lines of protests against war, climate change and racial inequality. But in huge protests that have shaken India and France this fall, students have revolted against the failed promises of education itself.

In India, students took to the streets after the government abruptly canceled the results of a medical-school admissions exam because test questions had been leaked online. Aspiring doctors had to take the test again, after many spent years studying for up to 12 hours a day in hopes of winning one of the coveted spots — and the chance of upward mobility. Young people across the country saw the scandal as emblematic of a broken education system and were furious.

In France, students in poor suburbs outside Paris began protesting the decrepit physical condition of their schools, an opaque university admissions system and absent teachers. French education had long promised to serve as a “social elevator” enabling young people to advance in society, but had fallen into disrepair. Soon, demonstrations spread across the country and, in some cities, spiraled into rioting.

In both countries, the anger of the students shows no signs of abating. And while in many ways France and India could hardly be more different, their unrest was sparked by a similar source of outrage: the breakdown of a foundational social contract that one could call the meritocratic bargain.

It is a promise that lies at the core of many countries’ political compacts. Though specifics can vary, the central proposition usually goes something like this: A fair society rewards merit. Educational achievements are a good measure of merit because education is open to all and leads to valuable skills. Therefore, education should be a route to social and economic advancement.

The value of this system is not just that it selects for those with high ability, but also that it offers a justification for the resulting socioeconomic hierarchy — including who ends up at the bottom. “All selection mechanisms generate losers,” said Nicholas Kuipers, a professor at Princeton University who studies the politics of meritocracy. “The question is, Who do you want to be a loser, and who do you want to be a winner?”

#65. African Miss Universe beauty queens in limbo as US visa restrictions bite

Roseanna Hall is not the only African contestant to be affected by US visa restrictions

Miss Universe Zimbabwe Roseanna Hall and several other contestants from Africa could miss this year's competition in Puerto Rico as they race to secure a US visa.

Hall is due to represent Zimbabwe at the 75th edition of the Miss Universe pageant, with the winner set to be crowned on 24 November. But she has yet to obtain a visa to travel to the US territory.

"We have been back and forth, trying to get my visa approved," Hall said in a video posted on her Instagram page.

Hall is not the only African contestant to be affected by the restrictions. Zambia has withdrawn its representative, Pamela Mubanga, from the competition because of US travel restrictions.

Contestants began arriving in Puerto Rico on 8 November for registration, fittings and other pre-pageant activities, putting pressure on Hall, and others including the representative from South Sudan, to secure their visas.

"Officially, today is the day I was supposed to be leaving for Puerto Rico for the 75th edition of Miss Universe," Hall said.

Zimbabwe is among 19 countries subject to partial US travel restrictions introduced under the Presidential Proclamation 10998, which took effect on 1 January 2026.

The measures suspend the issuance of visitor, student and exchange visas, as well as immigrant visas, to nationals of the affected countries. They also impose restrictions on other categories of non-immigrant visas.

In a statement, the Miss Universe organization in Zambia said Mubanga's situation was a consequence of the travel restrictions rather than individual circumstances.

"Unlike situations where a contestant's visa is denied due to personal circumstances, documentation or organisational challenges, Mubanga's situation is linked to the United States' presidential travel restriction affecting Zambia and other African countries," the statement read.

Mubanga, 25, a registered nurse, was crowned Miss Universe Zambia on 8 October and was due to represent the country at the international pageant.

The statement said five other African countries faced similar difficulties, although it did not name them.

Hall said she shared their concerns, adding that she had spent the past few weeks in South Africa trying to secure her visa.

"I am not alone in this predicament. Some of my African sisters are facing the same predicament, and I know exactly what they are going through," she said.

Miss Universe South Sudan also expressed uncertainty about her participation, commenting on Hall's video: "It is 50/50 whether we will make it, Miss Universe."

In another video posted three days earlier, Hall said she was still hoping to make it to Puerto Rico.

She captioned the video: "The fight to get Zimbabwe to Puerto Rico continues,"

The pageant's final is scheduled to take place at the JosÃ© Miguel Agrelot Coliseum in San Juan.

#63. Stuart Bonds made ‘racist’ and ‘divisive’ comments. But One Nation is showing no sign of dumping the NSW candidate

The former coalminer’s claim Australians are being ‘demographically replaced’ sparked wide criticism, but his party has shown no signs of dumping him

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

In a Facebook video from a Sydney anti-immigration rally on Sunday, as a man dressed like a medieval knight watches on, One Nation’s lead upper house candidate, Stuart Bonds, launches into a speech against “mass” migration.

“Make no mistake, we are being demographically replaced inside our own nation,” he says.

“If we don’t support Australian mothers having Australian children, we [are] going to pay for other cultures’ children to be brought up in our lands.”

In an apparent allusion to the “great replacement theory”, a far-right conspiracy theory that white westerners are being actively “replaced” by minority ethnic immigrants, he continued: “That’s the socialist plan, is to destroy and bring down and tear down the fabric of your civilisation.”

The comments sparked a wave of criticism, but the party has shown no signs of taking Bonds off the ticket for the 2027 New South Wales state election. One Nation did, however, dump one candidate this week: the Victorian branch disendorsed a lower-house candidate over an old social media post describing Hitler as the “GOAT”, an acronym for greatest of all time.

The rally was not a one-off. On a podcast released on Monday with the rally’s organiser, the Australian Lobby Group, Bonds appeared to double down on the remarks. He did not challenge host Scott Challen’s assertion that Labor governments were building “ethnic voting blocks with Middle Eastern and subcontinent people … to stack the numbers to entrench power for themselves”, instead responding: “Mate, and that’s why we’re going to get rid of all of the multicultural grants, right?”

On Wednesday, in an interview with ABC Upper Hunter, Bonds responded to questions about his comments by citing Australian Bureau of Statistics data that shows the proportion of Australians born overseas has trended upward since the end of the second world war, from 10% in 1945 to 32% in 2025. He did not mention that the proportion of Australians born overseas was at its highest – 32.4% – in 1891, the first year of available data.

Bonds said with the Australian birthrate beneath the 2.1 required to replace the population, “I would just like to know what people assume is going to happen.”

“Where are these people going to come from?” he said.

NSW Labor pounced on the rally comments immediately, with the state’s premier, Chris Minns, calling them “frankly outrageous and racist”. The government passed motions in both houses censuring Bonds, not yet a member.

The Coalition followed four days later. On Wednesday afternoon, the opposition leader, Kellie Sloane, said Bonds’ language was “clearly divisive”.

But she did not rule out a confidence and supply arrangement with One Nation after the March state election.

“We will not form a coalition with One Nation, [but] we’ll work with anyone post-election if we’re in a minority government,” she told reporters.

The Australian Financial Review has reported many of her MPs privately expect the party to go into a Coalition with One Nation. And with the Coalition consistently polling behind One Nation, that makes Bonds not a fringe candidate, but a potentially influential figure.

The ABC’s former election analyst, Antony Green, said the remarks played straight into Labor’s hands.

“This will certainly be something the Labor party uses in all those multi-ethnic seats in Sydney,” he said – seats like those on the rural fringes of Sydney, which One Nation said it plans to target.

Indeed the deputy premier, Prue Car, who has Indian heritage and whose electorate of Londonderry is one of the seats in question, gave an impassioned critique of Bonds’ comments in question time on Tuesday.

“Who’s he [Bonds] talking about when he talks about this?” she said. “I tell you who he’s talking about – he’s talking about families like mine, and families that I represent.”

Kos Samaras, a former Labor strategist who is now a director of polling group Redbridge, said that while the comments likely won’t play well in Sydney seats – including alienating moderate Liberal voters worried about the party going into Coalition with One Nation – “you might be able to get away with it in certain parts of NSW”.

And Bonds, a former coalminer, has made his base in the Hunter.

Samaras said One Nation could “clean the deck” in the regions, including Labor-held seats in the Hunter, and “pretty much anything else the Coalition hasn’t got bolted down to the ground”.

The NSW One Nation leader, Mike Newman, who in September said he “made a mistake” when he told a Sydney rally he wanted to stop abortions, did not respond directly when asked if he agreed with Bonds’ comments or if he would rein in his first-placed upper house candidate.

“What I will say is that what Stuart Bonds is a patriotic Aussie. He’s a coalminer. He has very, very strong views,” he said on Tuesday.

Newman, who previously rejected claims that One Nation was racist, said on Tuesday: “What we want to do is … protect Australian values, and that is the fundamental thing that Stuart Bonds is pushing for. It’s about assimilation.”

The NSW treasurer, Daniel Mookhey, whose parents immigrated to Australia from India in the 1970s, told the ABC that Bonds’ comments were not grounded in reality.

“I think he’s saying that there is a deliberate conspiracy to use migrants to replace the way he puts it, native-born Australians for political reasons,” Mookhey said.

The quota for election to the upper house is 4.5%, making Bonds all but guaranteed to be elected, with One Nation polling in the high 20s and low 30s in recent months.

Green expects the party to pick up four or five seats in the upper house; so far it has only named three candidates: Newman, Bonds and Jeff Bacon, a firearms manufacturer who is second on the upper house ticket.

But unlike Victoria, NSW has optional preferential voting, which means when that state goes to the polls in March, lower house preference flows aren’t guaranteed.

But Bonds has downplayed whether he may have jeopardised a future preference deal with the Coalition.

“If the Coalition have a problem with facts, the same as the Labor party, then maybe it will,” he told the ABC.

“But I’m not here to impress the Coalition.”

#59. The U.S.-Israel Relationship Is Broken. Here’s How Eisenkot Could Fix It.

Former Israeli and U.S. ambassadors, lawmakers and analysts largely agree on steps Gadi Eisenkot could take if he becomes prime minister. But they are risky.

Gadi Eisenkot, the former military chief who has been given a chance to form Israel’s new governing coalition and replace Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, is by no means assured of success.

But if he does take power, another hugely daunting challenge would immediately confront him: how to undo the vast damage done to Israel’s relationship with the United States, its closest and most important ally.

It’s hard to imagine things getting much worse.

Many Americans have turned against Israel over its devastating military campaign in Gaza, and even more so over its role in pressing for war with Iran, which led to soaring gas prices.

President Trump announced this month that he would cut off billions of dollars in annual U.S. military aid to Israel by the end of the year and blamed Israel in part for Republican losses in the midterm elections. Democratic control of Congress could quickly lead to sanctions on violent Israeli settlers and on new settlement construction in the occupied West Bank, as well as to new conditions on arms sales.

In the past, Israel could always turn to Congress for support in clashes with the White House, said Michael Oren, a former Israeli ambassador to Washington and deputy prime minister under Mr. Netanyahu. But no longer.

“For the first time in the history of our relationship with the United States, we have no recourse,” he said. “We have to start from scratch. We have a lot of fence mending to do and with no guarantee of success. Zero.”

#56. Aide to fugitive Ukrainian ex-PM wounded in latest Moscow car bombing

The site of a car explosion that wounded Sergei Zavorotny in Moscow

A car bomb attack in Moscow has wounded a former adviser to Ukrainian ex-prime minister Mykola Azarov, Russia's Investigative Committee says.

Sergei Zavorotny, now a journalist and commentator, was behind the wheel of his black Toyota when the explosion happened and Azarov told Russian TV his condition in hospital was "rather critical".

The car bomb went off a day after a Russian colonel was killed in another explosion in St Petersburg, and both blasts are seen as part of a series of attacks on military and prominent figures linked to Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine.

Zavorotny, 72, worked as a press aide to Mykola Azarov in Kyiv until 2014.

But when pro-Kremlin Ukrainian president Viktor Yanukovych was toppled from power, Azarov fled Kyiv for Russia and tried unsuccessfully to set up a government in exile.

In a 2020 US Senate report on interference in the 2016 American presidential election, Zavorotny is described at the time as an SVR [Russian foreign intelligence] officer involved in influence operations.

Russia's Investigative Committee said on Telegram on Friday: "Serhiy Zavorotny, a former adviser to the former Ukrainian Prime Minister Mykola Azarov, was injured while driving a car. He was taken to one of the city hospitals, where he is receiving medical care."

It added: "A set of necessary investigative actions is being carried out. A number of examinations will be appointed, including medical and explosives."

According to several unconfirmed reports, Zavorotny suffered a number of serious injuries after an explosive device was detonated remotely.

Russia said the car bombs are the latest in a series of attacks targeting pro-Kremlin figures.

On Thursday a car bomb in the Pushkinsky District of St Petersburg killed a member of the Russian military, again after it was attached to the base of the car and detonated remotely.

The man targeted in the attack is yet to be named officially, but reputable sources suggest he was a high-ranking figure in the 6th Guards Air and Air Defence Forces Army.

Overnight into Friday, Ukraine and Russia exchanged attacks on each other's territories.

Ukrainian authorities said a hospital had been targeted by Russian bombardment.

They added that three people, including a 17-year-old boy, were also killed in attacks across southern Odesa and Zaporizhzhia.

Russia said a Ukrainian strike on an industrial facility in the central Samara region killed four people.

And in the southern Volgograd region another four died after an industrial zone was hit by drones. A further person died in the Bryansk border region, an area that is often fired upon.

Ukraine said these were retaliatory attacks in response to Russia's persistent targeting of its cities.
