#55. US-bound flights out of Australia delayed after telecommunications outage grounded American planes

Sydney and Melbourne airports say flights were affected by now-lifted ban on departures in US

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Some US-bound flights from Sydney airport were grounded or delayed on Saturday after American authorities halted departures and delayed landings at all US airports.

Flights from Melbourne airport were also affected, with travel for passengers expected to depart for Los Angeles shortly after 10am being delayed by almost three hours.

Sydney airport confirmed the US ban on flight arrivals was lifted shortly before 1pm, although it was not known how this would impact delays.

Earlier, the airport confirmed some US-bound flights had been grounded.

“We are aware of the nationwide ground stop issued by the US Federal Aviation Administration and are monitoring the situation closely,” the spokesperson said.

“Passengers travelling to or via the United States should check with their airline for the latest information regarding their flight.”

Passengers on US bound flights from Sydney Airport reported being stuck on the tarmac for three hours before eventually departing. One plane required extra fuel after running air-conditioning for several hours while waiting.

The FAA announced the nationwide pause in response to “a temporary telecommunications outage”. It has since lifted the grounding order, as it continued to investigate the cause of the outage.

Passengers on two US-bound flights from Melbourne airport, which were due to depart at 12pm and 12.45pm, were also delayed due to the situation in the US.

A flight to Dallas, scheduled for 10am, departed at around 1pm, shortly after the US ban was lifted.

By 3pm Saturday, all delayed flights from Melbourne had departed.

Qantas thanked passengers for their patience and recommended people with upcoming flights to the US to check its website for more information.

Four flights from Brisbane were also delayed.. Two had departed by 3pm on Saturday.

“Passengers travelling to North America are advised to contact their airline for the latest information about their flight,” a Brisbane Airport spokesperson said.

#53. Caught Between Killers: Ebola on One Side, Islamic State on the Other

So many people are dying of Ebola that their bodies have been stacked, one atop the other, on makeshift racks in a shed by the hospital gate.

The town’s main cemetery is full, so fresh graves are being squeezed in between palm trees by the side of the road.

Medicine, IVs and basic protections have been so scarce that three-quarters of Ebola patients in this besieged Congolese town have died.

Six months on, Ebola continues to hack a deadly trail across eastern Democratic Republic of Congo.

When the outbreak was first detected in May, it resembled a single, giant blaze, with infections concentrated in a handful of towns in one province.

It has since morphed into a far bigger, more elusive foe, creeping into eight provinces and splintering into numerous hot spots as it moves through one of the world’s great rainforests.

Instead of one big fire, there are many now, scattered across a vast area about the size of Britain.

Health workers have corralled the virus where it was first detected, but infections are soaring elsewhere. Biakato, a small town deep in the rainforest, suddenly accounted for as many as one-quarter of new cases nationally. Yet its beleaguered residents were left almost entirely on their own, with dangerously little to defend themselves against the fastest-growing Ebola outbreak in history.

“We see the same mistakes as before,” said Dr. Jean-Jacques Muyembe, who helped discover Ebola in 1976 and now heads Congo’s National Institute for Biomedical Research. “It’s as if we were starting from zero. It’s very disappointing.”

And if that were not enough, the campaign to contain the epidemic has run into a terrifying new threat: the Islamic State.

#52. Microsoft ‘deeply sorry’ for global outage that could have billion-dollar impact on Australia

Experts say disruption exposes ‘Australia’s reliance on foreign technology’ as software giant says services being restored

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Microsoft outage could be a disaster for the Australian economy that forces some small businesses to close, with costs expected to run into the billions of dollars, experts have warned.

The US software company has said it is “deeply sorry” for a widespread outage that caused disruption to millions of users, organisations and businesses across the globe – and that it has “addressed the underlying issue”.

The company said the outage was a “technical issue” not related to cyber-attacks or AI agents, and that it continued to gradually restore services.

Experts have said the outage exposes “Australia’s reliance on foreign technology” and that people unable to be paid, send an invoice, or access government services “are entitled to be worried”.

The outage of Microsoft’s services, which began on Friday morning, lasted roughly 18 hours before services started to recover. Microsoft said it was bringing back services gradually to ensure a stable restoration.

Ashwin Pal, a partner at the risk advisory firm RSM Australia, said his clients had activated business continuity and disaster recovery plans, but many businesses didn’t have a fallback.

“A protracted event like this can easily be the straw that breaks the camel’s back for smaller entities,” Pal said.

Pal said Microsoft had obviously struggled to recover, which raised bigger questions.

“I’m a technologist and I’m actually flabbergasted as to how something like this could have happened … they pushed out a change which should have been tested,” Pal said.

The Australian Computer Society has renewed calls for the federal government to urgently review which critical IT systems rely on a single provider, and where backup options could keep the economy running.

The society’s chief executive, Prins Ralston, said the outage had “exposed Australia’s reliance on foreign technology”, particularly on small businesses.

“Australians who couldn’t get paid, couldn’t send an invoice, or couldn’t reach a government service this morning are entitled to be worried,” Ralston said.

Australian hospitals and health clinics were affected by the outage, although the Australian Medical Association (AMA) on Friday said had been no impacts for patient care.

“We’ve had some facilities return to using paper for scripts because authentication to get into lots of prescribing software requires multifactor verification, which requires you to be able to access your Microsoft-supported email,” said the AMA’s president, Dr Michael Bonning.

Courts in South Australia and Victoria reported that the outage had affeced its email system. Many schools across the country were also affected.

Service NSW, the one-stop shop agency for residents, drivers, and business owners to access government services, encouraged customers to delay their visits to its service centres.

Federal government departments have also been maffectedy the outage, including national security agencies.

The head the domestic spy agency Asio, Mike Burgess, on Friday said the organisation’s systems “operate on multiple levels”, adding that they had been affected “on one level, yes, but not completely”.

The outage has affected businesses and organisations across the world that use Microsoft products.

In the UK, healthcare systems were disrupted and several British police forces urged people not to try to report crimes online and to phone them in instead.

US government offices across the country have been affected by the outage, including congressional offices that heavily rely on Outlook email. The US House’s chief administrative officer that handles IT for the chamber sent out a “campus-wide alert” to staffers on Thursday about the outage, Politico reported.

In Japan, the shipping company Sagawa Express said it was unable to accept online reservations for some of its services at airports and train stations while Taisho, a leading pharmaceutical company, said it was having to handle orders by phone.

When stock market trading opened on Wall Street, shares in Microsoft dipped slightly, down 1.1% at $516.86.

Microsoft has suggested the outage may have been caused by problems with the settings applied to servers responsible for keeping Microsoft 365 up and running.

Microsoft’s executive vice-president of Copilot, Charles Lamanna, said: “We regret that today’s outages caused disruptions for our customers, and we are working to address the issue.”

#51. German spies handed new powers to tackle 'hybrid threats' from abroad

In future, German spies working for the BND foreign intelligence service will be able to conduct covert operations abroad

Germany's foreign and domestic intelligence services are to be given greater powers to combat espionage, terrorism and sabotage after the country's parliament, the Bundestag, approved a major overhaul of its legislation.

Interior Minister Alexander Dobrindt, who championed the bill, has called it a "reform of the century", necessary because of the increasing threats from foreign powers.

But the Left Party's security spokeswoman, Clara BÃ¼nger, said Dobrindt was creating a new German secret police.

The legislation gives numerous new powers to Germany's BND federal intelligence service, and its BfV domestic service, the Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution.

In the Bundestag, 309 MPs voted in favour of the new law on intelligence services, while 190 voted against and 71 abstained.

For historical reasons, German intelligence has mainly focused on gathering information rather than acting on it.

In future, German spies working for the BND, which is responsible for foreign intelligence, will be able to conduct covert operations abroad, including sabotage.

This could include paralysing the servers of state-sponsored hacker groups, disrupting disinformation networks, or even disrupting supply chains for weapons.

Agents for the domestic intelligence service, the BfV, will no longer to be limited to gathering information. For the first time they will be able to take protective action against property and IT systems, if specific threats are identified.

That means they will be allowed to dismantle or break explosives or weapons, divert or block data flows during cyber-attacks, delete or falsify data stored for malicious purposes, and plant false information on those involved.

Germany target of 'intensive' espionage, Merz says after suspected Russian spy arrest

Sabotage suspect held in Germany after spate of attacks on power grid

Germany says Russia behind Leipzig airport drone attack

Under certain conditions, BfV agents will also be permitted to enter homes covertly. However, the measure must always be authorised in advance.

They will also be given wider powers of digital surveillance, including monitoring encrypted telecommunications and carrying out online searches. They will also be allowed to use AI-supported data analysis and biometric image matching on the internet.

The government says the reform is needed because of an increase in foreign espionage, particularly from Russia, China and Iran.

"We are the target of hybrid threats," said Dobrindt. "Germany is a theatre of operations for terrorism, extremism and foreign powers."

In August, a drone carrying an explosive device was discovered at Leipzig Airport, close to Ukrainian cargo planes. However, it failed to explode as the bomb's detonator was apparently faulty.

German authorities say Russia was behind the attack.

The new law has been bitterly criticised by some opposition politicians, even though Dobrindt has said it is "madness" to think it means that Germany is creating a new secret police.

And Clara BÃ¼nger from the Left Party told public broadcaster ARD that the new law was "a monsterâ¦ summoning ghosts that we will probably never be able to get rid of".

The separation between the police and the secret service, she warned, was "being eroded" - and BÃ¼nger said that had been a lesson learned from the Nazi era.

"This means the secret service is being granted executive powers and will not only be allowed to act pre-emptively, but will also be granted police powers."

However, Sonja Eichwede of the centre-left SPD, which is part of Germany's governing coalition, said the reforms would make a significant contribution to ensuring the country's democracy was "robust and resilient".

#50. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#49. US nationwide flight ground stop: "Our routers got overloaded with data"

On Friday, the Federal Aviation Administration issued a rare nationwide ground stop. More than 8,200 flights, or over 31% of those scheduled in the United States, were canceled. The ground stop lasted nearly five hours and was the longest in the country since September 11, 2001. Officials blamed outdated air traffic communications equipment, and said that there was no evidence of a cyber attack. The airspace is now reopened and flights have resumed.

#48. Ukraine captures first Indonesian prisoner of war amid surge in Russian recruitment

Capture of man from Java follows reports that hundreds are being recruited from the south-east Asian country as Moscow seeks to boost its war effort

Ukraine says it has captured its first Indonesian prisoner of war, amid what it says is a spike in recruitment by Russia from the south-east Asian nation.

The 49-year-old man from Java was taken to Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia region to take part in a Russian assault, but managed to reach Ukrainian-held territory after the group came under drone attack, according to Ukraine’s state-run I Want to Live project. He was then captured by the Ukrainians.

According to the project, some 283 Indonesians were recruited in the first nine months of this year to fight for Russia against Ukraine. It said recruitment from Indonesia was no more than 10 in the previous three years.

The Russian embassy in Jakarta did not immediately respond to questions about the alleged recruitment effort but it is not the first time Russia has sought foreign mercenaries for its war effort.

In February, Kenya’s intelligence services reported that more than 1,000 Kenyans had been lured to fight for Russia.

A growing number of people from African countries – including Kenya, Uganda and South Africa – and elsewhere have been lured to the frontline as Russia seeks help to sustain its more than four-year war after invading Ukraine in February 2022.

This month Jakarta urged Moscow to ensure no Indonesians were drafted into its military, following the reported death in October of an Indonesian national serving with the military.

Indonesian foreign ministry spokesperson Yvonne Mewengkang said: “The Indonesian government asked the Russian government to take all necessary steps to ensure that no Indonesians will be recruited into the Russian military or any other military-related position in Russia through any avenues.”

Indonesia, she added, was particularly concerned about recruitment practices that could constitute human trafficking.

Days later, Indonesian police said they had identified a human trafficking syndicate targeting its nationals that used social media and messaging apps to recruit victims.

The posts promised lucrative jobs in logistics, border security and defence construction, but after the applicants travelled to Russia they were directed to sign contracts to serve as mercenaries, with some deployed to conflict zones, police said.

The ‘I Want to Live’ governmental initiative was established in September 2022 by the coordination headquarters for Prisoners of War with support from Ukraine defence ministry and defence intelligence (HUR).

It was designed to allow active-duty troops, including Russian servicemen and foreign nationals recruited into the Russian military, to safely surrender and exit the battlefield.

#47. Democratic Senate leader Schumer set to face leadership challenge

Chris Van Hollen (L) says he will challenge Chuck Schumer (R) and that the Democrats need new leadership

US Senator Chuck Schumer is facing a challenge to his position as majority leader from his fellow Democratic Senator Chris Van Hollen, as the party prepares to take control of Congress after sweeping the midterm elections.

Van Hollen, 67, said in a post on X on Thursday that Americans had voted for change earlier this month and "to deliver, we need new leadership".

Schumer, 75, has led Democrats in the chamber since 2017, serving as both majority and minority leader. He was first elected to the Senate in 1999.

Following the midterms earlier this month, his position has come under pressure by some left-wing Democrats who see him as too moderate and part of the party establishment.

Responding to the challenge, Schumer, who represents New York, said he welcomed "any discussion about the future of our caucus and the important work ahead".

He added that he was "proud of what we've accomplished together" in the midterms and that he had "the votes to continue as Democratic leader".

A vote on the new Senate leader will be held behind closed doors in early December and the winner will assume the role on 4 January, when Democrats take control of both the Senate and the House of Representatives after their midterms successes.

The move by Van Hollen, who represents Maryland, marks the most significant step by a high-profile Democrat to challenge the party's existing leadership following last week's midterms victory.

He wrote that Americans had called on the party to "unrig an economy rigged against working people" in an X post announcing his plan to run.

"We should not be preserving our institutions if they are not delivering for the American people," he said at a summit in Colorado hosted by majority Democrats. "And we should be making delivering for the American people the single mission of our government, even when that means shaking things up."

He added: "This country does not belong to the people who can afford to buy influence. It belongs to all of us. And it's time we govern like that."

In recent years, Van Hollen has taken positions popular with those on the left of his party.

In 2024, he supported restricting weapons shipments to Israel and criticised the Biden administration over its role in the war in Gaza.

Van Hollen - who has served in the Senate for nearly a decade - acknowledged that he was an "underdog" in an interview with MS NOW, but added that he believed the race was "very much in flux".

Independent Senator Bernie Sanders and Democratic Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed have both publicly come out in support of Van Hollen - while others have also called for new leadership.

Sanders said he believed Van Hollen "will stand up to powerful special interests, take on the billionaire class and fight for working families".

El-Sayed said the challenger would bring "bold leadership" and lead with both "courage and experience".

Others Democrats have publicly backed Schumer.

Senator Jacky Rosen of Nevada said: "He has the experience and the fight to continue leading our diverse caucus at this crucial moment."

Senator Kirsten Gillibrand said: "You will see, despite all the grumblings, enormous gratitude for the hard work he has done to get us to this position."

#44. Thursday briefing: What we do (and don’t) know about Alice Huang and the Nvidia affair

In today’s newsletter: US intelligence reportedly believes the former tech executive was recruited by Beijing, raising questions about the security of the AI industry and Trump’s close ties to Nvidia chief Jensen Huang

Good morning. Espionage is supposed to be a secretive business, and spies are supposed to be virtually invisible. That hasn’t been the case for Alice Huang, the mysterious Shanghai executive who formed a romantic relationship with Jensen Huang, the founder of Nvidia, one of America’s most powerful tech companies.

Many questions have arisen since it emerged that US intelligence believes that Huang (no relation of Jensen’s) is an asset recruited by the Chinese intelligence services to spy on behalf of Beijing. This morning’s newsletter explains what we know about the story, and what it tells us about the effectiveness of Chinese espionage. That’s after the headlines.

UK | Ministers are considering changing the law to force the entertainment industry to better protect women and children in the wake of the revelations over David Walliams and the conviction of Russell Brand.

Climate | The UK government has appointed the world’s first special representative for climate security, in the strongest sign yet of fears that climate breakdown will bring chaos to the country.

US midterms | Democrat Scott Colom has become the first Black senator in Mississippi to be elected since Reconstruction, in a stunning upset after more than a week of vote counting in one of the country’s most reliably red states.

Europe | Millions of people across Spain have joined the country’s first general strike in 14 years, setting up picket lines to protest against soaring housing prices before an election in which the far right could be catapulted into power.

Technology | Police officers risk arresting innocent people, especially from ethnic minorities, if they treat inaccurate facial recognition data as evidence, an official report has found.

Last month, the New York Times reported that Jensen Huang had formed a “close relationship” with Alice Huang, the former executive of a Singapore-based computing company, Megaspeed. Megaspeed is a customer of Nvidia; it buys the extremely valuable chips they produce, which are essential for powering AI, and rents computing space to Chinese companies. The US government had already been investigating whether Megaspeed could be flouting US restrictions and smuggling chips into China – allegations that both Nvidia and Megaspeed deny.

US intelligence officials, who had been conducting surveillance on Alice Huang, eventually concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset who had been passing on information to Beijing. The intelligence behind this assessment is classified, so we don’t know exactly what they found – or which intelligence body was conducting the surveillance. Nvidia said Jensen Huang’s interactions with Alice Huang were “not unusual or unexpected”, but on Sunday, Huang admitted in a letter to his employees that he had been having an affair. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children,” he said, adding that he was “deeply sorry”.

Nvidia’s valuation is inextricably linked to Huang. By yesterday, $430bn (5.5%) had been wiped off its share price, and in the early hours of this morning, that figure ballooned to about $600bn following reports that Nvidia’s board was deciding whether to oust Huang (you can follow the latest on that here). The company has appointed outside counsel to review the pair’s relationship and says it has no reason to believe that secrets or national security were compromised.

The most interesting aspects of this story are the questions we can’t (yet) answer. Did Nvidia’s board know that US intelligence suspected Alice Huang was a spy? What evidence led US intelligence to conclude she was spying for China? What information could she have passed to Beijing? And …

Reporters and sleuths on Chinese social media have been trying to get to the bottom of who the Megaspeed chief really is. On Tuesday, the Financial Times published an interesting report (£) about Alice Huang’s backstory. She was born in the rural area of Hunan, worked as a journalist at China’s state broadcaster, then at HSBC and the investment bank Citic Securities. In 2015, she joined an investment management company Hexi.

There, she met financier Kang Wei. The two became romantically involved, and together they reinvented themselves as AI entrepreneurs. In 2023, Huang and her business partners acquired Megaspeed, a Singaporean subsidiary of a Chinese gaming company, which she and Wei aimed to turn into a datacentre business. According to the FT’s report, the two pitched investors on their ability to secure access to Nvidia chips.

Nobody knows exactly when Jensen Huang met Alice Huang, when their relationship became romantic, or what information she might have passed to Beijing while it was going on. The earliest picture of them together is at an after-party following a tech trade show in Taipei, taken in 2024. As Aisha Down and Dan Milmo pointed out earlier this week, Jensen Huang is close to Donald Trump, so it’s possible that Alice Huang could have gained glimpses into US AI policy as it was being formed, into Trump’s thinking on AI, or into Nvidia’s company strategy. But without seeing the evidence, all this is speculation.

What does this reveal about Chinese espionage?

The US has been historically cautious about allowing AI chips to end up in Chinese hands. In 2022, Joe Biden’s administration introduced the Chips and Science Act, which restricted the sale of US chips in China, banning the export of most Nvidia chips. “The Chinese have a very strong pedigree for industrial espionage and copying other nations’ technologies,” Steve Tsang, the director of the SOAS China Institute, tells me. “They’ve been doing this for decades. And now it’s no longer engines they’re interested in, but AI.”

Both the US and China have an interest in gathering intelligence about each other’s AI firms. What’s more remarkable is the possibility that the Chinese spy agency, the MSS, could have recruited Alice Huang as a “honeytrap” to ensnare Jensen Huang – a method fraught with risk, which seems more reminiscent of 1970s spy fiction than contemporary counterintelligence (China, for its part, has rejected claims that Alice Huang was a spy).

“The facts clearly aren’t established, but it seems more likely that if Alice Huang was working for Beijing, the MSS would have recruited her after they became aware of her relationship with Huang, not before,” Nigel Inkster, the former director of operations and intelligence for the British Secret Intelligence Service, tells me.

China has built a large intelligence gathering operation: the MSS is now bigger than the CIA, and various national security laws mean that Chinese nationals can be required to provide assistance to the Chinese state. “You have a lot of people who can be co-opted into intelligence gathering, all of whom can be tasked with different requirements,” Inkster says. “Business delegations travelling abroad can be tasked with gathering information. So can visiting academics.”

“The big question, to my mind, is whether the Chinese intelligence agencies have actually been willing to deliver unwelcome information to the party leadership,” Inkster adds.

Jensen Huang is the most influential figure in the US AI industry outside major labs such as Anthropic, and has cultivated a close relationship with Donald Trump. He’s successfully lobbied the Trump administration to loosen regulations on AI and roll back restrictions on the sales of Nvidia’s chips to China (last year, Trump controversially approved the sale of Nvidia’s H200 chips directly to China in exchange for the federal government taking a 25% cut). Huang was present at the White House twice last month: once for a state dinner with Xi Jinping, and again for a meeting of tech and AI executives.

Huang’s close relationship with Trump could help explain why the president has so far played down concerns about Chinese espionage, appearing remarkably ambivalent about the recent allegations. The president was reportedly briefed last year about US intelligence conducting surveillance on Alice Huang, and in spring this year, his secretary of state, Marco Rubio, supervised the drafting of a classified memo warning that Jensen Huang was in a relationship with a Chinese spy. In May, Trump issued a last-minute invitation to Jensen Huang to board Air Force One in Alaska and join a delegation of executives bound to China. A US government official told reporters at the New York Times that when Rubio learned that Huang would be joining the trip in Alaska, he briefed Trump on the memo.

But on Monday, Trump, who is due to meet Xi Jinping later this month, said he had not known at the time of the trip about Huang’s relationship. He said the FBI had been investigating a matter relating to Huang, and defended the Nvidia CEO, describing him as an “honourable guy” who “built a great American company”.

Trump’s $10bn BBC battle: judge rules lawsuit can go ahead

Donald Trump’s $10bn defamation lawsuit against the BBC has been granted permission to proceed, after the corporation’s attempt to get it dismissed was thrown out by a Trump-appointed Florida judge. The case focuses on an edit in a 2024 Panorama documentary that spliced two comments together that were made by the president during a rally before the January 6 Capitol riots, for which the BBC has apologised. With the lawsuit now set to continue, what’s at stake for press freedom? Lucy Hough speaks to global editor, Devika Bhat.

A bit of good news to remind you that the world’s not all bad

Researchers from the Hoag Family Cancer Institute have made a potential breakthrough in the early diagnosis of ovarian cancer – a disease often detected after it has spread.

For decades, efforts to detect the cancer earlier have focused on finding smaller tumours. “We asked … what if we could identify the earliest molecular warning signs before invasive cancer even develops?” said study leader Rajan Gogna.

Ovarian cancer often begins with microscopic changes in the lining of the fallopian tubes. The study found that women experiencing these changes have elevated levels of a molecule called Tu-Stroma in their blood.

The study’s co-author Dr Esha Madan says the discovery “could transform how we approach prevention”.

#43. Eisenkot Accuses Netanyahu of Fueling ‘Racist’ Hostility Against Arabs

Gadi Eisenkot sharply criticized Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu days after the Israeli leader attacked Arab lawmakers in a divisive speech.

Gadi Eisenkot, the former military chief who is trying to form the next Israeli government, accused Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu on Friday of fueling a “racist” campaign against the country’s Arab citizens, in some of the strongest public criticism of his rival’s attacks on the community.

The comments were made days after Mr. Netanyahu delivered a divisive speech to the opening session of Parliament, or the Knesset, in which he referred to Arab lawmakers as “terror supporters” and “haters of Israel.” Diplomats and analysts condemned the remarks as a brazen display of hatred and incitement against Arab citizens, who make up about 20 percent of the overall population, and voted in massive numbers in inconclusive elections last month.

“Since the beginning of the election campaign, they have been subjected to a racist and aggressive campaign of delegitimization, fueled by the outgoing prime minister and those around him for political purposes,” Mr. Eisenkot said in a statement on social media.

“Arab citizens of Israel are an integral part of the Israeli story,” he said, adding that it was wrong to malign an entire community.

“The attempt to marginalize them and vilify them collectively is a profound violation of the principles of statesmanship,” Mr. Eisenkot said. “It dishonors Israel’s Declaration of Independence and the values we take pride in as a Jewish and democratic state.”

The Declaration of Independence says all citizens have equal social and political rights “irrespective of religion, race or sex.”

#42. The Peruvian city that has embraced Pope Leo as one of its own

The Pope, who has Peruvian citizenship, lived in Chiclayo for several years

Pope Leo XIV has celebrated Mass near the Peruvian city where he made his name as a compassionate bishop who worked closely with poor and migrant communities.

The ceremony was held on Friday at a park in Pimentel, about 10km (six miles) from Chiclayo in northern Peru as part of his tour of Latin America.

The Vatican estimates more than 1.5 million people gathered to welcome the pontiff.

"Thank you for your affection, support and prayers," the Pope told followers.

"Especially for my health during this time of trial which, with God's help, I am overcoming," he added, referencing the surgery he had for lung cancer which took place just weeks ago.

Some of the crowd arrived 12 hours in advance to wait in vigil before the Mass began.

"We spent the night praying, alongside family, excited to welcome the Pope from Chiclayo," said Isa Orderique.

Pope Leo celebrated Mass in front of a jubilant crowd

Many came from across Peru and even neighbouring countries to attend.

Merli Cruz Guerrero, from an area near the Ecuadorean border, travelled several hours by bus to attend. She also spent the entire night waiting.

Of all the events the Pope has attended so far during his visit to Peru, this felt particularly emotional.

The crowd fell into a hushed silence as the pontiff's motorcade approached.

They waited eagerly for his arrival despite spending long hours out in the open with the temperature pushing past 30C.

For many living here, the fact that the Pope worked extensively in this region before becoming the head of the Catholic Church has put them in the spotlight.

"The Pope has come home and Chiclayo is celebrating," said Lisbeth DÃ­az, a teacher who worked with Pope Leo when he served as the city's bishop between 2015 and 2023.

Lisbeth DÃ­az says Chiclayo is celebrating the Pope's return

Peru is the longest stop on the first tour of Latin America by the pontiff, who was elected in May 2025.

The trip has also taken in Argentina and Uruguay, but his time in this country carries particular significance.

The Pope not only lived in Peru for long periods between 1985 and 2023 but also became a citizen of the country in 2015, forging close ties with local communities.

Around Chiclayo, it is hard to meet anyone who doesn't claim to be a friend of the Pope or have a special anecdote about him.

Some recall how he would enjoy sampling Peruvian delicacies at El TrÃ©bol, a restaurant next to the city's cathedral. Others remember his love of tennis.

But for many it's the more serious moments of his time here, such as helping launch soup kitchens during the Covid-19 pandemic, or wading through deep waters on horseback when floods hit the area.

Several of these images are on display in a small museum dedicated to him at the entrance to the bishop's residence.

Commemorative pictures of the Pope are sold in the main of square of Chiclayo, including a famous image of him visiting a remote village on horseback

But for the city, and for a country that calls him the "Peruvian Pope", his return home means much more than a collection of photographs and anecdotes.

"It is the return of the man who brought Peruvians closer to the Church," said Samuel David Miranda Atoche, a local parish priest from Chiclayo who was ordained by the Pope back when he was still the bishop and known under his birth name of Robert Prevost.

And it is not just local people he connected with, according to DÃ­az, who came to Chiclayo from Venezuela.

"It was difficult for him to see so many migrants living on the streets," she added, recalling the time when large numbers of Venezuelans came through Chiclayo escaping the political and economic crisis in their home country.

"So, he got in touch with the migration authorities and coordinated with other organisations to speed up the paperwork needed for many of us to obtain legal residency and work permits."

Who is Robert Prevost, the new Pope Leo XIV?

Robert Prevost's time here as bishop also involved difficult decisions, such as having to deal with the controversy and scandal surrounding the dissolution of Sodalitium of Christian Life, a Peruvian Catholic organisation that had faced decades of sexual abuse allegations.

Santiago Roncagliolo, a Peruvian writer and journalist who has studied the Pope's life, believes his handling of that crisis earned him the favour of his predecessor, Pope Francis, and helped set him on the path to the papacy.

Father Miranda also stressed the role the now-pontiff played in bringing people together.

"If there is one thing Bishop Prevost worked for here," he added, referring to the Pope by his previous title, "it was unity".

That unity has been on display in the run up to the Mass, with the square in front of Chiclayo Cathedral filled with joyous celebrations for days.

Father Samuel David Miranda Atoche says the Pope brought Peruvians closer to the Church

Normally, 800,000 people live in the city and its surrounding area, but estimates say the Pope's visit has caused that to double.

Local businesses have been capitalising on the Pope's popularity to boost sales and hotels have sharply increased their nightly room rates.

But that didn't seem to put off the faithful who gathered for the Mass.

Shouts of: "The Pope is from Chiclayo!" echoed across the field during the event.

Their affection was clearly reflected by the pontiff.

"As I look at your faces, a prayer of thanksgiving to the Lord arises in my heart," he said from the stage, "for the blessing you have been and continue to be in my life."

Pope begins lengthy trip to Latin America weeks after lung cancer surgery

Pope diagnosed with lung cancer but had successful surgery, Vatican says

#38. China rules out hosting Cop33 climate summit in 2028

Decision disappoints experts hoping for world’s biggest emitter to take global lead on climate crisis

China has ruled out hosting the Cop33 UN climate summit in 2028 after months of deliberations, leaving the field open for other Asian countries to hold it, the Guardian understands.

The decision will disappoint many countries and climate experts, who had hoped the world’s biggest emitter would take the lead on the global stage, as the US under Donald Trump has vacated all forms of international cooperation on the climate.

Al Gore, the former US vice-president, was among those left disheartened by the decision. “I would be very much in favour of that idea [of China hosting the summit],” he told the Guardian. “It’s too bad.”

China is in the midst of an economic transformation, from being heavily dependent on coal to becoming an “electro state”. John Kerry, the former US secretary of state, told the Guardian that China was leaving the rest of the world behind in forging and using the low-carbon technologies of the future.

Renewable energy generation capacity has now outstripped coal in China, and its greenhouse gas emissions appear to have declined slightly this year.

Sales of electric vehicles in the country have also taken off, making up nearly two-thirds of the market last month, and China’s clean energy industries drove more than 90% of the country’s investment growth last year.

The rules of the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) mean an Asian country should host in 2028.

Gareth Redmond-King, the international lead at the Energy and Climate Intelligence Unit thinktank, said China’s position as an industrial powerhouse would have made it a formidable contender to hold the summit.

“It would undoubtedly be significant for the world’s biggest emitter and second-biggest economy to host a climate Cop,” he said.

“They’re leading the world on investment, manufacture and deployment of clean energy tech, and their emissions have started to tick down. As a leadership story, China hosting Cop33 would be a big deal.”

It is not clear why China has decided against hosting the conference, but several experts that spoke to the Guardian suggested that the US electoral cycle was likely to have been a factor. The next US presidential election will take place on 7 November 2028, and Cop33 is scheduled for 6 to 17 November that year.

After the strong showing by Democrats in the recent midterm elections, in which voter concern over the climate crisis was thought to be a factor, the Democrats will be hoping to elect a climate-friendly president in 2028. But even if they succeed, Trump will still be in office until the following January.

The US president rejects climate science and withdrew the US from the Paris agreement, and is also withdrawing from its parent treaty, the 1992 UNFCCC.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

China seeks to be seen as a leader among developing countries, but has been wary of stepping into the gap left by the US. Under the Democratic presidents Joe Biden and Barack Obama, the US and China enjoyed a close partnership on climate issues, setting joint targets on reducing methane and developing clean technology.

Cop33 will be an important meeting because it will include the “global stocktake” – a mechanism under the 2015 Paris climate agreement that examines countries’ progress on cutting emissions in line with the goal of limiting global temperature rises to 1.5C.

That limit is now certain to be surpassed, but experts say countries should now try to minimise the temperature “overshoot”, to avoid worse ravages from extreme weather.

India’s government considered offering to host Cop33 for several months earlier this year, but also decided against it. India’s coal use has been increasing, even as the country exceeds its own renewable energy targets.

The Guardian understands that other countries mulling a bid to host Cop33 include Uzbekistan, South Korea, Indonesia and Thailand.

No decision on a host is likely to be made at this year’s Cop31 meeting, which is ongoing in Turkey, with the Turkish and Australian governments jointly presiding over the summit.

#37. Two Celebrity Cases Put a Spotlight on British Courts’ Gag Orders

Russell Brand and the TV personality David Walliams both persuaded courts to suppress misconduct allegations against them. Some say the libel laws need to change.

Two recent cases have brought new attention to the ways that British laws can allow the wealthy and powerful to silence their critics.

The first was the rape and sexual assault trial of the comedian Russell Brand. Before his conviction last week, Mr. Brand had obtained an injunction prohibiting another accuser from telling almost anyone — including the police, her family and most doctors — about her claims that he assaulted her. (Mr. Brand has denied her allegations.) That injunction, first revealed by Dan Neidle, a tax lawyer and free-expression activist, apparently remains in force.

In the second instance, two British news organizations were temporarily blocked from reporting allegations of sexual misconduct against David Walliams, a television personality and author of children’s books, before a judge declined to make the injunction permanent. The Guardian newspaper and ITV News, a British broadcaster, published investigations last week into the allegations, which Mr. Walliams denies.

Though both men now face public scrutiny, the revelations of how they used court injunctions — essentially gag orders — to protect their reputations have provoked no shortage of public and political outrage. Judges in other cases have issued “super injunctions,” which prohibit publishing any information even about the injunction itself.

“What will the government do to prevent these abuses of process, and stop the abusive injunctions and super injunctions which stop scrutiny, accountability and the work of the free press?” Nick Timothy, the shadow justice minister, asked in Parliament on Tuesday.

The British government is now “looking at what we can do,” Alex Davies-Jones, the government minister responsible for victims of crime and violence against women and girls, said in response.

#35. Estonia bans Russian athletes as backlash intensifies

Estonia has become the latest country to say it will not allow Russian or Belarusian athletes to compete on its territory as a result of the ongoing war in Ukraine.

It comes after Norway, Finland and Canada made similar announcements.

The moves come amid a mounting backlash over the reinstatement of athletes from Russia and Belarus.

In September, the International Ski Federation (FIS) relaxed its rules, saying competitors from the two countries would be granted access to its events "provided that they sign a declaration of neutrality".

They had previously faced more stringent background checks on any voluntary link or paid duty with the military.

Participation remains limited to individual events, with athletes banned from displaying national symbols such as flags, anthems or uniforms.

However, it has now emerged that Estonia's government has written to the FIS, claiming that allowing entry to competitors from Russia and Belarus would undermine the country's support of Ukraine, and present an opportunity for propaganda. It said a ban issued in 2022 will be maintained.

In a statement issued to BBC Sport, the FIS said it "is committed to offering full support to athletes, national ski associations and local organisers - with most of which we have a long-standing relationship - in the process of navigating what we know to be a very complex situation.

"With that said, the Individual Neutral Athlete policy is clear in stating that notwithstanding eligibility, the host country visa and entry policies remain in place and are determined solely by the host country. FIS bears no responsibility for denial of visas or entry to any person granted eligibility under the FIS Individual Neutral Athlete Policy."

On Thursday, Scandinavian neighbours Norway and Finland both said they would also block visas for Russian athletes because of the war in Ukraine.

Norway is preparing to host the cross-country skiing World Cup next month, while Finland is due to stage the figure skating world championships in March. The International Skating Union (ISU) has said it "believes that athletes should not be punished for the actions of their governments in a world where, sadly, many conflicts are ongoing", and that it was "seeking clarifications" from officials in Finland.

This comes in the wake of last month's decision by the Court of Arbitration for Sport (Cas) to overturn a ban of Russia by the International Ice Hockey Federation (IIHF), a move condemned by the British, German, Finnish and Czech federations.

Russia have been suspended from competing in IIHF events since the country invaded Ukraine in February 2022, but the Russian Ice Hockey Federation (RIHF) then successfully appealed that decision at Cas. The court said that IIHF arguments citing safety and security were "not supported by sufficient evidence", but the Hockey Federation of Ukraine said the ruling caused "special outrage".

The Canadian government has since informed its hockey authorities that it will not issue visas for Russians to play in next month's World Junior Championship, including those who currently play for teams in Canada, as long as the war continues. A number of countries had said they would refuse to play against Russia, if they were allowed to take part.

Despite the controversy, the return of Russia and Belarus to international sport has been gathering pace this year.

In March, several athletes from the two countries were allowed to compete under their national flags at the Winter Paralympics after partial suspensions by the International Paralympic Committee were lifted.

In July, the International Olympic Committee (IOC) provisionally reinstated Russia to its competitions, stating a ban introduced in 2023 after the invasion of Ukraine was "no longer applicable".

However, sports federations can still enforce their own eligibility criteria. While World Gymnastics, World Aquatics and World Boxing have all removed restrictions on Russian athletes competing, a total ban has been maintained by World Athletics.

Last month, it was announced that Moscow will host the 2030 World Weightlifting Championships - the first time an Olympic federation has chosen to stage a global event in Russia since the 2022 invasion of Ukraine.

#32. Nvidia scandal fallout widens as Trump's treasury secretary blames cabinet colleague

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has blamed Howard Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to properly oversee the export of high-spec AI chips, as the fallout from the Nvidia scandal widens to President Donald Trump's cabinet.

Pressure is building on Lutnick from across the political spectrum to explain his department's actions.

Jensen Huang was ousted as Nvidia CEO after revelations he had an affair with a businesswoman who was also allegedly a Chinese spy.

Megaspeed, the firm she worked for, had been probed by US government agencies over suspicions it was smuggling Nvidia chips into China, reports said, but was not on a list of firms US companies are prevented from working with.

Bessent told CNBC several agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

Bessent's comments come after Lutnick was called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over the Commerce Department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

Other congressional committees have asked both Lutnick and his department to retain all records relating to Nvidia and Huang.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said some parts of the administration wanted more controls on trade with Megaspeed

Bessent said he was "frustrated" by delays in putting the company on the export control list and reports of leakage of AI chips into China.

"President Trump made it very clear that China should not receive any Nvidia chips above H100s, so any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts our great companies in the AI race, because it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Last year, the Commerce Department had been investigating Megaspeed over suspicions it had been smuggling some of Nvidia's advanced AI chips into China, according to the New York Times. Megaspeed has been contacted for comment.

There was also pressure on the Commerce Department to add it to the list, but the Financial Times separately reported that the department was delaying efforts to have the cloud computing firm included.

However, Bessent also pointed the finger at Nvidia.

"The story has been thus far of Jensen Huang's personal foibles," he told CNBC, external.

"The real story here should be: did Nvidia know their client, and was there a robust effort at Nvidia to make sure that there was no leakage and that they were selling to what were obviously pass-through shell companies?

"It's important that anyone at the company there be held accountable for that."

Bessent's pointed comments are a rare instance of internal debate within the Trump administration spilling out into the public view, although tensions have reportedly simmered between Bessent and Lutnick in the past.

In 2024, the two reportedly jostled for the role of treasury secretary ahead of Trump's return to the White House, and last year the pair butted heads over the administration's tariff policy, according to US media.

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

Huang has been summoned to testify before the Senate Banking Committee about his alleged affair with the former Megaspeed executive, who is suspected of being a Chinese spy.

The commerce secretary has also been summoned to appear before the committee in December.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," committee chairman Republican Senator Tim Scott posted on X.

Democrats on key congressional committees have asked the Commerce Department and Lutnick to keep all records relating to decisions to allow Nvidia to sell its advanced AI chips to Chinese companies, including all their communications with or about Nvidia and Huang.

Ways and Means Committee Ranking Member Richard E Neal and Senate Finance Committee Ranking Member Ron Wyden are starting investigations into the way Nvidia was allowed to sell these chips.

They say Nvidia's advanced H200 chips could be sold to Chinese firms if they were first imported from their manufacturing facility in Taiwan into the US for inspection.

A 25% tariff - a levy paid by US importers to the federal government - was imposed on those chips under a section of the Trade Act that Neal and Wyden say is meant to allow the US president to take action against imports that risk national security.

"Trump's use of the statute appears to have done the opposite, undermining US national security and putting Americans at risk," they said.
